Eilenberg Theorems for Free
Henning Urbat ,, Jiří Adámek ∗,, Liang-Ting Chen, Stefan Milius ,

Abstract
Eilenberg-type correspondences, relating varieties of languages (e.g. of finite words, infinite words, or trees) to pseudovarieties of finite algebras, form the backbone of algebraic language theory. Numerous such correspondences are known in the literature. We demonstrate that they all arise from the same recipe: one models languages and the algebras recognizing them by monads on an algebraic category, and applies a Stone-type duality. Our main contribution is a variety theorem that covers e.g. Wilke’s and Pin’s work on ∞ \infty -languages, the variety theorem for cost functions of Daviaud, Kuperberg, and Pin, and unifies the two previous categorical approaches of Bojańczyk and of Adámek et al. In addition we derive a number of new results, including an extension of the local variety theorem of Gehrke, Grigorieff, and Pin from finite to infinite words.

2]Lehrstuhl für Theoretische Informatik

1
Introduction

Algebraic language theory investigates the behaviors of finite machines
by relating them to finite algebraic structures. This has
proved
extremely
fruitful. For example, regular languages (the behaviors of finite automata) can be described purely algebraically as the
languages recognized by finite
monoids, and the decidability of
star-freeness rests on Schützenberger’s theorem
[
40
]
: a regular
language is star-free iff it is recognized by a finite aperiodic
monoid. To organize and study correspondence results of this kind systematically, Eilenberg
[
18
]
introduced the concepts of a
variety of regular languages
and a
pseudovariety of monoids
. By a pseudovariety of monoids is meant a class of finite monoids closed under homomorphic images, submonoids and finite products. A variety of regular languages associates to every finite alphabet
\Sigma
a set
W_{\Sigma}
of regular languages over
\Sigma
such that

•

W_{\Sigma}
is closed under finite union, finite intersection and complement;

•

W_{\Sigma}
is closed under derivatives: for any language
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and
y\in\Sigma^{*}
, the languages

y^{-1}L=\{\,x\in\Sigma^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}yx\in L\,\}
and
Ly^{-1}=\{\,x\in\Sigma^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}xy\in L\,\}
lie in
W_{\Sigma}
.

•

for any language
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and any monoid homomorphism
g\colon\Delta^{*}\to\Sigma^{*}
, the preimage

g^{-1}L=\{\,x\in\Delta^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g(x)\in L\,\}\subseteq\Delta^{*}

lies in
W_{\Delta}
.

Eilenberg’s celebrated
variety
theorem

[
18
]
asserts that varieties of regular languages and pseudovarieties of monoids are in bijective correspondence. This together with Reiterman’s
theorem
[
34
]
,
stating that pseudovarieties of monoids can be specified by profinite
equations, establishes a firm connection between automata, languages, and
algebras.

Over the past four decades, Eilenberg’s theorem inspired a lot of research and has been extended into two orthogonal directions. On the one hand, several authors established Eilenberg-type correspondences for classes of regular languages with modified closure properties, e.g. dropping closure under complement or intersection
[
28
,
32
,
42
,
20
]
. On the other hand, variety theorems were discovered for machine behaviors beyond finite words, including weighted languages over a
field
[
35
]
, infinite words
[
46
,
29
]
, words on linear orderings

[
8
,
9
]
, ranked trees
[
6
]
, binary trees

[
39
]
, and cost functions
[
17
]
. This plethora
of structurally similar results (often with similar proofs) has raised interest in
category-theoretic approaches which allow to derive all the
above results as special instances of only
one
general variety theorem. Two first steps in this direction, corresponding to the two orthogonal extensions of Eilenberg’s theorem, were achieved by Bojańczyk
[
11
]
and in our previous work
[
2
,
4
,
3
,
14
]
. Bojańczyk extends the
notion of language recognition by monoids to algebras for a
monad
on
sorted sets and proves a generic Eilenberg theorem, improving earlier results of Almeida
[
6
]
and Salehi and Steinby
[
38
]
on algebras over a finitary signature. In contrast, in our previous work one keeps monoids as the recognizing structures, but considers them in categories
\mathscr{D}

of (ordered) algebras such as posets, semilattices, and vector spaces. This led us to a uniform presentation of five Eilenberg theorems for languages of finite words

[
18
,
28
,
32
,
35
,
42
]
.

To obtain the desired general Eilenberg theorem, a unification of the two
approaches is required. On the one hand, one needs to take the step from sets
to more general categories
\mathscr{D}
to capture the proper notion of
language recognition; e.g. for the treatment of weighted
languages
[
35
]
one needs to work over the category of vector spaces.
On the other hand, to deal with machine
behaviors beyond finite words, one has to replace monoids by other algebraic
structures. The main contribution of this paper is a variety theorem that
achieves
the
desired unification, and in addition directly encompasses many Eilenberg-type
correspondences that follow from neither of the previous generic results, including
the
work in

[
8
,
9
,
46
,
29
,
39
,
17
]
.

To motivate our approach let us start with the observation that, traditionally, Eilenberg-type correspondences are established in essentially the same four steps:

1.

Identify an algebraic theory T such that the languages in mind are the
ones recognized by finite T-algebras. For example, for
regular languages one takes the theory of monoids.

2.

Describe the syntactic T-algebras, i.e. the minimal algebraic recognizers of languages.

3.

Infer the form of the derivatives under which varieties of languages are closed.

4.

Establish the Eilenberg correspondence between varieties of languages and
pseudovarieties of algebras by relating the languages of a variety to their corresponding syntactic algebras.

The key insight provided by this paper is that all four steps can be simplified or even completely
automatized.

For Step 1, putting a common roof over Bojańczyk’s and our own previous
work, we consider an algebraic category
\mathscr{D}
and algebras for
a
monad

\mathbf{T}
on
\mathscr{D}^{S}
, the category of
S
-sorted
\mathscr{D}
-algebras for
a
finite set
S
of sorts. This is illustrated by the following diagram:

Finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras

in
\mathscr{D}^{S}

(this paper)

Finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras

in
\mathbf{Set}^{S}

(Bojańczyk
[
11
]
)

Finite monoids

in
\mathscr{D}

(Adámek et. al.
[
2
,
4
]

Finite monoids

in
\mathbf{Set}

(Classical Eilenberg)

For example, to capture regular languages one
takes the free-monoid monad
\mathbf{T}\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
. For regular
\infty
-languages one takes the monad
\mathbf{T}(\Sigma,\Gamma)=(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega}+\Sigma^{*}\times\Gamma)
on
\mathbf{Set}^{2}
representing
\omega
-semigroups. And for weighted languages over a finite field
K
, ones takes the monad
\mathbf{T}
on the category of vector spaces representing associative algebras over
K
.

For Steps 2 and 3, we develop our main technical tool, the concept of a
unary presentation
of a monad. Intuitively, a unary presentation expresses in categorical terms how to present
\mathbf{T}
-algebras by unary operations; for example, a monoid
M
can be presented by the translation maps
x\mapsto yx
and
x\mapsto xy
for
y\in M
. It turns out that unary presentations are, in a precise sense, both necessary and sufficient for constructing syntactic algebras (Theorem
108
). This observation clarifies the role of syntactic algebras in classical work on Eilenberg-type theorems, as well as the nature of derivatives appearing in varieties of languages. In our paper, syntactic algebras are
not
used for proving the variety theorem: we rely solely on the elementary algebraic concept of a unary presentation.

We emphasize that, in general, nontrivial work lies in
finding a good unary presentation for a monad
\mathbf{T}
. However, our work here shows that
then Steps 3 and 4 are entirely generic: after choosing a unary
presentation, we obtain a notion of
variety of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages
(involving a notion of derivatives directly inferred from on the presentation) and the following

Variety Theorem.

Varieties of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages are in bijective correspondence with pseudovarieties of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras.

The proof of the Variety Theorem has two main ingredients. The first
one is duality: besides
\mathscr{D}
we also consider a variety
\mathscr{C}

that is dual to
\mathscr{D}
on the level of finite algebras. Varieties of
languages live in
\mathscr{C}
, while over
\mathscr{D}^{S}
we form pseudovarieties of

\mathbf{T}
-algebras. This is much inspired by the work of Gehrke, Grigorieff, and Pin
[
20
]
who interpreted the original Eilenberg theorem
[
18
]
in terms of Stone duality. In our categorical setting, this corresponds to choosing
\mathscr{C}=
boolean algebras and
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Set}
, and the free-monoid monad
\mathbf{T}\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
.

The second ingredient is the
profinite monad

\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
associated to
\mathbf{T}
,
introduced in
[
13
]
. It extends the classical construction of
the free profinite monoid, and allows for the introduction of profinite topological
methods to our setting. For example, Pippenger’s result
[
31
]
that the boolean
algebra of regular languages dualizes to the free profinite monoid is shown to hold at the level of monads. Moreover, the profinite monad will lead us to a categorical Reiterman theorem (Theorem
58
), asserting that pseudovarieties of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras correspond to profinite equational theories. The proof of the Variety Theorem then boils down to the fact that

(i)

varieties of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages dualize to profinite equational theories, and

(ii)

by the Reiterman theorem, profinite equational theories correspond to pseudovarieties of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras.

Our Eilenberg-Reiterman theory for a monad establishes a conceptual and highly
parametric, yet easily applicable framework for algebraic language theory. In fact, to establish an Eilenberg correspondence using our framework, the traditional four steps indicated above are replaced by the following simple three-step procedure:

1.

Find a monad
\mathbf{T}
whose finite algebras recognize the desired languages.

2.

Choose a unary presentation for
\mathbf{T}
.

3.

Spell out what a variety of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages and a pseudovariety of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras is (by instantiating our general definitions), and invoke the Variety Theorem.

To illustrate the strength of this approach, we will show that
roughly a dozen Eilenberg theorems in the literature emerge as special cases of our results. In addition, we will get several new correspondence results for free, e.g. an extension of the local
variety
theorem of
[
20
]
from finite words to infinite
words and trees, and a variety theorem for
\omega
-regular languages.

Our paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
we recall the construction of the profinite monad associated to a monad
\mathbf{T}
. In Section
3
we investigate pseudovarieties of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras and establish our categorical Reiterman theorem: pseudovarieties corrrespond to profinite theories. In Section
4
we introduce unary presentations for
\mathbf{T}
-algebras, our key concept for defining the derivatives of a language. In Section
5
we set up our duality framework for algebraic language theory and demonstrate that some important topological characterizations of regular languages hold at the level of monads. In Section
6
we present the main result of our paper, the variety theorem for varieties of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages and pseudovarieties of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras. In Section 7 the variety theorem is generalized to reduced languages and reduced
\mathbf{T}
-algebras, a useful variant in a many-sorted setting. Finally, in Section
8
, we derive about twenty concrete Eilenberg correspondences as direct instances of our general results, including a number of correspondences that are not yet know in the literature.

Readers should be familiar with basic category theory, topology and universal algebra. A toolkit collecting some important definitions and facts is given in the Appendix.

2
The Profinite Monad

In the section, we recall from
[
13
]
the construction of the
profinite monad

\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
associated to a monad
\mathbf{T}
. The profinite monad will play a key role for our categorical approach to Eilenberg-type theorems.

Recall that for a finitary one-sorted signature
\Gamma
, a
variety of algebras
is a class of
\Gamma
-algebras presented by equations
s=t
between
\Gamma
-terms. A
variety of ordered algebras
is a class of ordered
\Gamma
-algebras (i.e.
\Gamma
-algebras on a poset with monotone
\Gamma
-operations) presented by inequations
s\leq t
between
\Gamma
-terms. Morphisms of (ordered)
\Gamma
-algebras are (monotone)
\Gamma
-homomorphisms. In our applications we will work with the following varieties:

{tabularx}

lX

\mathbf{Set}
sets and functions

\mathbf{Pos}
posets and monotone maps

\mathbf{BA}
boolean algebras and boolean homomorphisms

\mathbf{DL}_{01}
distributive lattices with
0
and
1
and lattice homomorphisms preserving
0
and
1

{\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}
join-semilattices with
0
and semilattice homomorphisms preserving
0

\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
vector spaces over a finite field
K
and linear maps

We view
\mathbf{Pos}
as a variety of ordered algebras over the empty signature, and the other categories above as varieties of algebras. All these varieties are
locally finite
, i.e. every finitely generated algebra is finite.

Notation 1
.

For a category
\mathscr{D}
and a set
S
of sorts, we denote by
\mathscr{D}^{S}
the
S
-fold product category of
\mathscr{D}
. Thus an object of
\mathscr{D}^{S}
is a family
D=(D_{s})_{s\in S}
of objects in
\mathscr{D}
and a morphism
f\colon D\to D^{\prime}
is a family
(f_{s}\colon D_{s}\to D^{\prime}_{s})_{s\in S}
of morphisms in
\mathscr{D}
, with composition and identities taken sortwise. We often write
f
for
f_{s}
if the sort
s
is clear from the context. If
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of algebras or ordered algebras, we call a morphism
f
of
\mathscr{D}^{S}

surjective (injective, order-reflecting)
if this property holds sortwise, i.e. for every component
f_{s}
.

For the rest of this paper let us fix the following data:

(i)

a variety
\mathscr{D}
of algebras or ordered algebras;

(ii)

a finite set
S
of sorts;

(iii)

a monad
\mathbf{T}=(T,\eta,\mu)
on
\mathscr{D}^{S}
;

(iv)

a class
\mathcal{F}
of finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras.

We denote by
\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}

the categories of (finite)
\mathbf{T}
-algebras and
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms, and we view
\mathcal{F}
as a full subcategory of
\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,\mathbf{T}
.

The purpose of the monad
\mathbf{T}
is to represent formal languages and the possibly many-sorted algebras recognizing them. Languages are modeled as morphisms from free
\mathbf{T}
-algebras into a suitable object of outputs in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
. The class
\mathcal{F}
consists of those finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras that we use to recognize languages. In most applications, we will chose
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras.

Example 2

(Monoids)
.

Languages of finite words are represented by the free-monoid monad
\mathbf{T}_{\ast}\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
whose algebras are monoids. A language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
corresponds (via its characteristic function) to a function from
T_{\ast}\Sigma
into the set
\{0,1\}
.

Example 3

(
\omega
-semigroups)
.

Languages of finite and infinite words (
\infty
-languages
for short) are
represented by the monad

\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{2}
associated to
the
algebraic theory
of
\omega
-semigroups
[
27
]
. By an
\omega
-semigroup
is meant a
two-sorted
set
A=(A_{+},A_{\omega})

equipped with a binary product
A_{+}\times A_{+}\xrightarrow{\cdot}A_{+}
, a mixed
binary product
A_{+}\times A_{\omega}\xrightarrow{\cdot}A_{\omega}
and an

\omega
-ary
product
A_{+}^{\omega}\xrightarrow{\pi}A_{\omega}
satisfying
the following (mixed) associative laws:

(i)

(a\cdot b)\cdot c=a\cdot(b\cdot c)
for all
a,b,c\in A_{+}
;

(ii)

(a\cdot b)\cdot z=a\cdot(b\cdot z)
for all
a,b\in A_{+}
and
z\in A_{\omega}
;

(iii)

\pi(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\ldots)=a_{0}\cdot\pi(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots)
for all
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},,\ldots\in A_{+}
;

(iv)

\pi(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},\ldots)=\pi(a_{0}\ldots a_{k_{0}-1},a_{k_{0}}\ldots a_{k_{1}-1},a_{k_{1}}\ldots a_{k_{2}-1},\ldots)
for all
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\ldots\in A_{+}
and all strictly increasing sequences
0<k_{0}<k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots
of natural numbers.

A
morphism
between two
\omega
-semigroups
A
and
B
is a two-sorted map
h\colon A\to B
that preserves all finite and infinite products. The free
\omega
-semigroup generated by the two-sorted set

(\Sigma,\Gamma)
is carried by
(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega}+\Sigma^{*}\times\Gamma)
, with products given by concatenation of words. Thus
T_{\infty}(\Sigma,\Gamma)=(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega}+\Sigma^{*}\times\Gamma)
and, in particular,
T_{\infty}(\Sigma,\emptyset)=(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
. An
\infty
-language
L\subseteq(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
thus corresponds to a two-sorted function from
T_{\infty}(\Sigma,\emptyset)
to
(\{0,1\},\{0,1\})\in\mathbf{Set}^{2}
.

Example 4

(
K
-algebras)
.

Weighted languages over a finite field

K
are represented
by the monad
\mathbf{T}_{K}
on
\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
constructing free

K
-algebras (= associative algebras over the field
K
). For the vector space
K^{\Sigma}
with finite basis
\Sigma
we
have

\mathbf{T}_{K}(K^{\Sigma})=K[\Sigma]
, the
K
-algebra of all polynomials
\sum_{i<n}k_{i}w_{i}
with
k_{i}\in K
and

w_{i}\in\Sigma^{*}
. Since
K[\Sigma]
has basis
\Sigma^{*}
, a weighted language
L\colon\Sigma^{*}\to K
corresponds to a linear map from
T_{K}(K^{\Sigma})
to
K
.

Assumptions 5
.

From now on, we assume that

(i)

the variety
\mathscr{D}
is locally finite;

(ii)

all epimorphisms in
\mathscr{D}
are surjective;

(iii)

T
preserves epimorphisms;

(iv)

\mathcal{F}
is closed under quotients, subalgebras and finite products.

Remark 6
.

1.

The variety
\mathscr{D}
has the factorization system of surjective morphisms and injective (resp. order-reflecting) morphisms, cf.
A
. It extends sortwise to
\mathscr{D}^{S}
.
Quotients
and
subobjects
is
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{D}^{S}
are taken in this factorization system.

2.

Since

T

preserves surjections, the factorization system of
\mathscr{D}^{S}
lifts to the category
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
: every
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism factorizes into a
surjective homomorphism followed by an injective (resp.
order-reflecting) one.

Quotients
and
subalgebras
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
are taken in this
factorization system.

3.

The
homomorphism theorem
holds for
\mathbf{T}
-algebras: let
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
and
h\colon A\to C
be
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms with
e
surjective, and suppose that, for every sort
s
and any two elements
a,a^{\prime}\in{|A_{s}|}
,

e(a)=e(a^{\prime})\quad\text{implies}\quad h(a)=h(a^{\prime}),

respectively (in the ordered case)

e(a)\leq e(a^{\prime})\quad\text{implies}\quad h(a)\leq h(a^{\prime}).

Then there exists a unique
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
\overline{h}\colon B\to C
with
h=\overline{h}\cdot e
. Indeed, the homomorphism theorem for the
S
-sorted variety
\mathscr{D}^{S}
(see
C
) yields a morphism
\overline{h}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
with
h=\overline{h}\cdot e
, and since
T
preserves surjections,
\overline{h}
is a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism.

Notation 7
.

For a variety
\mathscr{D}
of algebras, let
\mathbf{Stone}(\mathscr{D})
be the category of
Stone
\mathscr{D}
-algebras
; its objects are
\mathscr{D}
-algebras equipped with a Stone topology and continuous
\mathscr{D}
-operations, and its morphisms are continuous
\mathscr{D}
-morphisms. Similarly, for a variety
\mathscr{D}
of ordered algebras, let
\mathbf{Priest}(\mathscr{D})
be the category of
Priestley
\mathscr{D}
-algebras
; its objects are
\mathscr{D}
-algebras with a Priestley topology and continuous monotone
\mathscr{D}
-operations, and its morphisms are continuous monotone
\mathscr{D}
-morphisms. We denote by
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Stone}(\mathscr{D})
(resp.
\mathbf{Priest}(\mathscr{D})
) on
profinite
\mathscr{D}
-algebras
, i.e. inverse limits of algebras in
\mathscr{D}_{f}
. Here
\mathscr{D}_{f}
is viewed as a full subcategory of
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
by equipping objects of
\mathscr{D}_{f}
with the discrete topology.

Example 8
.

For the varieties
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Set}
,
\mathbf{Pos}
,
{\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}
and
\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
that we encounter in our applications, every Stone (resp. Priestley)
\mathscr{D}
-algebra is profinite: we have

1.

\widehat{\mathbf{Set}}=\mathbf{Stone}
(Stone spaces);

2.

\widehat{\mathbf{Pos}}=\mathbf{Priest}
(Priestley spaces);

3.

\widehat{{\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}}=\mathbf{Stone}({\mathbf{JSL}_{0}})
(Stone semilattices);

4.

\widehat{\mathbf{Vec}_{K}}=\mathbf{Stone}(\mathbf{Vec}_{K})
(Stone vector spaces).

See e.g. Johnstone
[
21
]
.

In the following we establish some important properties of the category
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
. Recall from
A
the concept of a
pro-completion
(= free completion under cofiltered limits) of a category.

Lemma 9
.

\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
is the pro-completion of
\mathscr{D}_{f}
.

A proof is sketched in
[
21
, Remark VI.2.4]
for the unordered case, and the argument is similar for the ordered case. For convenience, we present a complete proof for the latter.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of ordered algebras. By
[
7
, Prop. 1]
, the category
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
is complete (with
limits formed on the level of
\mathbf{Set}
) and, by definition, every object of

\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
is a cofiltered limit of objects in
\mathscr{D}_{f}
. Thus, by

A
, it only remains to show that every object
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}
is
finitely copresentable in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
: given a cofiltered limit cone
(p_{i}\colon X\to X_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
and a morphism
f\colon X\to D
, we need to show that
f

factors through the cone essentially uniquely. The uniqueness part is clear since,
forgetting the
\mathscr{D}
-algebraic structure,
D
is finitely copresentable in

\mathbf{Priest}
. The existence of a factorization is established as follows:

(1)

We first consider the case where all
X_{i}
’s are finite. Since
(p_{i})
is a cofiltered
limit cone in
\mathbf{Priest}
and
D
is a finite poset with discrete topology (and
thus finitely copresentable in
\mathbf{Priest}
), there exists an
i\in I
and a
monotone map
f^{\prime}\colon X_{i}\to D
with
f^{\prime}\cdot p_{i}=f
. Choose
j\in I
and a
connecting morphism
g\colon X_{j}\to X_{i}
with
g[X_{j}]=p_{i}[X]
, see Lemma

B.4
. We claim that the composite
h=f^{\prime}\cdot g
is a
morphism of
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
, i.e. preserves all
\mathscr{D}
-operations. Indeed, given an

n
-ary operation symbol
\sigma
in the signature of
\mathscr{D}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in X_{j}
, choose
x_{k}^{\prime}\in X
with
g(x_{k})=p_{i}(x_{k}^{\prime})
for each
k=1,\ldots,n
.
Then

\displaystyle h(\sigma(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}))

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}(g(\sigma(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})))

\displaystyle(\text{$h=f^{\prime}g$})

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}(\sigma(g(x_{1}),\ldots,g(x_{n})))

\displaystyle(\text{$g$ morphism of $\widehat{\mathscr{D}}$})

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}(\sigma(p_{i}(x_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,p_{i}(x_{n}^{\prime})))

\displaystyle(\text{$g(x_{k}^{\prime})=p_{i}(x_{k}^{\prime})$})

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}(p_{i}(\sigma(x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{n}^{\prime})))

\displaystyle(\text{$p_{i}$ morphism of $\widehat{\mathscr{D}}$})

\displaystyle=f(\sigma(x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{n}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle(\text{$f^{\prime}p_{i}=f$})

\displaystyle=\sigma(f(x_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,f(x_{n}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle(\text{$f$ morphism of $\widehat{\mathscr{D}}$})

\displaystyle=\sigma(f^{\prime}\cdot p_{i}(x_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,f^{\prime}\cdot p_{i}(x_{n}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle(\text{$f=f^{\prime}p_{i}$})

\displaystyle=\sigma(f^{\prime}\cdot g(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{\prime}\cdot g(x_{n}))

\displaystyle(\text{$p_{i}(x_{k}^{\prime})=g(x_{k})$})

\displaystyle=\sigma(h(x_{1}),\ldots,h(x_{n}))

\displaystyle(\text{$h=f^{\prime}g$})

Thus
h
is a morphism in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
. Moreover, we have
f=f^{\prime}\cdot p_{i}=f^{\prime}\cdot g\cdot p_{j}=h\cdot p_{j}
, i.e.
f
factors through
p_{j}
.

(2)

Now let the
X_{i}
’s be arbitrary. We may assume that
I
is a codirected
poset, see
A
. The connecting morphism for
i\leq j
is denoted by
g_{ij}\colon X_{i}\to X_{j}
. Form the codirected poset

J=\{\,(i,e)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\in I\text{ and }e\colon X_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A_{e}\text{ is a surjective morphism in $\widehat{\mathscr{D}}$ with $A_{e}\in\mathscr{D}_{f}$ }\,\}

ordered by

(i,e)\leq(j,q)\quad\text{iff}\quad i\leq j\text{ and }q\cdot g_{ij}=g\cdot e\text{ for some }g\colon A_{e}\to A_{q}.

Note that
g
is necessarily unique because
e
is surjective. A routine verification shows that the diagram
Q\colon J\to\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
given by

(i,e)\mapsto A_{e}\quad\text{and}\quad((i,e)\leq(j,q))\mapsto g

has the limit cone

(e\cdot p_{i}\colon X\to A_{e})_{(i,e)\in J}.

By (1), there exists an
(i,e)\in J
and a morphism
f^{\prime}\colon A_{e}\to D
with
f^{\prime}\cdot e\cdot p_{i}=f
. This shows that
f
factors through
p_{i}
. ∎

Remark 10
.

From the above lemma and

A
, it follows the category
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
is
locally
finitely copresentable, and its finitely copresentable objects are the objects
of
\mathscr{D}_{f}
. Since the set
S
of sorts is assumed to be finite, this implies that the product
category
\mathscr{D}^{S}
is also locally finitely copresentable and its finitely
copresentable objects
are the objects of
\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
.

Notation 11
.

We have the two forgetful functors

V\colon\widehat{\mathscr{D}}\to\mathscr{D}\quad\text{and}\quad V^{S}\colon\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}\to\mathscr{D}^{S}.

We usually drop the superscript and write
V
for
V^{S}
. Moreover, we write
D
for
VD
if
D
is finite, and likewise
h
for
Vh
if
h
is a morphism between finite objects.

Remark 12
.

The forgetful functor
V\colon\widehat{\mathscr{D}}\to\mathscr{D}
is a right adjoint, see
[
13
, Proposition 2.8]
. Consequently, it preserves limits and in particular monomorphisms. Since limits in

\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
and
\mathscr{D}^{S}
are computed sortwise, the same holds for the
S
-sorted
forgetful functor
V\colon\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}\to\mathscr{D}^{S}
.

Lemma 13
.

All epimorphisms in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
are surjective.

Proof.

(1)

First, observe that all monomorphisms in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
are injective because

V\colon\widehat{\mathscr{D}}\to\mathscr{D}
preserves
monomorphisms, see Remark
12
, and monomorphisms in the variety
\mathscr{D}
are injective.

(2)

We show that every epimorphism
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathscr{D}_{f}
is an epimorphism in
\mathscr{D}
(and therefore surjective by Assumption
5
(ii)). To this end, suppose that

f,g\colon B\to C
are morphisms
in
\mathscr{D}
with
f\cdot e=g\cdot e
. Express
C
as a directed union
(c_{i}\colon C_{i}\rightarrowtail C)_{i\in I}
of finite subobjects, using that
\mathscr{D}
is a locally
finite variety. Since
B
is finite and the union is directed, the morphisms

f
and
g
factor through some
c_{i}
, i.e. there exist morphisms
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}

with
f=c_{i}\cdot f^{\prime}
and
g=c_{i}\cdot g^{\prime}
. Then

c_{i}\cdot f^{\prime}\cdot e=f\cdot e=g\cdot e=c_{i}\cdot g^{\prime}\cdot e,

and since
c_{i}
is monic, it follows that
f^{\prime}\cdot e=g^{\prime}\cdot e
.
Since
e
is an epimorphism in
\mathscr{D}_{f}
and
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
are morphisms in
\mathscr{D}_{f}
, this implies

f^{\prime}=g^{\prime}
and therefore
f=g
.

(3)

We prove that every epimorphism
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
with
B\in\mathscr{D}_{f}
is surjective. To see this, factorize
e
as
e=m\cdot q
with
q
surjective and
m
injective/order-reflecting. The morphism
m
has finite domain and is thus a morphism in
\mathscr{D}_{f}
. Moreover, since
e=m\cdot q
and
e
is an epimorphism in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
, so is
m
. In particular,
m
is an epimorphism in
\mathscr{D}_{f}
. Then part (2) shows that
m
is surjective, which by the equation
e=m\cdot q
implies that
e
is surjective.

(4)

Now let
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
be an arbitrary epimorphism in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
. By

A
, one can express
e
in the locally finitely copresentable
category
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{\to}
as a cofiltered limit
((a_{i},b_{i})\colon e\to f_{i})_{i\in I}

of morphisms
f_{i}\colon A_{i}\to B_{i}
in
\mathscr{D}_{f}
. Take the (epi, strong mono)
factorizations of
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
, see
A
(c). Diagonal
fill-in gives a morphism
e_{i}
as in the
diagram
below:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{a_{i}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{i}}
\scriptstyle{b_{i}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A_{i}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{i}}
\scriptstyle{a_{i}^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{B_{i}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{i}^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\textstyle{B_{i}}

The objects

A_{i}^{\prime}

and
B_{i}^{\prime}
are finite by part (1) of the proof. Moreover, since
e
and
b_{i}^{\prime}

are
epimorphic, so is
e_{i}
, and thus part (2) shows that
e_{i}
is
surjective. Moreover, by part (3), also
a_{i}^{\prime}
is surjective. Finally, observe that
((a_{i}^{\prime},b_{i}^{\prime})\colon e\to e_{i})_{i\in I}
is a
cofiltered limit cone in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{\to}
by
A
(a),(c). Since limits in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{\to}
are computed componentwise,
Lemma
B.1
shows
that
e
is surjective.∎

In the algebraic theory of regular languages, topology enters the stage via the Stone space
\widehat{\Sigma^{*}}
of
profinite words
over an alphabet
\Sigma
. It is formed as the inverse (a.k.a. cofiltered) limit of all finite quotient monoids of the free monoid
\Sigma^{*}
. In
[
13
]
we generalized this construction from monoids to algebras for a monad, leading to the concept of the profinite monad
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
and, more generally, the pro-
\mathcal{F}
monad
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
associated to the monad
\mathbf{T}
. In the following, we review the construction of pro-
\mathcal{F}
monads and their basic properties. Recall from
A
the concept of a
codensity monad
of a functor.

Definition 14

(see
[
13
]
)
.

The
pro-
\mathcal{F}
monad
of
\mathbf{T}
is the codensity monad of the functor

K\colon\mathcal{F}\to\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S},\quad(A,\alpha)\mapsto A.

We denote this monad on
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
by
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}=(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}},{\hat{\eta}}^{\mathcal{F}},{\hat{\mu}}^{\mathcal{F}})
. If
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras, we write
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
for
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
and call
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
the
profinite monad
of
\mathbf{T}
.

Remark 15

(see
[
13
]
)
.

1.

A more explicit construction of
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
can be described as follows. For any
\mathscr{D}\in\mathscr{D}^{S}_{f}
, let
\mathbf{T}D=(TD,\mu_{D})
be the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra on
D
(cf.
A
). Form the comma category
(\mathbf{T}D\downarrow\mathcal{F})
of all

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms
h\colon\mathbf{T}D\to(A,\alpha)
with
(A,\alpha)\in\mathcal{F}
, see

A
. Then the object
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
is the limit of the cofiltered diagram

(\mathbf{T}D\downarrow\mathcal{F})\to\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S},\quad(h\colon\mathbf{T}D\to(A,\alpha))\to A.

The cofilteredness of
(\mathbf{T}D\downarrow\mathcal{F})
follows from our assumption that
\mathcal{F}
is closed under subalgebras and finite products. We denote the limit projection associated to
h\colon\mathbf{T}D\to(A,\alpha)
by

h^{+}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\to A.

In particular, for any
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)\in\mathcal{F}
we have the projection

\alpha^{+}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}A\to A

because
\alpha\colon\mathbf{T}A\to(A,\alpha)
is a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism by the associative law. The unit
{\hat{\eta}}^{\mathcal{F}}_{D}
and multiplication
{\hat{\mu}}^{\mathcal{F}}_{D}
are uniquely determined by the commutative diagrams

$(\mathbf{T}D\downarrow\mathcal{F})$}.

(1)

2.

In the above explicit construction of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)
(
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
) as a cofiltered limit, it actually suffices to form
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
as the limit of all
quotients
of
\mathbf{T}D
with codomain in
\mathcal{F}
. More precisely, let
\mathsf{Quo}(\mathbf{T}D,\mathcal{F})
be the full subcategory of
(\mathbf{T}D\downarrow\mathcal{F})
on all surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms
e\colon\mathbf{T}D\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
with codomain in
\mathcal{F}
. Then the inclusion functor
\mathsf{Quo}(\mathbf{T}D,\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow(\mathbf{T}D\downarrow\mathcal{F})
is final (see

A
). Therefore
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
is also the cofiltered limit of the smaller diagram

\mathsf{Quo}(\mathbf{T}D,\mathcal{F})\to\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S},\quad(e\colon\mathbf{T}D\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha))\to A,

and the above limit cone
(\,h^{+}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\to A\,)_{h\in(\mathbf{T}D\downarrow F)}
restricts to a limit cone

(\,e^{+}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow A\,)_{e\in\mathsf{Quo}(\mathbf{T}D,\mathcal{F})}.

Note that the projections
e^{+}
are surjective by Lemma
B.2
.

Example 16
.

1.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\ast}
on
\mathbf{Set}
and
\mathcal{F}=
all finite monoids.
The monad
\widehat{\mathbf{T}_{*}}
on
\mathbf{Stone}
assigns to each finite set (= finite discrete space)

\Sigma
the
Stone space
\widehat{\Sigma^{*}}
of profinite words, obtained as the inverse limit of all finite quotient monoids of the free monoid
\Sigma^{*}
.

2.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{K}
on
\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
and
\mathcal{F}=
all finite vector spaces. The monad
\widehat{\mathbf{T}_{K}}
on

\mathbf{Stone}(\mathbf{Vec}_{K})
assigns
to each finite vector space
K^{\Sigma}
the Stone vector space obtained as
the limit of all finite linear quotient spaces of
K[\Sigma]
.

Remark 17
.

We list some important technical properties of
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
that we will use throughout our paper. See
[
13
]
for proofs.

1.

There is a natural transformation

\iota\colon TV\to V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}
(where
V\colon\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}\to\mathscr{D}^{S}
is the forgetful functor) whose component

\iota_{D}\colon TVD\to V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
for
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
is uniquely determined by the commutative triangles

\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 55.83649pt\raise-15.73611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.17558pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Vh^{+}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 55.83649pt\raise-24.19446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.47223pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern The morphism
\iota_{D}
is
dense
, i.e. its image forms (sortwise) a dense subset of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
. The density of
\iota_{D}
implies two statements that we will use frequently:

(i)

For any two morphisms
f,g\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\to A
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
with
Vf\cdot\iota_{D}=Vg\cdot\iota_{D}
, one has
f=g
. Indeed, this follows from the fact that the topology of
A
is Hausdorff.

(ii)

For any surjective morphism
e\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow A
in

\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
with finite codomain, the restriction
Ve\cdot\iota_{D}\colon TD\twoheadrightarrow A
is also surjective, as this map is dense and the topology of
A
is discrete.

2.

If
(A,\alpha)\in\mathcal{F}
, then
(A,\alpha^{+})
is a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra: putting
h=\alpha
in the commutative diagram (
1
) gives the
unit and associative law. Conversely, every finite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra
(B,\beta)
restricts to the finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(B,V\beta\cdot\iota_{B})
in
\mathcal{F}
. This yields
an isomorphism

\mathcal{F}\cong\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}

given by

(A,\alpha)\mapsto(A,\alpha^{+})
and
h\mapsto h
; its inverse is given by
(B,\beta)\mapsto(B,V\beta\cdot\iota_{B})
and
h\mapsto h
. We will therefore tacitly identify finite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras with
\mathbf{T}
-algebras in
\mathcal{F}
.

3.

Every
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism
h\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D\to(B,\beta)
with
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
and finite codomain
(B,\beta)
restricts to the
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
Vh\cdot\iota_{D}\colon\mathbf{T}D\to(B,V\beta\cdot\iota_{B})
.

4.

Every finite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra is a finitely
copresentable object (cf.
A
) in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
.

5.

\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}
preserves
surjections. Therefore, in analogy to Remark
6
, the factorization system of surjective and injective/order-reflecting morphisms of
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
lifts to
\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
.
Quotients
and
subalgebras
of
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras are taken w.r.t. this factorization system. Moreover, the homomorphism theorem holds for
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras.

Lemma 18
.

Every
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}D^{\prime}\to\mathbf{T}D
with
D,D^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
extends
uniquely to a

{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism
{\hat{g}}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D^{\prime}\to{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D
such that the following
diagrams commute for all
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms
h\colon\mathbf{T}D\to A
with
A\in\mathcal{F}
:

\textstyle{TD^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{D^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{TD\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{D}}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V{\hat{g}}}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D}

\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{g}}
\scriptstyle{{(hg)^{+}}}
\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{h^{+}}}
\textstyle{A}

(2)

Proof.

The morphisms
(hg)^{+}
form a compatible family over the diagram defining

\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
, i.e. for all
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms
k\colon A\to A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{F}
we
have
(khg)^{+}=k\cdot(hg)^{+}
. Indeed, this holds when precomposed with the
dense map
\iota_{D^{\prime}}
:

V(khg)^{+}\cdot\iota_{D^{\prime}}=khg=k\cdot V(hg)^{+}\cdot\iota_{D^{\prime}}.

Thus there exists a unique
\hat{g}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D^{\prime}\to\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
with
(hg)^{+}=h^{+}\cdot\hat{g}
for all
h
, i.e. making the right-hand diagram of (
2
) commute. This
also implies that the left-hand diagram commutes, since it commutes when
postcomposed with every limit projection
Vh^{+}
(cf. Remark
12
).

\textstyle{TD^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{D^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{hg}
\textstyle{TD\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{D}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\hat{g}}
\scriptstyle{V(hg)^{+}}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Vh^{+}}
\textstyle{A}

It remains to show that
\hat{g}
is a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism. To this end consider the following diagrams for all
h\colon\mathbf{T}D\to(A,\alpha)
with
(A,\alpha)\in\mathcal{F}
:

\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(hg)^{+}}
\scriptstyle{{\hat{\mu}}^{\mathcal{F}}_{D^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}\hat{g}}
\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{g}}
\scriptstyle{(hg)^{+}}
\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\hat{\mu}}^{\mathcal{F}}_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}h^{+}}
\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{+}}
\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{+}}
\textstyle{A}

The outside and the lower square commute by (
1
), and the left-hand and right-hand parts by the definition of
\hat{g}
. Therefore the upper square commutes when postcomposed with the limit projections
h^{+}
. It follows that the upper square commutes, showing that
\hat{g}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D^{\prime}\to{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D
is a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism.
∎

3
Pseudovarieties of

\mathbf{T}
-algebras

In this section we study pseudovarieties of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras, the
algebraic half of any Eilenberg-type correspondence, and their connection to profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras. The main result is a categorical Reiterman theorem that describes pseudovarieties of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras in terms of
pro-
\mathcal{F}
theories
. This result will form the basis for our duality approach to Eilenberg-type theorems.

3.1
Profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras

In the last section, we introduced the category
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
of profinite
\mathscr{D}
-algebras. The concept of profiniteness can be extended to
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras as follows.

Definition 19
.

A
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra is called
profinite
if it is a cofiltered limit of finite

{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras (= finite
\mathcal{F}
-algebras, see Remark
17
.
2
) in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
.

Remark 20
.

Every free
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D
with
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
is profinite. Indeed,
since the forgetful functor from
\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
to
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
reflects
limits, see

A
, the
right-hand square of (
1
) shows that the
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D

is the cofiltered limit of the diagram

(\mathbf{T}D\downarrow\mathcal{F})\to\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}},\quad(h\colon\mathbf{T}D\to(A,\alpha))\mapsto(A,\alpha^{+}),

with limit projections
h^{+}
.

Lemma 21
.

Every profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra
A
is the cofiltered limit of its canonical diagram. That is, the cofiltered
diagram

(A\downarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})\to\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}},\quad(h\colon A\to A^{\prime})\mapsto A^{\prime},

has limit
A
with limit projections
h\colon A\to A^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
A
be a profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra. Then, by definition, there exists a cofiltered limit cone
(p_{i}\colon A\to A_{i})_{i\in I}
in

\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
with
A_{i}\in\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
. Since the forgetful functor
\hat{U}\colon\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
reflects
limits,
it suffices to show that the cofiltered cone
(h\colon\hat{U}A\to\hat{U}A^{\prime})
in

\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
is a
limit cone.
To this end
we verify the criterion of
A
.

For (i), let
f\colon\hat{U}A\to B
be a morphism in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
with

B\in\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{f}^{S}
. Since
\hat{U}
preserves limits, we have the limit
cone
(p_{i}\colon\hat{U}A\to\hat{U}A_{i})
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
. Moreover, since
B

is finitely copresentable in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
, see Remark
10
,
there exists an
i
and morphism
f^{\prime}\colon\hat{U}A_{i}\to B
with

f=f^{\prime}\cdot p_{i}
. This proves that
f
factors through the cone

(h\colon\hat{U}A\to\hat{U}A^{\prime})
via
h=p_{i}
and
f^{\prime}
, as desired.

For (ii), suppose that
h\colon A\to A^{\prime}
in
(A\downarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})
and

f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}\colon\hat{U}A^{\prime}\to B
are given with

f^{\prime}\cdot h=f^{\prime\prime}\cdot h
. Since
A^{\prime}
is finitely copresentable in

\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
by Remark
17
.
4
, there
exists
an
i
and a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism
h^{\prime}\colon A_{i}\to A^{\prime}
with
h=h^{\prime}\cdot p_{i}
. Then

(f^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})\cdot p_{i}=(f^{\prime\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})\cdot p_{i}
. Thus, by (ii) applied to
the cofiltered limit cone
(p_{i}\colon\hat{U}A\to\hat{U}A_{i})
, we have a connecting morphism

a_{ji}\colon A_{j}\to A_{i}

with
f^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime}\cdot a_{ji}=f^{\prime\prime}\cdot h^{\prime}\cdot a_{ji}
. Thus
h^{\prime}\cdot a_{ji}\colon p_{j}\to h
is a morphism in
(A\downarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})
that
merges
f^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime\prime}
, as desired.

\textstyle{\hat{U}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{j}}
\scriptstyle{p_{i}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\hat{U}A_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{ji}}
\textstyle{\hat{U}A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\hat{U}A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{B}

∎

Notation 22
.

Let
(A\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})
be the full subcategory of
(A\downarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})
on all surjective
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphisms
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
with
finite codomain.

Corollary 23
.

A
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra
A
is profinite iff
A
is the limit of the cofiltered
diagram

(A\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})\to\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}},\quad(e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime})\mapsto A^{\prime},

with limit projections
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Remark
17
.
5
, the inclusion functor
(A\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})\rightarrowtail(A\downarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})
is final. This immediately implies the claim, see
A
.
∎

3.2
Local pseudovarieties and the local Reiterman theorem

In this subsection, we present a local version of a categorical Reiterman theorem. “Local” means that we fix a finite
S
-sorted set
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
and consider finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras and profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras equipped with a
\Sigma
-indexed set of generators. On the level of languages, this will correspond to considering languages over the alphabet
\Sigma
.

Notation 24
.

For any
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
, let
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
denote the free object in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
generated by
\Sigma
, taken w.r.t. the forgetful functor
{|\mathord{-}|}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{D}^{S}\to\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
. Note that
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is finite because
\mathscr{D}
is locally finite. Recall that
\mathsf{Quo}(\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}},\mathcal{F})
denotes the class of all quotients
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
with codomain
A\in\mathcal{F}
. We view it as a poset ordered by
e\leq e^{\prime}
if
e
factorizes through
e^{\prime}
, i.e.
e=q\cdot e^{\prime}
for some
q
. As usual, two quotients
e
and
e^{\prime}
are identified if
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq e
, i.e. if they are connected by an isomorphism.

Definition 25
.

Let
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
. By a
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebra
is meant an element of
\mathsf{Quo}(\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}},\mathcal{F})
.
A
local pseudovariety of

\Sigma
-generated

\mathcal{F}
-algebras
is a nonempty class
\mathscr{P}\subseteq\mathsf{Quo}(\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}},\mathcal{F})
of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras that forms an ideal of the poset
\mathsf{Quo}(\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}},\mathcal{F})
, i.e.

(i)

\mathscr{P}
is downwards closed:
e_{0}\leq e_{1}
and
e_{1}\in\mathscr{P}
implies
e_{0}\in\mathscr{P}
;

(ii)

\mathscr{P}
is directed: for any two elements
e_{0},e_{1}\in\mathscr{P}
, there exists an
e\in\mathscr{P}
with
e_{0},e_{1}\leq e
.

Remark 26
.

Recall from
A
the notion of a
subdirect product
of two quotients.
Clearly condition (ii) in the above definition can be replaced by

(ii’)

\mathscr{P}
is closed under subdirect products: for any two elements
e_{0},e_{1}\in\mathscr{P}
, the subdirect product
e
of
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
lies in
\mathscr{P}
.

Definition 27
.

A
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra
is a
quotient

\varphi\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P
in

\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
whose codomain
P
is a profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra.
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras are
ordered by

\varphi\leq\varphi^{\prime}
iff
\varphi
factors through
\varphi^{\prime}
.

Our goal in this subsection is to show that
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras and local pseudovarieties of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras are equivalent concepts (see Theorem
31
). To this end, we first explain how
to translate a local pseudovariety into a
\Sigma
-generated
profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra and vice versa.

Construction 28
.

1.

To each local pseudovariety
\mathscr{P}
of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras
we associate a
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra

\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P^{\mathscr{P}}

as follows.
Viewed as full
subcategory of the comma category
(\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\downarrow\mathcal{F})
, the
category
\mathscr{P}
is cofiltered because
\mathscr{P}
is directed w.r.t.
\leq
.
Let
P^{\mathscr{P}}
be the cofiltered limit of the diagram

\mathscr{P}\to\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}},\quad(e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A)\mapsto A,

and denote the limit projections by

e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\colon P^{\mathscr{P}}\twoheadrightarrow A.

The projections are
surjective by Lemma
B.2
.
Thus
P^{\mathscr{P}}
is a profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra. Moreover, the

{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphisms
e^{+}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A

(where
e
ranges over all elements of
\mathscr{P}
) form a compatible family over the
above diagram. Hence there exists a unique
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism

\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P^{\mathscr{P}}
with
e^{+}=e_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}\cdot\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}
for
all
e\in\mathscr{P}
.

\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{P^{\mathscr{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{A}

Note that
\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}
is surjective by Lemma

B.1
. Thus
\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P^{\mathscr{P}}
is a
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra.

2.

Conversely, given a
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra

\varphi\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P
, define
\mathscr{P}^{\varphi}
to be the class of all

\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras of the form

e=(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 8.61108pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.61108pt\raise where

is a surjective
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism with
A\in\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}\cong\mathcal{F}
. Note that any such morphism

is indeed a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism by Remark
17
.
1
and
17
.
3
.
One easily verifies that
\mathscr{P}^{\varphi}
forms a local pseudovariety of

\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras.

Lemma 30
.

For each
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra
\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P
we have
\varphi=\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}
where
\mathscr{P}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathscr{P}^{\varphi}
.

More precisely, the lemma states that
\varphi
and

\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}
are identical as quotients of
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
,
i.e. there exists
an
isomorphism
j\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\xrightarrow{\cong}P^{\mathscr{P}}
with

\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}=j\cdot\varphi
.

Proof.

Let
(P\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})
be the full subcategory of
(P\downarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})
on all surjective
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphisms
e^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
with
A\in\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
. Consider the functor

that maps
