to the
\Sigma
-generated finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebra

F(e^{\prime})=(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.22743pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.22743pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.11136pt\raise 5.00694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.22743pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.22743pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.21129pt\raise 6.07222pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.71112pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{V\varphi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 90.72054pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.99997pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 90.72054pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{VP\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.02225pt\raise 5.8978pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.8978pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Ve^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 136.58513pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.99997pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 136.58513pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces).

and acts as identity on morphisms. Note that
F(e^{\prime})\in\mathscr{P}
by the
definition of
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}^{\varphi}
, so
F
is well-defined. We claim that
F
is
an isomorphism. Indeed,
F
is injective on objects because

\varphi
is surjective and
\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is dense. The surjectivity on
objects is the definition of
\mathscr{P}
. The
bijectivity on morphisms is clear.

Next observe that
F
commutes with the projection functors
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
:

\textstyle{(P\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}}

The limit of
\pi
is
P
by
Corollary
23
, and
the limit of
\pi^{\prime}
is
P^{\mathscr{P}}
by the definition of

P^{\mathscr{P}}
. Since

F
is an isomorphism (in particular, a final functor) and limits are unique
up to isomorphism, there is an
isomorphism
j\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\xrightarrow{\cong}P^{\mathscr{P}}
with
e^{\prime}=F(e^{\prime})^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\cdot j
for all

e^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\twoheadrightarrow A
in
(P\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}})
. Thus in the diagram below the outside and all inner parts
except, perhaps, for the upper inner triangle commute:

\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{F(e^{\prime})}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{VF(e^{\prime})^{+}}
\scriptstyle{V\varphi}
\scriptstyle{V\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{VP\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Vj}
\scriptstyle{Ve^{\prime}}
\textstyle{VP^{\mathscr{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{VF(e^{\prime})^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{A}

It follows that this triangle also commutes, as it
commutes when precomposed with the dense map

\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
and postcomposed with the limit projections

VF(e^{\prime})^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}
.
∎

Theorem 31

(Local Reiterman Theorem)
.

For each
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
, the maps

\mathscr{P}\mapsto\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi\mapsto\mathscr{P}^{\varphi}

give an isomorphism between the lattice of local pseudovarieties of

\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras (ordered by inclusion) and the lattice of
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras.

Proof.

By Lemma

29
and

30
the maps

\mathscr{P}\mapsto\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}
and
\varphi\mapsto\mathscr{P}^{\varphi}
are mutually
inverse, and it only
remains to prove that they are order-preserving. Given two
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras
\varphi\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P
and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P^{\prime}
with
\varphi\leq\varphi^{\prime}
, we have

\mathscr{P}^{\varphi}\subseteq\mathscr{P}^{\varphi^{\prime}}
because every quotient of
P
is
also a quotient of
P^{\prime}
. Given two local pseudovarieties

\mathscr{P}\subseteq\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
, the morphisms
e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}\colon P^{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow A
,
where
e
ranges over all
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathscr{P}
, form a
compatible family over the diagram defining
P^{\mathscr{P}}
. Indeed,
for each morphism
h\colon e\to e^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{P}\subseteq\mathscr{P}^{\prime}

(cf. Construction
28
.
1
), the equation

h\cdot e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}=(e^{\prime})^{*}_{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}
holds for the limit
projections. Hence there exists a unique morphism

q\colon P^{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}\to P^{\mathscr{P}}
with
e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}=e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\cdot q

for all
e\in\mathscr{P}
. It follows that
q\cdot\varphi^{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}=\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}
,
because this holds when postcomposed with the limit projections

e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}
.

\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{{P^{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{P^{\mathscr{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{A}

Indeed, the outside of the above diagram commutes because both sides
yield
e
when we apply
V
and precompose with the dense map

\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
. Therefore we have
\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}\leq\varphi^{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}
as desired.
∎

Theorem

31
can be interpreted in terms of profinite (in-)equations:

Definition 32
.

1.

If
\mathscr{D}
a variety of algebras, a
profinite equation
over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
is a pair of elements
u,v\in{|\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
in some sort
s
, denoted by
u=v
. We say that a

\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebra
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A

satisfies
u=v
if
e^{+}(u)=e^{+}(v)
.

2.

If
\mathscr{D}
a variety of ordered algebras, a
profinite inequation
over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
is a pair of elements
u,v\in{|\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
in some sort
s
, denoted by
u\leq v
. We say that a

\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebra
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A

satisfies
u\leq v
if
e^{+}(u)\leq e^{+}(v)
.

Notation 33
.

For a set
E
of profinite (in-)equations over
\Sigma
, let
\mathscr{P}[E]
denote the
class of all
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras satisfying all (in-)equations in

E
. Conversely, for a class
\mathscr{P}
of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras,
let
E[\mathscr{P}]
be the set of all profinite (in-)equations over
\Sigma
satisfied by all
algebras in
\mathscr{P}
.

Corollary 34

(Equational Local Reiterman Theorem)
.

A class
\mathscr{P}
of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras forms a local pseudovariety iff
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}[E]
for some set
E
of profinite equations (resp. profinite inequations) over
\Sigma
.

Proof.

We consider the case where
\mathscr{D}
is variety of ordered algebras; for the unordered case, replace all inequations by equations throughout the proof.

The “if” direction is a straightforward verification. For the “only
if” direction, suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a local pseudovariety of

\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras, and let

\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P^{\mathscr{P}}
be the corresponding

\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra. Let us first observe that a profinite inequation

u\leq v
lies in
E[\mathscr{P}]
iff
\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(u)\leq\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(v)
. Indeed, for every quotient
e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathscr{P}
we have the commutative triangle

\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{P^{\mathscr{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}}
\textstyle{A}

by the definition of
\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}
. Therefore,
\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(u)\leq\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(v)
implies
e^{+}(u)\leq e^{+}(v)
for every
e\in\mathscr{P}
, i.e. the profinite inequation
u\leq v
lies in
E[\mathscr{P}]
. Conversely, if
u\leq v
is a profinite inequation in
E[\mathscr{P}]
, then

e_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}\cdot\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(u)=e^{+}(u)\leq e^{+}(v)=e_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}\cdot\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(v)
for every
e\in\mathscr{P}
. This implies
\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(u)\leq\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(v)
because the limit projections
e^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}
(
e\in\mathscr{P}
) are jointly order-reflecting.

We claim that

\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}[E[\mathscr{P}]]
. The inclusion
\subseteq
is trivial. To prove
\supseteq
,
let
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
be an element of
\mathscr{P}[E[\mathscr{P}]]
, i.e.
e

satisfies every profinite inequation that every algebra in
\mathscr{P}
satisfies. By the
homomorphism theorem, see Remark
17
.
5
, there
exists a (surjective)
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism
h\colon P^{\mathscr{P}}\twoheadrightarrow A

with
e^{+}=h\cdot\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}
. Indeed, by the argument above every pair
u,v
with

\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(u)\leq\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}(v)
forms a profinite inequation
u\leq v

satisfied by
\mathscr{P}
. Thus
u\leq v
is satisfied by
e
,
i.e.
e^{+}(u)\leq e^{+}(v)
.

Therefore
e=Vh\cdot V\varphi^{\mathscr{P}}\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
lies in
\mathscr{P}^{(\varphi^{\mathscr{P}})}
by the definition of
\mathscr{P}^{(\mathord{-})}
. Since
\mathscr{P}^{(\varphi^{\mathscr{P}})}=\mathscr{P}
by Theorem
31
, we thus have
e\in\mathscr{P}
, as required.
∎

3.3
Pseudovarieties and Reiterman’s theorem

In this subsection we consider finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras that are not quotients of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
for a
fixed
alphabet
\Sigma
, as in the local case, but where the generators
\Sigma
can be chosen from a subclass
\mathds{A}\subseteq\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
. On the language side, this corresponds to considering languages over alphabets in
\mathds{A}
. For example, the classical Eilenberg theorem deals with regular languages
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
over all possible finite alphabets
\Sigma
, and thus we will choose
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}
in this case. However, as Example
3
shows, in a many-sorted setting one may need to make a
choice
of alphabets in
\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
for capture the desired languages; then
\mathds{A}
is chosen to be a proper subclass of
\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
.

Notation 35
.

For the rest of our paper, let
\mathds{A}\subseteq\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
be a fixed class of alphabets.

Definition 36
.

A
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
is called
\mathds{A}
-generated
if there exists
a
surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
with

\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
.

We emphasize that, in contrast to Definition
25
, an
\mathds{A}
-generated
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
is not equipped with a
fixed
surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
. Only the existence of
e
is required.

Example 37
.

Every
finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebra

(A,\alpha)

is
\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
-generated. Indeed, since
A
is a finite (and thus a finitely generated) object of
\mathscr{D}^{S}
, there exists a surjective morphism

e\colon{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
with
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
, and therefore
(A,\alpha)
is a
quotient of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
via
(\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{Te}\mathbf{T}A\xrightarrow{\alpha}(A,\alpha))
. Note that
Te
is surjective by our assumption that
T
preserves surjections, and
\alpha
is surjective by the unit law for the
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
.

Example 38

(
\omega
-semigroups)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{2}
. As suggested by Example
3
, we choose

\mathds{A}=\{\,(\Sigma,\emptyset)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}\,\}
.
A finite

\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
-algebra (= finite
\omega
-semigroup)
A
is

\mathds{A}
-generated
iff it is
complete
, i.e. every element
a\in A_{\omega}
can be expressed as an infinite product

a=\pi(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots)
for some
a_{i}\in A_{+}
. Indeed, for
the “only if” direction, suppose that
A
is
\mathds{A}
-generated, i.e. there exists a surjective
\omega
-semigroup morphism
e\colon(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})\twoheadrightarrow(A_{+},A_{\omega})
for some
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
. For any
a\in A_{\omega}
, choose

s_{0}s_{1}\ldots\in\Sigma^{\omega}
with
a=e(s_{0}s_{1}\ldots)
. Then

a=e(s_{0}s_{1}\ldots)=e(\pi(s_{0},s_{1},\ldots))=\pi(e(s_{0}),e(s_{1}),\ldots),

which shows that
A
is complete. For the “if”
direction, suppose that
A
is complete. Let
\Sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=A_{+}\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, and
extend the map

(\mathit{id},\emptyset)\colon(\Sigma,\emptyset)\to(A_{+},A_{\omega})

to an

\omega
-semigroup morphism
e\colon(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})\to(A_{+},A_{\omega})
, using that
(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
is the free

\omega
-semigroup on
(\Sigma,\emptyset)
. Clearly the component
e\colon\Sigma^{+}\to A_{+}
is surjective
because
e(a)=a
for all
a\in A_{+}
. To show that also the component
e\colon\Sigma^{\omega}\to A_{\omega}
is surjective, let
a\in A_{\omega}
and choose elements
a_{i}\in A_{+}
with
a=\pi(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots)
, using the completeness of
A
.
It follows that

a=\pi(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots)=\pi(e(a_{0}),e(a_{1}),\ldots)=e(\pi(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots)).

Thus
e
is surjective, which proves that
A
is
\mathds{A}
-generated.

Definition 39
.

A
pseudovariety of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras
is a full subcategory
\mathscr{V}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
such that all algebras in
\mathscr{V}
are
\mathds{A}
-generated, and
\mathscr{V}
is closed under quotients and
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebras of finite
products. That is,

(i)

for every
A\in\mathscr{V}
and every quotient
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
, one has
B\in\mathscr{V}
;

(ii)

for every finite family
A_{i}

(i\in I)
of algebras in
\mathscr{V}
and every
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebra
A\rightarrowtail\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}
, one has
A\in\mathscr{V}
.

Remark 40
.

In most applications,
\mathds{A}
is chosen such that finite products of
\mathds{A}
-generated finite

\mathbf{T}
-algebras are
\mathds{A}
-generated. In this case the definition of a
pseudovariety
simplifies: it is a class
\mathscr{V}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
of
\mathds{A}
-generated algebras closed under
quotients,

\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebras, and finite products. This holds, e.g., in the following two examples:

Example 41
.

Let
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,\mathbf{T}
. Then every finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebra is
\mathds{A}
-generated by Example
37
. Thus, instantiating Definition
39
, a pseudovariety is
a
class of finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras closed under quotients, subalgebras, and finite
products. This concept was studied in
[
13
]
. For
the
monad
\mathbf{T}_{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
we recover the original concept of Eilenberg: a
class
of finite monoids closed under quotients, submonoids, and finite products.

Example 42

(
\omega
-semigroups)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{2}
, and choose
\mathds{A}=\{\,(\Sigma,\emptyset)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}\,\}
and
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\omega
-semigroups. Then
\mathds{A}
-generated
\omega
-semigroups are precisely complete
\omega
-semigroups by Example
38
, and clearly products of
complete
\omega
-semigroups are complete. Thus, instantiating Definition
39
, a

pseudovariety of
\omega
-semigroups
is a class of
finite
complete
\omega
-semigroups closed under quotients, complete

\omega
-subsemigroups, and
finite products. This concept
is due to Wilke
[
46
]
; see also
[
27
]
.

Definition 43
.

A
pro-
\mathcal{F}
theory
is a family

\varrho=(\,\varrho_{\Sigma}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P_{\Sigma}\,)_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}

such that (i)
\varrho_{\Sigma}
is a
\Sigma
-generated profinite

{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra for every
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
and (ii) for
every

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
with

\Sigma,\Delta\in\mathds{A}
there exists a

{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism
g_{P}\colon P_{\Delta}\to P_{\Sigma}
with
\varrho_{\Sigma}\cdot\hat{g}=g_{P}\cdot\varrho_{\Delta}
.

\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{g}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{\Delta}}
\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{\Sigma}}
\textstyle{P_{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{P}}
\textstyle{P_{\Sigma}}

Here
\hat{g}
is the continuous extension of
g
, see Lemma
18
. Pro-
\mathcal{F}
theories are ordered by
\varrho\leq\varrho^{\prime}
iff

\varrho_{\Sigma}\leq\varrho_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
for each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
.

Using the local Reiterman theorem, pro-
\mathcal{F}
theories can be equivalently described in terms of local pseudovarieties:

Definition 44
.

An
\mathcal{F}
-theory
is a family

\mathcal{T}=(\,\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}\,)_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}

such that (i)
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
is a local pseudovariety of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras for every
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, and (ii) for every
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
with
\Sigma,\Delta\in\mathds{A}
and every
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
, there exists an
\overline{e}\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
and a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
\overline{g}\colon B\to A
with
\overline{g}\cdot\overline{e}=e\cdot g
.

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g}}
\textstyle{A}

\mathcal{F}
-theories are ordered by
\mathcal{T}\leq\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
iff
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
for all
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
.

Remark 45
.

Since the local pseudovariety
\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
is downwards closed, one can always choose
\overline{e}
and
\overline{g}
in the above definition as the factorization of
e\cdot g
into a surjective and an injective/order-reflecting
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism.

Construction 46
.

1.

For any pro-
\mathcal{F}
theory
\varrho
, form the family

\mathcal{T}^{\varrho}=(\,\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\varrho}\,)_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}

where
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\varrho}
is the local pseudovariety of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras associated to
\varrho_{\Sigma}
(see Construction
28
.2).

2.

For any
\mathcal{F}
-theory
\mathcal{T}
, form the family

\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}=(\,\varrho_{\Sigma}^{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P_{\Sigma}^{\mathcal{T}}\,)

where
\varrho_{\Sigma}^{\mathcal{T}}
is the
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra associated to
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
(see Construction
28
.1). The limit projection associated to
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
is denoted by

e_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\colon P_{\Sigma}^{\mathcal{T}}\to A.

Lemma 47
.

1.

For any pro-
\mathcal{F}
theory
\varrho
, the family
\mathcal{T}^{\varrho}
forms an
\mathcal{F}
-theory.

2.

For any
\mathcal{F}
-theory
\mathcal{T}
, the family
\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}
forms a pro-
\mathcal{F}
theory.

3.

The maps
\varrho\mapsto\mathcal{T}^{\varrho}
and
\mathcal{T}\mapsto\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}
give an isomorphism between the poset of pro-
\mathcal{F}
theories and the poset of
\mathcal{F}
-theories.

Proof.

1.

Suppose that
\varrho
is a pro-
\mathcal{F}
theory, and let
g\colon\mathbf{T}\Delta\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
be a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism with
\Sigma,\Delta\in\mathds{A}
and
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\varrho}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\varrho}
, the quotient
e
has the form

e=(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{V\varrho_{\Sigma}}VP_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{Vq}A)

for some
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism
q
. Factorize the
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism
q\cdot g_{P}
into a surjective homomorphism
\overline{q}
followed by an injective/order-reflecting homomorphism
\overline{g}
. Then

\overline{e}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(T{\mathbb{\Delta}}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbb{\Delta}}}V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\xrightarrow{V\varrho_{\Delta}}VP_{\Delta}\xrightarrow{V\overline{q}}A)

lies in
\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}^{\varrho}
and
e\cdot g=\overline{g}\cdot\overline{e}
, as shown by the commutative diagram below:

\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}

\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbb{\Delta}}}
\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\hat{g}}
\scriptstyle{V\varrho_{\Delta}}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\varrho_{\Sigma}}

\textstyle{A}

This proves that
\mathcal{T}^{\varrho}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-theory.

2.

Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-theory, and let
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
be a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism with
\Sigma,\Delta\in\mathds{A}
. We need to define
g_{P}\colon P^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Delta}\to P^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}
with
\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}\cdot\hat{g}=g_{P}\cdot\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Delta}
. For any

in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
, choose
\overline{e}\colon\mathbf{T}\twoheadrightarrow A_{e}

\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
and

with

, using that
\mathcal{T}

\mathcal{F}

(where

. Therefore there exists a unique

. It follows that

, since this holds when precomposed with the dense map

and postcomposed with the limit projections

.

3.

By the local Reiterman theorem (Theorem
31
), the two maps
\varrho\mapsto\mathcal{T}^{\varrho}
and
\mathcal{T}\mapsto\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}
give mutually inverse and order-preserving bijections between the two posets.∎

Remark 48
.

\mathcal{F}
-theories generalize the
varieties of filters of congruences
introduced by Almeida
[
6
]
for algebras over a finitary signature, and earlier by Thérien
[
43
]
for monoids.

Our goal in this subsection is to show that
\mathcal{F}
-theories (and thus also pro-
\mathcal{F}
theories) correspond to pseudovarietes of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras. First an auxiliary result:

Lemma 49
.

For every
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
, the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is projective. That is, for any two
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to B
and
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
with
e
surjective, there exists a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
with
h=e\cdot g
.

Proof.

Consider the morphism
h^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=h\cdot\eta\colon{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to B
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
. Since the free
\mathscr{D}^{S}
-object
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is projective, one can choose a morphism
g^{\prime}\colon{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to A
with
h^{\prime}=e\cdot g^{\prime}
. Let
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
be the unique extension of
g^{\prime}
to a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism (i.e. with
g^{\prime}=g\cdot\eta
), using that
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra on
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
. Then
h=g\cdot e
, as this holds when precomposed with the unit
\eta
.

\textstyle{{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B}

∎

Lemma 50
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an
\mathcal{F}
-theory, and let
(A,\alpha)
be an

\mathds{A}
-generated algebra in
\mathcal{F}
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

There exists a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
with
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
and
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
.

(ii)

Every
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to(A,\alpha)
with
\Delta\in\mathds{A}
factors through some
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) Given a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to A
with
\Delta\in\mathds{A}
, choose a

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
with
h=e\cdot g
, see Lemma
49
. Since
\mathcal{T}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-theory, we have
e\cdot g=\overline{g}\cdot\overline{e}
for some
\overline{e}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
and some
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
\overline{g}
. Therefore
h
factors through
\overline{e}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
, which proves (ii).

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g}}
\textstyle{A}

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) Since
A
is
\mathds{A}
-generated, there exists a surjective

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
for some
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
.
Thus, by (ii),
e
factors through some quotient
q\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
, i.e.
e\leq q
. Since the local pseudovariety
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
is downwards closed, it follows that
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
. This proves (i).
∎

Notation 51
.

For any
\mathcal{F}
-theory
\mathcal{T}
, we denote by
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
the class of all
\mathds{A}
-generated algebras
(A,\alpha)\in\mathcal{F}
satisfying the equivalent properties (i)/(ii) of Lemma
50
.

Lemma 52
.

If
\mathcal{T}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-theory, then
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
is a pseudovariety of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras.

Proof.

The closure of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
under quotients follows from
Lemma
50
(i) and the fact that
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
is downwards closed. To show that
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
is closed under
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebras of finite products, let
A_{i}
(
i\in I
) be a finite family of objects in
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
and let
m\colon A\rightarrowtail\prod_{i}A_{i}
be an
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebra of their product. Denote the product projections by

\pi_{i}\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to A_{i}
. We show that every

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism

h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to A
with
\Delta\in\mathds{A}

factors through some element of
\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
. For each
i
, the
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
\pi_{i}\cdot m\cdot h
has codomain
A_{i}\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
and thus, by definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
, factors through some element of
\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
; that is,
\pi_{i}\cdot m\cdot h=h_{i}\cdot e_{i}
for some
e_{i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
and some
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h_{i}
. Since
\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
is a local pseudovariety, there exists
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
with
e_{i}\leq e
for all
i
, i.e.
e_{i}=g_{i}\cdot e
for some
g_{i}
.
It follows that
h
factors through
e
by the diagonal fill-in
property:

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle h_{i}\cdot g_{i}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{h_{i}\cdot g_{i}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{\prod_{i}A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{A_{i}}

By the definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
, this shows
A\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
.
∎

Notation 53
.

For any pseudovariety
\mathscr{V}
of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras and
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
we form the family

\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}=(\,\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}\,)_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}

where, for each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
,

\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}=\{\,e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A\,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\,A\in\mathscr{V}\,\}.

Lemma 54
.

For any pseudovariety
\mathscr{V}
of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras,
\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-theory.

Proof.

Clearly
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
is a local pseudovariety of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras for every
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
: it is downwards closed because
\mathscr{V}
closed under quotients, and it is closed under subdirect products because
\mathscr{V}
is closed under
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebras of finite products.

To show that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
forms an
\mathcal{F}
-theory, let
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
be a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism with
\Sigma,\Delta\in\mathds{A}
and
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
, Factorize
e\cdot g
into a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
\overline{e}\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\twoheadrightarrow B
followed by an injective/order-reflecting
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
\overline{g}\colon B\rightarrowtail A
:

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g}}
\textstyle{A}

Then
B
is an
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebra of
A
. Since
A\in\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{V}
is closed under
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebras, it follows that
B\in\mathscr{V}
and therefore
\overline{e}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}^{\mathscr{V}}
. Thus
\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-theory.
∎

Lemma 55
.

For any pseudovariety
\mathscr{V}
of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras we have
\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
where
\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
.

Proof.

To show
\mathscr{V}\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
, let
A\in\mathscr{V}
. Since
A
is
\mathds{A}
-generated, there exists a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
with
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
. Then
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
by the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
and therefore
A\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
by the definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
.

For the reverse inclusion
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}\subseteq\mathscr{V}
, let
A\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
. Then, for some
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, there exists
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
with codomain
A
. But by the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
, this means that
A\in\mathscr{V}
.
∎

Lemma 56
.

For any
\mathcal{F}
-theory
\mathcal{T}

we
have
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
, where
\mathscr{V}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
.

Proof.

To show
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
, let
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
. Then
A\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}=\mathscr{V}
by the definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
. But then
e\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
be the definition of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
.

For the reserve inclusion
\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, let
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
, this means that
A\in\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
, i.e. there exists
\Delta\in\mathds{A}
and
q\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
with codomain
A
. Using projectivity of free
\mathbf{T}
-algebras (see Lemma
49
), choose a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}
with
q\cdot g=e
. Since
\mathcal{T}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-theory, there exists
\overline{q}\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
and
\overline{g}\colon B\to A
with
q\cdot g=\overline{g}\cdot\overline{q}
.

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\overline{q}}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g}}
\textstyle{A}

Therefore
e=\overline{g}\cdot\overline{q}
, i.e.
e\leq\overline{q}
. Since
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
is downwards closed and
\overline{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
, it follows that
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
.
∎

Lemma 57
.

The maps

\mathcal{T}\mapsto\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{V}\mapsto\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}

give an isomorphism between the lattice of
\mathcal{F}
-theories and the lattice of pseudovarieties of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras (ordered by inclusion).

Proof.

By Lemma
55
and
56
, the two maps are mutually inverse. Moreover, they are clearly order-preserving, which proves the claim.
∎

From this and Lemma
47
we get the main result of the present section:

Theorem 58

(Reiterman Theorem)
.

The lattice of
pseudovarieties of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras is isomorphic to the
lattice of pro-
\mathcal{F}

theories.

As in the local case, we can interpret the above theorem in terms of profinite (in-)equations.

Definition 59
.

Let
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
and
u,v\in{|\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
for some sort
s
.

1.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a variety of algebras. A
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
in
\mathcal{F}

satisfies the profinite equation
u=v
if
h^{+}(u)=h^{+}(v)
for every
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to A
.

2.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a variety of ordered algebras. A
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
in
\mathcal{F}

satisfies the profinite inequation
u\leq v
if
h^{+}(u)\leq h^{+}(v)
for every
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to A
.

Notation 60
.

For a class
E
of profinite (in-)equations over sets
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, let
\mathscr{V}[E]
denote the
class of all
\mathds{A}
-generated algebras in
\mathcal{F}
satisfying all (in-)equations in

E
. Conversely, for a subclass
\mathscr{V}\subseteq\mathcal{F}

let
E[\mathscr{V}]
be the class of all profinite (in-)equations over
\mathds{A}
satisfied by all
algebras in
\mathscr{V}
.

Corollary 61

(Equational Reiterman Theorem)
.

A class
\mathscr{V}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
of
\mathds{A}
-generated algebras forms a pseudovariety iff
\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{V}[E]
for some class
E
of profinite (in-)equations over
\mathds{A}
.

Proof.

We consider the case where
\mathscr{D}
is variety of ordered algebras; for the unordered case, replace all inequations by equations.

For the “if” direction, we may assume that
E
consists of a single profinite inequation
u\leq v
over
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
(since the class of all pseudovarieties forms a complete lattice under intersection). Then

1.

\mathscr{V}[u\leq v]
is closed under quotients:
Let
A\in\mathscr{V}[u\leq v]
and
q\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
be a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism. Given a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to B
, choose a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to A
with
q\cdot g=h
, using projectivity of the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
. Then
g^{+}(u)\leq g^{+}(v)
because
A
satisfies
u\leq v
, and thus
h^{+}(u)=q\cdot g^{+}(u)\leq q\cdot g^{+}(v)=h^{+}(v)
. It follows that
B
satisfies
u\leq v
and thus
B\in\mathscr{V}[u\leq v]

2.

\mathscr{V}[u\leq v]
is closed under
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebras of finite products.
Let
A_{i}
(
i\in I
) be a finite family of algebras in
\mathscr{V}[u\leq v]
, and suppose that
m\colon B\rightarrowtail\prod A_{i}
is an
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebra of the product
\prod A_{i}
. For any
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to B
and
i\in I
, we have the composite
h_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{i}\cdot m\cdot h
where
\pi_{i}\colon\prod A_{i}\to A_{i}
is the product projection. Note that
h_{i}^{+}=\pi_{i}\cdot m\cdot h^{+}
because this holds when precomposed with the dense map
\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
. Since
A_{i}
satisfies
u\leq v
, we have
h_{i}^{+}(u)\leq h_{i}^{+}(v)
and thus
\pi_{i}\cdot m\cdot h^{+}(u)\leq\pi_{i}\cdot m\cdot h^{+}(u)
for all
i
. Since the projections
\pi_{i}
are jointly order-reflecting and
m
is order-reflecting, it follows that
h^{+}(u)\leq h^{+}(v)
. Therefore
B
satisfies
u\leq v
, i.e.
B\in\mathscr{V}[u\leq v]

For the “only if” direction,
let
\mathscr{V}
be a pseudovariety of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras, let
\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
be the
\mathcal{F}
-theory corresponding to
\mathscr{V}
, and let
\varrho_{\mathcal{T}}=(\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P_{\Sigma}^{\mathcal{T}})_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}
be the pro-
\mathcal{F}
theory corresponding to
\mathcal{T}
. Let us first observe that every profinite inequation
u\leq v
over
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
with
\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}(u)\leq\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}(v)
is satisfied by every algebra
A\in\mathscr{V}
. Indeed, let
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to A
be a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism, and factorize
h=m\cdot e
with
e
surjective and
m
order-reflecting. Then
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
because
\mathscr{V}
is closed under
\mathds{A}
-generated subalgebras. Thus
\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}(u)\leq\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}(v)
implies
e^{+}(u)=e^{*}_{\mathcal{T}}\cdot\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}(u)\leq e^{*}_{\mathcal{T}}\cdot\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}(v)=e^{+}(v)
and thus
h^{+}(u)=m\cdot e^{+}(u)\leq m\cdot e^{+}(v)=h^{+}(v)
, showing that
A
satisfies the profinite inequation
u\leq v
.

We claim that

\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{V}[E[\mathscr{V}]]
. The inclusion
\subseteq
is trivial. To prove
\supseteq
, let

A\in\mathscr{V}[E[\mathscr{V}]]
, i.e.
A
satisfies every profinite inequation over
\mathds{A}
that the algebras in
\mathscr{V}
satisfy. Since
A
is
\mathds{A}
-generated, there is a surjective

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
with
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
. By the above argument, any profinite inequation
u\leq v
over
\Sigma
with
\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}(u)\leq\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}(v)
is satisfied by every algebra in
\mathscr{V}
and thus also by
A
. Hence
e^{+}(u)\leq e^{+}(v)
. This implies by the homomorphism theorem that
e^{+}
factors through
\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}
. In particular,
A
is a quotient of
P_{\Sigma}^{\mathcal{T}}
. This implies that
A
lies in
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
by the definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
. Since
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}=\mathscr{V}
by Theorem
58
, we conclude that
A\in\mathscr{V}
.
∎

The special case
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
of the previous corollary has been proved in
[
13
]
.

4
Unary Presentations

In this section we develop the notion of a
unary presentation
of a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra that later, in Section
6
, will serve as the key tool for defining the derivatives of a language. For motivation, consider a monoid
M
and recall that an equivalence relation
\equiv
on
M
is a
monoid congruence
if it is stable under multiplication, i.e.
x\equiv x^{\prime}
and
y\equiv y^{\prime}
implies
xy\equiv x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
. To show that
\equiv
is a monoid congruence, it actually suffices to show that
\equiv
is stable under left and right multiplication, i.e.
x\equiv x^{\prime}
implies
xy\equiv x^{\prime}y
and
yx\equiv yx^{\prime}
for all
y\in M
. This observation generalizes to algebras
A
over a finitary
S
-sorted signature
\Gamma
: an
S
-sorted equivalence
\equiv
on
A
is a
\Gamma
-congruence iff it is stable under all
elementary translations
, see
C
. Analogously for ordered algebras
A
and
S
-sorted preorders
\preceq
on
A
.

The concept of a unary presentation states this phenomenon for algebras for the monad
\mathbf{T}
on
\mathscr{D}^{S}
(in lieu of just
\Gamma
-algebras) in categorical terms. First, by identifying congruences/stable preorders with their corresponding quotients (see
C
and
C
), we get the following concept:

Definition 62
.

Let
(A,\alpha)
be a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra. A quotient
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
is called a
\mathbf{T}
-quotient
of
(A,\alpha)
if it carries a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism, i.e. if there exists a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra structure
(B,\beta)
on
B
such that
e\colon(A,\alpha)\twoheadrightarrow(B,\beta)
is a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism.

\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{Te}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{B}

If moreover
(B,\beta)
lies in
\mathcal{F}
, we say that
e
is an
\mathcal{F}
-quotient
of
(A,\alpha)
.

Remark 63
.

Clearly the
\mathbf{T}
-algebra structure
\beta
on
B
is uniquely determined because
Te
is an epimorphism. Moreover, to show that
e
is a
\mathbf{T}
-quotient it suffices to find a morphism
\beta
making the above square commute; then
(B,\beta)
is always a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra, see
A
.

Remark 64
.

In view of our applications, it is helpful to rephrase the categorical definitions of this section in terms of congruences/stable preorders instead of quotients. Definition
62
has the following equivalent formulation:

1.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of algebras and view
\mathscr{D}^{S}
as a variety of
S
-sorted algebras, see
C
. Let
A\in\mathscr{D}^{S}
. Given a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
, we call a congruence
\equiv
on
A
a
\mathbf{T}
-congruence
if there exists a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon(A,\alpha)\twoheadrightarrow(B,\beta)
with kernel
\equiv
. If moreover
(B,\beta)\in\mathcal{F}
, we call
\equiv
an
\mathcal{F}
-congruence
. In the case
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras, this means precisely that
\equiv
is a
\mathbf{T}
-congruence of finite index, i.e. with only finitely many congruence classes. By
C
, we have the bijective correspondences

congruences on
A
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{quotients of $A$ in $\mathscr{D}^{S}$}

\mathbf{T}
-congruences on
(A,\alpha)

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{$\mathbf{T}$-quotients of $(A,\alpha)$}

\mathcal{F}
-congruences on
(A,\alpha)

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{$\mathcal{F}$-quotients of $(A,\alpha)$}

2.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of ordered algebras, and view
\mathscr{D}^{S}
as a variety of
S
-sorted ordered algebras. Let
A\in\mathscr{D}^{S}
. Given a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
, we call a stable preorder
\preceq
on
A
an
\mathbf{T}
-stable preorder
if there exists a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon(A,\alpha)\twoheadrightarrow(B,\beta)
with ordered kernel
\preceq
. If moreover
(B,\beta)\in\mathcal{F}
, we call
\preceq
an
\mathcal{F}
-stable preorder
. In the case
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras, this means precisely that
\preceq
is
\mathbf{T}
-stable preorder of finite index, i.e. the induced congruence
\equiv\,=\,\preceq\cap\succeq
has finite index. By
C
, we have the bijective correspondences

stable preorders on
A
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{quotients of $A$ in $\mathscr{D}^{S}$}

\mathbf{T}
-stable preorders on
(A,\alpha)

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{$\mathbf{T}$-quotients of $(A,\alpha)$}

\mathcal{F}
-stable preorders on
(A,\alpha)

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{$\mathcal{F}$-quotients of $(A,\alpha)$}

The characterization of congruences/stable preorders of
\Gamma
-algebras in terms of translations has the following categorical formulation:

Definition 65
.

Let
A
be an object of
\mathscr{D}^{S}
.

1.

By a
unary operation
on
A
is meant a morphism
u\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
in
\mathscr{D}
, where
s
and
t
are arbitrary sorts.

2.

Given a set
\mathds{U}
of unary operations on
A
, a quotient
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
is called a
\mathds{U}
-quotient
of
A
if every unary operation
u\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
in

\mathds{U}
admits a
lifting along
e
, i.e. a morphism
u_{B}\colon B_{s}\to B_{t}
making the following square commutative:

\textstyle{A_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{A_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{B}}
\textstyle{B_{t}}

Remark 66
.

Again, let us state this concept in terms of congruences/stable preorders.

1.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a variety of algebras. Given
A\in\mathscr{D}^{S}
and a set
\mathds{U}
of unary operations on
A
, we call a congruence
\equiv
on
A
a
\mathds{U}
-congruence
if it stable under all operations in
\mathds{U}
, that is,
a\equiv_{s}a^{\prime}
implies
u(a)\equiv_{t}u(a^{\prime})
for all
u\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
and
a,a^{\prime}\in A_{s}
. By the homomorphism theorem (see
C
), we get the bijective correspondence

\mathds{U}
-congruences on
A

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{$\mathds{U}$-quotients of $A$}.

2.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a variety of ordered algebras. Given
A\in\mathscr{D}^{S}
and a set
\mathds{U}
of unary operations on
A
, we call a stable preorder
\preceq
on
A
a
\mathds{U}
-stable preorder
if it stable under all operations in
\mathds{U}
, that is,
a\preceq a^{\prime}
implies
u(a)\preceq_{t}u(a^{\prime})
for all
u\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
and
a,a^{\prime}\in A_{s}
. By the homomorphism theorem for ordered algebras (see
C
), we get the bijective correspondence

\mathds{U}
-stable preorders on
A

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{$\mathds{U}$-quotients of $A$}.

For
\Gamma
-algebras over an infinitary signature
\Gamma
, the characterization of congruences by elementary translations generally fails, but remains valid for equivalence relations
\equiv
that are refinable to a
\Gamma
-congruence of finite index, see Examples
76
and
78
. The concept of refinement also has a categorical formulation:

Definition 67
.

Given a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
, a quotient
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
is called
\mathcal{F}
-refinable
if it factorizes through some
\mathcal{F}
-quotient of
(A,\alpha)
.

Remark 68
.

Again, in terms of congruences/stable preorders we get the following concepts:

1.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of algebras. Given a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
, a congruence on
A
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
is called
\mathcal{F}
-refinable
if it contains an
\mathcal{F}
-congruence. By the homomorphism theorem for
\mathbf{T}
-algebras (see Remark
6
), we have the bijective correspondence

\mathcal{F}
-refinable congruences on
A

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{$\mathcal{F}$-refinable quotients of $A$}.

2.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of ordered algebras. Given a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
, a stable preorder on
A
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
is called
\mathcal{F}
-refinable
if it contains an
\mathcal{F}
-stable preorder. By the homomorphism theorem for
\mathbf{T}
-algebras (see Remark
6
), we have the bijective correspondence

\mathcal{F}
-refinable stable preorders on
A

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{$\mathcal{F}$-refinable quotients of $A$}.

This leads to following central definition of our paper, the notion of a
unary presentation
of a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra.

Definition 69
.

By a
unary presentation
of a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
is meant a set

\mathds{U}
of unary operations on
A
such that for any
\mathcal{F}
-refinable quotient
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
,

\text{$e$ is a $\mathbf{T}$-quotient}\quad\text{iff}\quad\text{$e$ is a $\mathds{U}$-quotient}.

Remark 70
.

Every
\mathcal{F}
-refinable
\mathbf{T}
-quotient is an
\mathcal{F}
-quotient because
\mathcal{F}
is closed under quotients. Therefore the last line of the previous definition could be replaced by

\text{$e$ is an $\mathcal{F}$-quotient of $(A,\alpha)$}\quad\text{iff}\quad\text{$e$ is a $\mathds{U}$-quotient of $(A,\alpha)$}.

Remark 71
.

Here is the equivalent version of Definition
69
for congruences and stable preorders:

1.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of algebras. Then a set
\mathds{U}
of unary operations on
A
forms a unary presentation of
(A,\alpha)
iff for any
\mathcal{F}
-refinable congruence
\equiv
on
A
,

\text{$\equiv$ is a $\mathbf{T}$-congruence on $(A,\alpha)$}\quad\text{iff}\quad\text{$\equiv$ is a $\mathds{U}$-congruence on $(A,\alpha)$}.

2.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of ordered algebras. Then a set
\mathds{U}
of unary operations on
A
forms a unary presentation of
(A,\alpha)
iff for any
\mathcal{F}
-refinable stable preorder
\preceq
on
A
,

\text{$\preceq$ is a $\mathbf{T}$-stable order on $(A,\alpha)$}\quad\text{iff}\quad\text{$\preceq$ is a $\mathds{U}$-stable preorder on $(A,\alpha)$}.

Remark 72
.

In practice, if the monad
\mathbf{T}
represents algebras with finitary operations, the restriction to
\mathcal{F}
-refinable
quotients can often be dropped, i.e. the equivalence of the two properties in Definition
69
holds for arbitrary quotients
e
. However, the restriction is usually necessary in the presence of infinitary operations. See the examples in the following section.

4.1
Examples of unary presentations

To illustrate the above definitions, we derive unary presentations for monoids, finitary
\Gamma
-algebras,
\omega
-semigroups and, more generally, algebras for a monad on
\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
.

Example 73

(Monoids)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\ast}
on
\mathbf{Set}
and
\mathcal{F}=
all finite monoids. Every monoid
M
has a unary presentation given by the unary operations
x\mapsto yx
and
x\mapsto xy
on
M
, where
y
ranges over all elements of
M
. Indeed, an equivalence relation
\equiv
on
M
forms a monoid congruence iff it is stable under left and right multiplication, i.e.
x\equiv x^{\prime}
implies
yx\equiv yx^{\prime}
and
xy\equiv x^{\prime}y
for all
y\in M
. This holds even without the condition that
\equiv
is
\mathcal{F}
-refinable, i.e. refinable to a monoid congruence of finite index.

Example 74

(
\Gamma
-algebras)
.

Generalizing the previous example, let
\Gamma
be a finitary
S
-sorted signature, and let
\mathbf{T}
be the free-algebra monad on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
associated to some variety
\mathscr{A}
of
\Gamma
-algebras; that is,
\mathscr{A}\cong\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
. Put
\mathcal{F}=
all finite algebras in
\mathscr{A}
. Every algebra
A\in\mathscr{A}
has a unary presentation given by the set of elementary translations on
A
. Indeed, an
S
-sorted equivalence relation
\equiv
on
A
is a
\Gamma
-congruence (=
\mathbf{T}
-congruence) iff it is stable under all elementary translations, see
C
. Again the condition that
\equiv
is
\mathcal{F}
-refinable, i.e. refinable to a
\Gamma
-congruence of finite index, is not needed.

Likewise, if
\mathbf{T}
is the free-algebra monad on
\mathbf{Pos}^{S}
associated to an
S
-sorted variety
\mathscr{A}
of ordered algebras, the elementary translations form a unary presentation.

Example 75

(
\omega
-semigroups)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{2}
and
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\omega
-semigroups. Every
\omega
-semigroup
A=(A_{+},A_{\omega})
has a unary presentation
\mathds{U}
given by the operations

(1)

x\mapsto yx
and
x\mapsto xy
on
A_{+}
,

(2)

x\mapsto xz
and
x\mapsto x^{\omega}=\pi(x,x,x,\ldots)
from
A_{+}
to
A_{\omega}
, and

(3)

z\mapsto yz
on
A_{\omega}
,

where
y\in A_{+}\cup\{1\}
and
z\in A_{\omega}
. Here we view
1
as a neutral element of the semigroup
A_{+}
, i.e. we put
1x=x1=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x
and
1z=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}z
.

To show that
\mathds{U}
is a unary presentation, suppose that
\equiv
is an
\mathcal{F}
-refinable two-sorted equivalence relation on
A
, i.e.
\equiv
contains an
\omega
-semigroup congruence
\mathord{\sim}\subseteq\mathord{\equiv}
of finite index. We need to prove that
\equiv
is an
\omega
-semigroup congruence (i.e. stable under finite and infinite multiplication) iff
\equiv
is stable under the operations in
\mathds{U}
.

(
\Rightarrow
) If
\equiv
is an
\omega
-semigroup congruence, then
\equiv
is stable under finite and infinite multiplication and thus in particular under the operations in
\mathds{U}
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Suppose that
\equiv
is stable under the operations in
\mathds{U}
. Stability under the operation (1), the first one in (2) and the one in (3) implies that
\equiv
is stable under (mixed) binary products. It remains to show that
\equiv
is stable under infinite products: given
v_{i},w_{i}\in A_{+}
with
v_{i}\equiv w_{i}
(
i=0,1,2,\ldots
) we need to show that
\pi(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}\ldots)\equiv\pi(w_{0},w_{1},w_{2}\ldots)
. This rests on the following combinatorial lemma, an instance of Ramsey’s theorem:

Lemma

(see
[
27
]
, p.77)
.

Let
X
be a set,
h\colon X^{+}\to E
a function into a finite set
E
and
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
a sequence in
X
. Then there exists a strictly increasing sequence
k_{0}<k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots
of natural numbers such that

h(x_{k_{i}}\ldots x_{k_{i+1}-1})=h(x_{k_{0}}\ldots x_{k_{1}-1})\quad\text{for all $i\geq 0$}.

Apply the lemma to the set
X=A_{+}\times A_{+}
, the semigroup morphism

h\colon(A_{+}\times A_{+})^{+}\to(A_{+}/\mathord{\sim})\times(A_{+}/\mathord{\sim})

mapping
(v,w)\in A_{+}\times A_{+}
to
([v]_{\sim},[w]_{\sim})
,
and the sequence
(v_{0},w_{0}),(v_{1},w_{1}),(v_{2},w_{2}),\ldots
in
A_{+}\times A_{+}
. This gives a strictly increasing sequence
k_{0}<k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots
of natural numbers such that

v_{k_{i}}\ldots v_{k_{i+1}-1}\sim v_{k_{0}}\ldots v_{k_{1}-1}\quad\text{and}\quad w_{k_{i}}\ldots w_{k_{i+1}-1}\sim w_{k_{0}}\ldots w_{k_{1}-1}\quad

for all
i\geq 0
. It follows that

\displaystyle\pi(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\ldots)

\displaystyle=v_{0}\ldots v_{k_{0}-1}\cdot\pi(v_{k_{0}}\ldots v_{k_{1}-1},v_{k_{1}}\ldots v_{k_{2}-1},v_{k_{2}}\ldots v_{k_{3}-1},\ldots)

\displaystyle\sim v_{0}\ldots v_{k_{0}-1}\cdot\pi(v_{k_{0}}\ldots v_{k_{1}-1},v_{k_{0}}\ldots v_{k_{0}-1},v_{k_{0}}\ldots v_{k_{0}-1},\ldots)

\displaystyle=v_{0}\ldots v_{k_{0}-1}\cdot(v_{k_{0}}\ldots v_{k_{1}-1})^{\omega}

where the first step uses the associative laws, the second one that
\sim
is an
\omega
-semigroup congruence, and the third one is the definition of
(\mathord{-})^{\omega}
. In particular, since
\sim\,\subseteq\,\equiv
, we get

\pi(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\ldots)\equiv v_{0}\ldots v_{k_{0}-1}(v_{k_{0}}\ldots v_{k_{1}-1})^{\omega}

and analogously

\pi(w_{0},w_{1},w_{2},\ldots)\equiv w_{0}\ldots w_{k_{0}-1}(w_{k_{0}}\ldots w_{k_{1}-1})^{\omega}.

But since
\equiv
is stable under left and right multiplication and
\omega
-powers by assumption,
v_{i}\equiv w_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
implies

v_{0}\ldots v_{k_{0}-1}(v_{k_{0}}\ldots v_{k_{1}-1})^{\omega}\equiv w_{0}\ldots w_{k_{0}-1}(w_{k_{0}}\ldots w_{k_{1}-1})^{\omega}.

Thus
\pi(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\ldots)\equiv\pi(w_{0},w_{1},w_{2},\ldots)
, as required.

Example 76

(Monads on
\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an arbitrary monad on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
and
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras. Then every
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
has a generic unary presentation given as follows. Let
1_{s}\in\mathbf{Set}^{S}
be
the
S
-sorted set with one element in sort
s
and otherwise empty; thus a
morphism
1_{s}\to A
in
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
chooses an element of

A_{s}
. A

polynomial
over
A
is a morphism
p\colon 1_{t}\to T(A+1_{s})
with
s,t\in S
, i.e. a “term” of output sort
t
in a distinguished
variable of sort
s
. Every polynomial
p\colon 1_{t}\to T(A+1_{s})
over
A
induces an evaluation map
[p]\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
sending an element

x\colon 1_{s}\to A
of
A_{s}
to the element

1_{t}\xrightarrow{p}T(A+1_{s})\xrightarrow{T(A+x)}T(A+A)\xrightarrow{T\left[\mathit{id},\mathit{id}\right]}TA\xrightarrow{\alpha}A

of
A_{t}
. We claim that the set

\mathds{U}=\{\,[p]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{$p$ a polynomial over $A$}\,\}

forms unary presentation of
(A,\alpha)
.

Thus let
\equiv
be an
\mathcal{F}
-refinable equivalence relation on
A
. We need to show that
\equiv
forms a
\mathbf{T}
-congruence iff it is stable under polynomial evaluation maps.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Suppose that
\equiv
is a
\mathbf{T}
-congruence, i.e. there exists a (finite) quotient
e\colon(A,\alpha)\twoheadrightarrow(B,\beta)
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
with kernel
\equiv
. Every polynomial
p\colon 1_{t}\to T(A+1_{s})
over
A
yields a polynomial over
B
given by

q\colon 1_{t}\xrightarrow{p}T(A+1_{s})\xrightarrow{T(e+1_{s})}T(B+1_{s})

Then the following square commutes (which then implies that
\equiv
is a
\mathds{U}
-congruence):

\textstyle{A_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[p]}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{A_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[q]}
\textstyle{B_{t}}

To see this, let
x\colon 1_{s}\to A
be an element of
A_{s}
and consider the diagram below:

\textstyle{1_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{T(A+1_{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(A+x)}
\scriptstyle{T(e+1_{s})}
\textstyle{T(A+A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T[id,id]}
\scriptstyle{T(e+e)}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{Te}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{T(B+1_{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(A+e\cdot x)}
\textstyle{T(B+B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T[id,id]}
\textstyle{TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{B}

The upper horizontal path is precisely the image of
x
under
e\cdot[p]
, and the lower horizontal path is the image of
x
under
[q]\cdot e
. Therefore, all we need to show is that the above diagram commutes.
The leftmost square commutes by the definition of
q
, the rightmost square because
e
is a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism, and the two other squares commute trivially. Thus the diagram commutes, showing that
e\cdot[p](x)=[q]\cdot e(x)
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
Recall e.g. from
[
24
]
that every monad
\mathbf{T}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
arises from a signature and equations. More precisely, there exists an
S
-sorted signature
\Gamma
(possibly consisting of a
proper class
of operations) and a class
E
of equations between well-founded
\Gamma
-terms such that
\mathbf{T}
is the free-algebra monad associated to
\Gamma
and
E
. In particular, every
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)
corresponds to a
(\Gamma,E)
-algebra with the same carrier
A
, and every
\mathbf{T}
-congruence corresponds to a
\Gamma
-congruence, i.e. an equivalence relation stable under all
\Gamma
-operations. To avoid complicated notation, we restrict ourselves to the single-sorted case.

Now suppose that
\equiv
is an equivalence relation on a
(\Gamma,E)
-algebra
A
that contains a
\Gamma
-congruence of finite index, and that moreover
\equiv
is stable under polynomial evaluation maps. We need to show that
\equiv
is a
\Gamma
-congruence. Thus let
\gamma
be an operation symbol in
\Gamma
of (possibly infinite) arity
\kappa
, and let
x=(x_{i})_{i<\kappa}
and
y=(y_{i})_{i<\kappa}
be two
\kappa
-tuples in
A
with
x_{i}\equiv y_{i}
for all
i<\kappa
. It is our task to prove that
\gamma^{A}(x)\equiv\gamma^{A}(y)
.

Since
\equiv
contains a
\Gamma
-congruence
\sim
of finite index, we may assume that only finitely many distinct elements of
A
appear in the tuples
x
and
y
. Indeed, otherwise choose a fixed representative of every
\sim
-equivalence class and replace every component of
x
and
y
by its unique
\sim
-representative. The resulting tuples
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
contain only finitely many distinct elements of
A
because
\sim
has finite index, and one has
\gamma^{A}(x)\equiv\gamma^{A}(x^{\prime})
and
\gamma^{A}(y)\equiv\gamma^{A}(y^{\prime})
because
\sim
is a
\Gamma
-congruence and
\mathord{\sim}\subseteq\mathord{\equiv}
. Therefore it suffices to work with
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
in lieu of
x
and
y
.

The finiteness assumption on
x
and
y
implies that, in particular, there are only finitely many pairs
(u,v)\in A\times A
for which the set

I_{u,v}=\{\,i<\kappa\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x_{i}=u\text{ and }y_{i}=v\,\}

is nonempty; say the pairwise distinct pairs
(u_{1},v_{1}),\ldots,(u_{n},v_{n})
. Then the sets
I_{u_{j},v_{j}}
form a partition of
\kappa
. For each
j=0,\ldots,n
, consider the
\kappa
-tuple
x^{j}=(x_{i}^{j})_{i<\kappa}
arising from
x
by replacing all occurrences of the values
u_{1},\ldots,u_{j}
by
v_{1},\ldots,v_{j}
, respectively. Formally,

x_{i}^{j}=\begin{cases}v_{k},&i\in I_{u_{k},v_{k}}\text{ for some $k\in\{1,\ldots,j\}$}\\
x_{i},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Note that
x^{0}=x
and
x^{n}=y
. For each
j=1,\ldots n
, form the polynomial
p^{j}
over
A
given by

p^{j}=\gamma^{T(A+1)}(z^{j})\in T(A+1),

where the
\kappa
-tuple
z^{j}=(z^{j}_{i})_{i<\kappa}
in
T(A+1)
emerges from
x
by replacing all occurrences of
u_{1},\ldots,u_{j-1}
by
v_{1},\ldots,v_{j-1}
, respectively, and
u_{j}
by the unique element
\ast
of
1
. Here we view
1
and
TA
as subsets of
T(A+1)
. Formally,

z_{i}^{j}=\begin{cases}\ast,&i\in I_{u_{j},v_{j}}\\
x_{i}^{j-1},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Substituting
u_{j}
for
\ast
in
z^{j}
yields
x^{j-1}
, and thus the evaluation map
[p^{j}]\colon A\to A
maps
u_{j}
to
\gamma^{A}(x^{j-1})
. Similarly, substituting
v_{j}
for
\ast
yields
x^{j}
, and thus
[p^{j}]
maps
v_{j}
to
\gamma^{A}(x^{j})
. Since
u_{j}\equiv v_{j}
and
\equiv
is stable under polynomial evaluation maps, we get

\gamma^{A}(x^{j-1})=[p^{j}](u_{j})\equiv[p^{j}](v_{j})=\gamma^{A}(x^{j})\quad\text{for $j=1,\ldots,n$}.

This implies

\gamma^{A}(x)=\gamma^{A}(x^{0})\equiv\gamma^{A}(x^{1})\equiv\gamma^{A}(x^{2})\equiv\cdots\equiv\gamma^{A}(x^{n})=\gamma^{A}(y),

proving that
\equiv
is a
\Gamma
-congruence.

Remark 77
.

Note that in the case of monoids and
\omega
-semigroups, the polynomial presentation is rather unwieldy and much larger than the unary presentations given in Example
73
and
75
. For example, for a monoid
M
the polynomial presentation contains all unary operations

x\mapsto y_{0}xy_{1}x\ldots xy_{n}
on
M
with
y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}\in M
.

As the example of
\omega
-semigroups shows, finding a small unary presentation is generally a nontrivial challenge for algebras with infinitary operations. On the other hand, for algebras with finitary operations one can always take the unary presentation given by the elementary translations (see Example
74
), which is much smaller than the generic presentation by all polynomials.

Example 78
.

In contrast to Example
76
, in general not every
\mathbf{T}
-algebra admits a unary presentation if
\mathscr{D}\neq\mathbf{Set}
. To see this, consider the variety
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Set}_{c,d}
of sets with two constants, i.e. algebras for the signature
\Gamma=\{c,d\}
with two constant symbols. Let
\mathbf{Set}_{c\neq d}
be the full reflective subcategory on the terminal object
1
and all
X\in\mathbf{Set}_{c,d}
with
c^{X}\neq d^{X}
. Then the inclusion
\mathbf{Set}_{c\neq d}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{Set}_{c,d}
is a monadic right adjoint, and thus it induces a monad
\mathbf{T}
on
\mathscr{D}
satisfying
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}\cong\mathbf{Set}_{c\neq d}
. Explicitly,
\mathbf{T}
is given by

TX=\begin{cases}X,&c^{X}\neq d^{X};\\
1,&c^{X}=d^{X}.\end{cases}

Put
\mathcal{F}
= all finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras. For any
X\in\mathscr{D}
, a nontrivial equivalence relation
\equiv
on
{|X|}
is a
\mathbf{T}
-congruence iff
c^{X}\not\equiv d^{X}
, i.e. the corresponding quotient
X/\mathord{\equiv}
in
\mathscr{D}
lies in
\mathbf{Set}_{c\neq d}
.

Claim
.

The
\mathbf{T}
-algebra corresponding to
X=\{x,c,d\}
in
\mathbf{Set}_{c\neq d}
has no unary presentation.

Proof.

Suppose that, on the contrary,
X
has a unary presentation
\mathds{U}
. We consider two cases:

1.

\mathds{U}
contains a non-identity morphism
u
. Then
u(x)\in\{c,d\}
, and we assume w.l.o.g. that
u(x)=d
. Let
\equiv
be the smallest equivalence relation on
X
with
x\equiv c
. Then
\equiv
is a
\mathbf{T}
-congruence because
c\not\equiv d
. On the other hand,
\equiv
is not a
\mathds{U}
-congruence: we have
x\equiv c
but
u(x)=d\not\equiv c=u(c)
, where the last equation holds because
u
is a morphism of
\mathbf{Set}_{c,d}
. This contradicts our assumption that
\mathds{U}
is a unary presentation of
X
.

2.

\mathds{U}
is contains only identity morphisms. Let
\equiv^{\prime}
be the smallest equivalence relation on
X
with
c\equiv^{\prime}d
. Then
\equiv^{\prime}
is trivially a
\mathds{U}
-congruence. On the other hand,
\equiv^{\prime}
is not a
\mathbf{T}
-congruence because
c\equiv^{\prime}d
. This contradicts our assumption that
\mathds{U}
is a unary presentation.∎

Example 79
.

We have seen that in the two finitary Examples
73
and
74
the restriction to
\mathcal{F}
-refinable quotients was not needed. However, for algebras with infinitary operations, this restriction is crucial. To demonstrate this, we devise a monad
\mathbf{T}
on
\mathbf{Set}
and a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
A
such that for the polynomial presentation
\mathds{U}
, not every
\mathds{U}
-congruence is a
\mathbf{T}
-congruence.

Consider the free-algebra monad
\mathbf{T}
on
\mathbf{Set}
associated to the signature
\Gamma
with a single
\omega
-ary operation symbol
\gamma
and no equations. The free
\Gamma
-algebra
\mathbf{T}X
generated by a set
X
is carried by the set of well-founded
\Gamma
-trees (i.e.
\omega
-branching trees containing no infinite path) with leaves labeled by elements of
X
. We call a
\Gamma
-tree
t

bounded
if, for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, every path in
t
has length at most
\ell
. Now fix
X\neq\emptyset
and consider the equivalence relation
\equiv
on
TX
with
s\equiv t
iff either both
s
and
t
are bounded, or both
s
and
t
are unbounded.

Let
\mathds{U}
be the polynomial presentation of the free
\Gamma
-algebra
A=\mathbf{T}X
. We claim that the equivalence relation
\equiv
is a
\mathds{U}
-congruence, i.e. for
s\equiv t
in
TX
and any polynomial
p\in T(TX+\{\ast\})
over
TX
, we have
[p](s)\equiv[p](t)
. Here
[p](u)
(
u\in TX
) is the tree arising from
p
by substituting the tree
u
for every
\ast
-labeled leaf. There are three cases to consider:

1.

p
is unbounded. Then both
[p](s)
and
[p](t)
are unbounded, and therefore
[p](s)\equiv[p](t)
.

2.

p
is bounded and both
s
and
t
are bounded. Then both
[p](s)
and
[p](t)
are bounded, and therefore
[p](s)\equiv[p](t)
.

3.

p
is bounded and both
s
and
t
are unbounded. If the variable
\ast
occurs in
p
at least once, then both
[p](s)
and
[p](t)
are unbounded and therefore
[p](s)\equiv[p](t)
. Otherwise we have
[p](s)=p=[p](t)
and thus trivially
[p](s)\equiv[p](t)
.

Thus
\equiv
is a
\mathds{U}
-congruence. On the other hand,
\equiv
is not a
\mathbf{T}
-congruence (=
\Gamma
-congruence) on
\mathbf{T}X
. To see this, choose for each
n\geq 0
a tree
t_{n}\in TX
of height
n
. Then
t_{0}\equiv t_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
because
t_{n}
is bounded. But

\gamma^{TX}(t_{0},t_{0},t_{0},\ldots)\not\equiv\gamma^{TX}(t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},\ldots)

because the tree
\gamma^{TX}(t_{0},t_{0},t_{0},\ldots)
is bounded and the tree
\gamma^{TX}(t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},\ldots)
is unbounded. Thus
\equiv
not stable under the operation
\gamma^{TX}
, showing that
\equiv
is not a
\Gamma
-congruence.

4.2
Properties of unary presentations

Next, we establish some important technical properties of unary presentations.

Lemma 80
.

Let
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
and let
\mathds{U}
be a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}D
. Suppose that
e\colon\mathbf{T}D\twoheadrightarrow A

and
k\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
are surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms with
A,B\in\mathcal{F}
. Then the following diagram commutes for all
u\colon(TD)_{s}\to(TD)_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
, where
u_{A}
and
u_{B}
are the liftings of
u
along
e

and
k\cdot e
, respectively.

\textstyle{(TD)_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{A_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\scriptstyle{u_{A}}
\textstyle{B_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{B}}
\textstyle{(TD)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{A_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{B_{t}}

Proof.

The left-hand and the outward square commute by definition of
u_{A}
and
u_{B}
. Thus the right-hand square commutes, as it does when precomposed with the epimorphism
e
.
∎

Lemma 81
.

Let
A\in\mathscr{D}^{S}
and
\mathds{U}
a set of unary operations on
A
. Then for any two
\mathds{U}
-quotients
e_{i}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A_{i}
(
i=0,1
) the following statements hold:

1.

The subdirect product of
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
(see
A
) is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient.

2.

The pushout of
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient.

Proof.

1.

Let
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
be the subdirect product of
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
. Thus
\langle e_{0},e_{1}\rangle=m\cdot e
for some injective/order-reflecting morphism
m
.
Suppose that
u\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
is a unary operation in
\mathds{U}
. We need to show that
u
has a lifting along
e
. Consider the following diagram, where
u_{A_{i}}
is the lifting of
u
along
e_{i}
, using that
e_{i}
is a
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-quotient.

\textstyle{A_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{e_{i}}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{A_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{e_{i}}
\textstyle{B_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{B_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{(A_{0}\times A_{1})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{u_{A_{0}}\times u_{A_{1}}}
\textstyle{(A_{0}\times A_{1})_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{(A_{i})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{A_{i}}}
\textstyle{(A_{i})_{t}}

All parts except possibly the upper rectangle commute. This implies that the upper rectangle also commutes, as is does when postcomposed with the product projections
\pi_{i}
. Diagonal fill-in yields a morphism
u_{B}\colon B_{s}\to B_{t}
with
e\cdot u=u_{B}\cdot e
, i.e. a lifting of
u
along
e
. This proves that
e
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient.

2.

Let
p=p_{0}\cdot e_{0}=p_{1}\cdot e_{1}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow P
be the pushout of
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
, and let
u\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
. We need to show that
u
lifts along
p
. The proof is illustrated by the diagram below, where
u_{A_{i}}
is the lifting of
u
along the
\mathds{U}
-quotient
e_{i}
.

\textstyle{A_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{0}}
\scriptstyle{e_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{e_{0}}
\scriptstyle{e_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{0,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{0,s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{A_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1,s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{A_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{1,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{P}}
\textstyle{P_{t}}

Then the morphisms
p_{0}\cdot u_{A_{0}}
and
p_{1}\cdot u_{A_{1}}
merge
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
. Therefore, by the universal property of the pushout
p
, there exists a unique
u_{P}\colon P_{s}\to P_{t}
with
p_{0}\cdot u_{A_{0}}=u_{P}\cdot p_{0}
and
p_{1}\cdot u_{A_{1}}=u_{P}\cdot p_{1}
. Thus all parts of the above diagram commute. This implies
p\cdot u=u_{P}\cdot p
, i.e.
u
lifts along
p
.

∎

4.3
Unary presentations for
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras

In this subsection, we show that any unary presentation of a free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
\mathbf{T}D
(
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
) can be extended to a presentation of the free
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D
. First, we characterize
\mathcal{F}
-refinable quotients of
\mathbf{T}D
in terms of
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
:

Lemma 82
.

Let
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
. A quotient
e\colon TD\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
is
\mathcal{F}
-refinable iff it is extensible to a quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
, i.e.
e=V\hat{e}\cdot\iota_{D}
for some
\hat{e}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
.

\textstyle{TD\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{D}}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\hat{e}}
\textstyle{A}

Proof.

Suppose the
e
is
\mathcal{F}
-refinable, i.e.
e=p\cdot q
for some quotient
q\colon\mathbf{T}D\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
with
B\in\mathcal{F}
and some morphism
p\colon B\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
. By Remark
17
.
1
it follows that

e=p\cdot q=p\cdot Vq^{+}\cdot\iota_{D},

i.e.
e
is extensible to the quotient
p\cdot q^{+}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
.

Conversely, suppose that
e
is extensible to a quotient
\hat{e}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
. Since
A
is finitely copresentable in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
, see Remark
10
, the morphism
\hat{e}
factors through the cofiltered limit cone defining
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
. That is, there is a finite quotient
