q\colon\mathbf{T}D\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
with
B\in\mathcal{F}
and a morphism
p\colon B\twoheadrightarrow A
with
\hat{e}=p\cdot q^{+}
. Therefore

e=V\hat{e}\cdot\iota_{D}=p\cdot Vq^{+}\cdot\iota_{D}=p\cdot q,

showing that
e
is
\mathcal{F}
-refinable.
∎

Next, we show that given a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}D
, the unary operations extend to
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D
:

Lemma 83
.

Let
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
and let
\mathds{U}
be a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}D
. Then every unary operation

u\colon(TD)_{s}\to(TD)_{t}
in
\mathds{U}

extends uniquely to a morphism
\hat{u}\colon(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{s}\to(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{t}
in

\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
making the following square commute.

\textstyle{(TD)_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{D}}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{(TD)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{D}}
\textstyle{V(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\hat{u}}
\textstyle{V(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{t}}

Proof.

For each
u\colon(TD)_{s}\to(TD)_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
, the morphisms

u_{A}\cdot e^{+}\colon(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{s}\to A_{t}

(where
e
ranges over
surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms
e\colon\mathbf{T}D\twoheadrightarrow A
with

A\in\mathcal{F}
and
u_{A}
is the lifting of
u
along
e
) form a
compatible family over the diagram
defining
(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{t}
by Lemma
80
.
Hence there exists a unique morphism
\hat{u}\colon(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{s}\to(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{t}
in

\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
with
e^{+}\cdot\hat{u}=u_{A}\cdot e^{+}
for all
e
. Therefore in the
diagram below the outside and all parts except, perhaps, for the upper square
commute:

\textstyle{(TD)_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{D}}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{(TD)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{D}}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{V(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Ve^{+}}
\scriptstyle{V\hat{u}}
\textstyle{V(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Ve^{+}}
\textstyle{A_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{A}}
\textstyle{A_{t}}

It follows that the upper square commutes when postcomposed with the morphisms

Ve^{+}
. Since by Remark
10
the functor
V

preserves limits (and thus the morphisms
Ve^{+}
are jointly
monomorphic), the upper square commutes. Moreover,
\hat{u}
is unique with
this property because
\iota_{D}
is dense and
(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{t}
is a Hausdorff space (see Remark

17
.
1
).
∎

Therefore we can extend our notion of
\mathds{U}
-quotients and
\mathcal{F}
-quotients of
\mathbf{T}D
as follows:

Definition 84
.

Let
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
and
\varphi\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow P
a quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
.

1.

\varphi
is called a
profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-quotient
of
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D
if it carries a profinite quotient algebra of
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D
, i.e. there exists a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra structure
(P,\varrho)
on
P
such that
(P,\varrho)
is a profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra and
\varphi\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow(P,\varrho)
is a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism.

2.

If
\mathds{U}
is a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}D
, then
\varphi
is called a
\mathds{U}
-quotient
of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
if for every
u\colon(TD)_{s}\to(TD)_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
, the extension
\hat{u}\colon(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{s}\to(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{t}
has a lifting along
p
:

\textstyle{(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{u}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{P_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists u_{P}}
\textstyle{P_{t}}

Remark 85
.

If the object
P
in the above definition is finite, then
\varphi\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow P
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
iff its restriction
V\varphi\cdot\iota_{D}\colon TD\twoheadrightarrow A
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
TD
. This follows immediately from the definition of
\hat{u}
and the fact that
\iota_{D}
is dense, see Remark
17
.
1
.

Lemma 86
.

Let
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
and suppose that
\mathds{U}
is a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}D
.
Then for any two
\mathds{U}
-quotients
e_{i}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow P_{i}
(
i=0,1
) in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
the following statements hold:

1.

The subdirect product of
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient.

2.

The pushout of
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient.

3.

Given a
\mathds{U}
-quotient
\varphi\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P
and a finite quotient
e\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A
, there exists a finite quotient
h\colon P\twoheadrightarrow B
such that
h\cdot\varphi
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
and
e\leq h
.

\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A}

4.

Given a
\mathds{U}
-quotient
\varphi\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow P
and a finite quotient
e\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A
, let
\mathscr{S}
be the full subcategory of
(P\downarrow\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{f}^{S})
on all finite quotients
e\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A
such that
e\cdot\varphi
is a
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-quotient. Then
P
is the cofiltered limit of the diagram

\pi_{S}\colon\colon\mathscr{S}\to\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S},\quad(e\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A)\mapsto A,

with limit projections
e
.

Proof.

The first two statements are completely analogous to Lemma
81
and its proof.

3.

The proof is illustrated by the
diagram below:

\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{+}}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{A}

Since the finite object
A
is finitely copresentable in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
, the morphism
e\cdot\varphi
factors through the cofiltered limit cone defining
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
. That is, there exists a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}D\twoheadrightarrow C
with
C\in\mathcal{F}
and a morphism
j\colon C\twoheadrightarrow A
with
e\cdot\varphi=j\cdot g^{+}
. Then
g^{+}
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
by Remark
85
, since
\mathds{U}
is a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}D
. Form the pushout
q=h\cdot\varphi=k\cdot g^{+}
of
\varphi
and
g^{+}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
. The pushout exists because
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
, being a locally finitely copresentable category, is cocomplete (see
A
). Note that
k
and
q
are surjective because pushouts preserve epimorphisms, and epimorphisms in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
are surjective by Lemma
13
. This implies that
B
is finite, because
C
is finite. Moreover,
q
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient by part 2 of this lemma. Since the morphisms
e
and
j
merge
\varphi
and
g^{+}
, the universal property of the pushout yields a morphism
l
with
l\cdot h=e
. Therefore
e\leq h
, and
h\cdot\varphi=q
is a finite
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
, as required.

4.

We first show that the poset
\mathscr{S}
is cofiltered. First,
\mathscr{S}
is nonempty because it contains the image of the unique morphism
h\colon P\to 1
into the terminal object. Secondly,
\mathscr{S}
is directed, i.e. for any two elements
e_{i}\in\mathscr{S}
(
i=0,1
) there exists an element
q\in\mathscr{S}
with
e_{i}\leq q
. To see this, form the subdirect product
e
of
e_{0}\cdot\varphi
and
e_{1}\cdot\varphi
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
. It is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
by part 1 of this lemma. Moreover since
e_{i}\cdot\varphi\leq\varphi
, the minimality of
e
gives
e\leq\varphi
, i.e.
e=q\cdot\varphi
for some
q
. Then
q\in\mathscr{S}
and
e_{i}\leq q
, as desired.

\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{e_{i}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{i}}

Since the category
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
is locally finitely copresentable, the object
P
is the limit of its canonical diagram

\pi\colon(P\downarrow\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{f}^{S})\to\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S},\quad(g\colon P\to A)\mapsto A,

see
A
. Therefore it suffices to show that the inclusion
I\colon\mathscr{S}\to(P\downarrow\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{f}^{S})
is a final functor, i.e. every morphism
g\colon P\to A
with finite codomain factors through some quotient in
\mathscr{S}
. But this follows immediately from part 3 of this lemma.∎

The following lemma shows that the lifting property of a unary presentation extends to profinite algebras:

Lemma 87
.

Let
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
and let
\mathds{U}
be a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}D
.
Then for
any quotient
\varphi\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow P
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
,

\text{$\varphi$ is a profinite ${\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}$-quotient}\quad\text{iff}\quad\text{$\varphi$ is a $\mathds{U}$-quotient}.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
) Suppose that
\varphi
is a profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-quotient, i.e.
P
carries a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra structure such that
P
is profinite and
\varphi\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow P
is
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism.
For any finite quotient
h\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A
in

\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
we have the corresponding
\mathcal{F}
-quotient
e\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=V(h\cdot\varphi)\cdot\iota_{D}\colon\mathbf{T}D\twoheadrightarrow A
by Remark

17
.
3
. Since
\mathds{U}
is a
unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}D
, each
u\colon(TD)_{s}\to(TD)_{t}
in
\mathds{U}

has a lifting

u_{A}\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
along
e
.

Since
P
is a profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra,
P
is the cofiltered limit of the diagram of all
finite quotient algebras

h\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A
, see Lemma
21
. The morphisms

u_{A}\cdot h\colon P_{s}\to A_{t}
form a compatible family
over this diagram
by Lemma
80
. Therefore there
exists a morphism
u_{P}\colon P_{s}\to P_{t}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
with
h\cdot u_{P}=u_{A}\cdot h

for all
h
. It follows that
u_{P}
is a lifting of
\hat{u}
along
\varphi
(i.e.
\varphi\cdot\hat{u}=u_{P}\cdot\varphi
) since this holds when postcomposed with the limit
projections
h
.

\textstyle{(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{u}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{P_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{P}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{P_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{A}}
\textstyle{A_{t}}

This shows that
\varphi
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Let
\hat{e}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow P
be a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
. We need to show that
P
can be equipped with a

{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra structure such that
P
is profinite and
\hat{e}
is a

{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism. Let
\mathscr{S}
be the full subcategory of
(P\downarrow\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{f}^{S})
on all finite quotients
e\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A
such that
e\cdot\varphi\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow A
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
. By Lemma
81
.4, the object
P
is the cofiltered limit of the diagram

\pi\colon\mathscr{S}\to\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S},\quad(e\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A)\mapsto A,

with limit projections
e
. For each
e\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathscr{S}
we have that
e\cdot\varphi
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D
by the definition of
\mathscr{S}
. Therefore
q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=Ve\cdot V\varphi\cdot\iota_{D}
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient of
TD
by Remark
85
, and moreover
q
is
\mathcal{F}
-refinable by Lemma
82
. Since
\mathds{U}
forms a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}D
, it follows that there exists a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra structure
(A,\alpha)
on
A
such that
q\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
is a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism. Thus
q^{+}=e\cdot\varphi\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha^{+})
is a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism by diagram (
1
) in Remark
15
.

Since the forgetful functor from
\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
to
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
creates
limits, see
A
, it follows that there is a
unique
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra structure

\varrho\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}P\to P
making the cone
(e\colon P\twoheadrightarrow A)_{e\in\mathscr{S}}
a cofiltered
limit cone in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Thus
(P,\varrho)
is profinite. To see that
\varphi\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}D\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
is a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism, consider the diagram
below:

\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\hat{\mu}}^{\mathcal{F}}_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi}
\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}e}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{+}}
\textstyle{A}

The lower square commutes for all
e\in\mathscr{S}
in
\mathscr{S}
by the
definition of
\varrho
, and the outside commutes because
e\cdot\varphi=q^{+}
is a
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-homomorphism. Thus
also the upper square commutes, as it commutes when postcomposed with the limit
projections
e
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
.

5
Recognizable Languages and Duality

In this section, we set up our duality framework for algebraic language theory. As explained in the introduction, the idea is to consider a variety
\mathscr{C}
of algebras that dualizes to the variety
\mathscr{D}
on the level of finite algebras, and form varieties of languages inside
\mathscr{C}
. To this end, we make the following

Assumptions 88
.

In addition to the Assumptions
5
on
\mathscr{D}
and the monad
\mathbf{T}
on
\mathscr{D}^{S}
, we fix a variety
\mathscr{C}
of algebras such that

(i)

\mathscr{C}
is locally finite;

(ii)

the signature of
\mathscr{C}
contains a
constant;

(iii)

the full subcategories
\mathscr{C}_{f}
and

\mathscr{D}_{f}
on
finite algebras are dually equivalent.

Remark 89
.

Assumption (ii) will be used to define derivatives of languages in the many-sorted case, see Definition
113
. If
S=1
, it can be dropped.

Example 90
.

The following pairs of categories
\mathscr{C}
/
\mathscr{D}
satisfy our assumptions:

1.

\mathscr{C}=\mathbf{BA}
and
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Set}
: Stone duality

[
21
]
yields a dual equivalence
\mathbf{BA}_{f}^{op}\simeq\mathbf{Set}_{f}
,
mapping a finite boolean algebra to the set of its atoms.

2.

\mathscr{C}=\mathbf{DL}_{01}

and
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Pos}
: Birkhoff
duality

[
10
]
gives a dual equivalence

(\mathbf{DL}_{01})_{f}^{op}\simeq\mathbf{Pos}_{f}
, mapping a finite distributive lattice to the
poset
of its join-irreducible elements.

3.

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{D}={\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}
: the self-duality
({\mathbf{JSL}_{0}})_{f}^{op}\simeq({\mathbf{JSL}_{0}})_{f}
maps a finite semilattice
(X,\vee,0)
to its opposite
semilattice
(X,\wedge,1)
.

4.

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
for a finite field
K
:
the familiar self-duality of
(\mathbf{Vec}_{K})_{f}
maps a finite (= finite-dimensional)
vector space

X
to its dual space
X^{*}=\mathbf{Vec}_{K}(X,K)
.

Remark 91
.

1.

Recall from Lemma
9
that the category
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
is the
pro-completion of
\mathscr{D}_{f}
. Moreover, since the variety
\mathscr{C}
is locally finite,
\mathscr{C}
is the

ind-completion
(the free completion under
filtered colimits) of
\mathscr{C}_{f}
.
Then
the dual equivalence between
\mathscr{C}_{f}
and
\mathscr{D}_{f}
extends to a dual
equivalence between
\mathscr{C}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
: we have

\widehat{\mathscr{D}}\simeq\mathsf{Pro}\,(\mathscr{D}_{f})\simeq[\mathsf{Ind}\,(\mathscr{D}_{f}^{op})]^{op}\simeq[\mathsf{Ind}\,(\mathscr{C}_{f})]^{op}\simeq\mathscr{C}^{op}.

We denote the equivalence functors by

P\colon\widehat{\mathscr{D}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{C}^{op}\quad\text{and}\quad P^{-1}\colon\mathscr{C}^{op}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widehat{\mathscr{D}}

and the corresponding natural isomorphism between
\mathsf{Id}_{\mathscr{C}^{op}}
and
PP^{-1}
by

\lambda\colon\mathsf{Id}_{\mathscr{C}^{op}}\xrightarrow{\cong}PP^{-1}.

2.

Denote by
{|\mathord{-}|}
the forgetful functors of
\mathscr{C}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
into
\mathbf{Set}
, and by

\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{D}}
the free objects on one generator both in
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
, respectively. Note that both
\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{D}}
are finite because
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
are assumed to be locally finite. The two finite objects
O_{\mathscr{C}}\in\mathscr{C}_{f}
and
O_{\mathscr{D}}\in\mathscr{D}_{f}
defined by

O_{\mathscr{C}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=P\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{D}}\quad\text{and}\quad O_{\mathscr{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=P^{-1}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}

play the role of a

dualizing object
(also called a
schizophrenic object
in

[
21
]
) of
\mathscr{C}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
. This means that there is a natural
isomorphism
between the functors
{|\mathord{-}|}\cdot P^{op}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}(\mathord{-},O_{\mathscr{D}})\colon\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{op}\to\mathbf{Set}
given for all
D\in\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
by

{|PD|}\cong\mathscr{C}(\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}},PD)\cong\widehat{\mathscr{D}}(P^{-1}PD,P^{-1}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}})=\widehat{\mathscr{D}}(P^{-1}PD,O_{\mathscr{D}})\cong\widehat{\mathscr{D}}(D,O_{\mathscr{D}}).

Symmetrically, we have a natural isomorphism
{|\mathord{-}|}\cdot{P^{-1}}\cong\mathscr{C}(\mathord{-},O_{\mathscr{C}})\colon\mathscr{C}^{op}\to\mathbf{Set}
.
It follows that the objects
O_{\mathscr{C}}
and
O_{\mathscr{D}}
have the essentially the same underlying set:

{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}\cong\widehat{\mathscr{D}}(\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{D}},O_{\mathscr{D}})\cong{|P\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{D}}|}={|O_{\mathscr{C}}|}.

Explicitly, this bijection maps an element
y\colon 1\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
of
{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
to the element

1\xrightarrow{\eta}{|\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}|}\xrightarrow{\lambda}{|PP^{-1}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}|}={|PO_{\mathscr{D}}|}\xrightarrow{Py^{\mathscr{D}}}{|P\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{D}}|}={|O_{\mathscr{C}}|}

of
{|O_{\mathscr{C}}|}
, where
\eta\colon 1\to{|\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}|}
is the inclusion of the generator and
y^{\mathscr{D}}\colon\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{D}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
is the unique extension of
y
to a morphism in
\mathscr{D}
.

3.

Recall from Lemma
13
that epimorphisms in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
coincide with the surjective morphisms. Accordingly,
subobjects
in the dual category
\mathscr{C}
are represented by monomorphisms, i.e. injective morphisms.

Example 92
.

Consider the categories of Example
90
.

1.

\mathscr{C}=\mathbf{BA}
and
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Set}
with
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}=\mathbf{Stone}
: the dual equivalence
P\colon\mathbf{Stone}\simeq\mathbf{BA}^{op}
is the classical Stone duality
[
21
]
, mapping a Stone space to the boolean algebra of its clopen subsets. We have

O_{\mathbf{BA}}=\{0<1\}\quad\text{and}\quad O_{\mathbf{Set}}=\{0,1\}.

2.

\mathscr{C}=\mathbf{DL}_{01}
and
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Pos}
with
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}=\mathbf{Priest}
: the dual equivalence
P\colon\mathbf{Priest}\simeq\mathbf{DL}_{01}^{op}
is the classical Priestley duality
[
33
]
, mapping a Priestley space to the lattice of its clopen upper sets. We have

O_{\mathbf{DL}_{01}}=\{0<1\}\quad\text{and}\quad O_{\mathbf{Pos}}=\{0<1\}.

3.

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{D}={\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}
with
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}=\mathbf{Stone}({\mathbf{JSL}_{0}})
: the dual equivalence
P\colon\mathbf{Stone}({\mathbf{JSL}_{0}})\simeq{\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}^{op}
maps a Stone semilattice to the semilattice of its clopen ideals. We have

O_{\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}=\{0<1\}.

4.

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
with
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}=\mathbf{Stone}(\mathbf{Vec}_{K})
: the dual equivalence
P\colon\mathbf{Stone}(\mathbf{Vec}_{K})\simeq\mathbf{Vec}_{K}^{op}
maps a Stone vector space
X
to the space of linear continuous maps from
X
into
K
. We have

O_{\mathbf{Vec}_{K}}=K.

5.1
Recognizable languages

A language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
of finite words may be identified with its characteristic
function
L\colon\Sigma^{*}\to\{0,1\}
. To model languages in our categorical setting, we replace the one-sorted
alphabet
\Sigma
by an
S
-sorted alphabet
\Sigma
in

\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
, and represent it in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
via the free object
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\in\mathscr{D}^{S}_{f}
generated
by
\Sigma
.
The output set
\{0,1\}
is replaced by a finite “object
of outputs” in
\mathscr{D}^{S}_{f}
, viz. the object with
O_{\mathscr{D}}\in\mathscr{D}_{f}
in each sort. By abuse of notation, we
denote this object of
\mathscr{D}^{S}_{f}
also by
O_{\mathscr{D}}
. This leads to the following definition, unifying concepts in
[
11
]
and
[
3
]
.

Definition 93
.

A

language
over the alphabet
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
is a morphism

L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}

in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
. It is called
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable
if there exists a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to(A,\alpha)
with
(A,\alpha)\in\mathcal{F}
and a morphism
p\colon A\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
with
L=p\cdot h
.

\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{O_{\mathscr{D}}}
\textstyle{(A,\alpha)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}

In this case, we say that
L
is recognized by
h
(via
p
)
. If
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras, an
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language is called
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable
. We denote by

{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma)

the set of all
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable and
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages over
\Sigma
, respectively.

Note that, although languages are morphisms in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
, the set
{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
is one-sorted.

Example 94

(Monoids)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
with
O_{\mathbf{Set}}=\{0,1\}
. A language

L\colon T_{*}\Sigma\to O_{\mathbf{Set}}
corresponds to
a language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
of finite words. It is recognized
by
a monoid morphism
h\colon\Sigma^{*}\to A
iff
L=h^{-1}[Y]
for some
subset
Y\subseteq A
. Recognizable languages coincide with regular languages, i.e. those accepted by finite automata; see e.g.
[
30
]
.

Example 95

(
\omega
-semigroups)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
on

\mathbf{Set}^{2}
with
O_{\mathbf{Set}}=\{0,1\}
. Since

T_{\infty}(\Sigma,\emptyset)=(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
, a language
L\colon T_{\infty}(\Sigma,\emptyset)

\to O_{\mathbf{Set}}
corresponds to an
\infty
-language

L\subseteq(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
. It is recognized by an

\omega
-semigroup morphism
h\colon(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})\to A
iff

L=h^{-1}[Y]
for some two-sorted subset
Y\subseteq A
. An
\infty
-language is recognizable iff it is
\infty
-regular
, i.e.
accepted by some finite Büchi automaton; see
[
27
]
.

Example 96

(
K
-algebras)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{K}
be the free
K
-algebra monad in
\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
with
O_{\mathbf{Vec}_{K}}=K
.
As seen in Example
4
we have
\mathbf{T}_{K}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}=K[\Sigma]
, the
K
-algebra of polynomials over
\Sigma
, and a language
L\colon T_{K}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to K
corresponds to a weighted language
L^{@}\colon\Sigma^{*}\to K
over the field
K
. It is recognizable if there exists a
K
-algebra homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}_{K}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to A
into some finite
K
-algebra and a linear map
p\colon A\to K
with
L=p\cdot h
.
A weighted language is recognizable
iff it is accepted by some finite linear weighted automaton over
K
; see
[
35
]
.

The topological perspective on regular languages rests on the important observation
that the regular languages over
\Sigma
correspond exactly to the clopen
subsets of the Stone space
\widehat{\Sigma^{*}}
of profinite words, or equivalently
to
continuous maps from
\widehat{\Sigma^{*}}
into the discrete space
\{0,1\}
;
see e.g.
[
30
, Prop. VI.3.12]
. This generalizes from the
monad
\mathbf{T}_{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
to arbitrary monads
\mathbf{T}
:

Theorem 97
.

\mathcal{F}
-recognizable languages over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
correspond bijectively to morphisms from

\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
to
O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
. The bijection is given by

(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{\hat{L}}O_{\mathscr{D}})\mapsto(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{V\hat{L}}O_{\mathscr{D}}).

Proof.

We first show that the indicated bijection is well-defined, i.e. for any morphism
\hat{L}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
the language
L\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=V\hat{L}\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
is
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable. Since the finite object
O_{\mathscr{D}}
is finitely copresentable in

\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
, see Remark
10
, the morphism
\hat{L}
factors
through the cofiltered limit cone defining
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
, i.e. there exists
a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to(A,\alpha)
with
(A,\alpha)\in\mathcal{F}
and a
morphism
p\colon A\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
with
\hat{L}=p\cdot{h^{+}}
. Then
L
is recognized by
h
via
p
:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=V\hat{L}\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}

(def.
L
)

\displaystyle=p\cdot Vh^{+}\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}

(
\hat{L}=p\cdot h^{+}
)

\displaystyle=p\cdot h

\displaystyle\text{(def. $\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}$ in Rem. 2.11)}.

Therefore
L
is
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable. Conversely, let
L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
be an
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language.
Choose a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to(A,\alpha)
with
(A,\alpha)
and a
morphism
p\colon A\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
with
L=p\cdot h
. This yields the following
morphism in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
:

\hat{L}=(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{{h^{+}}}A\xrightarrow{p}O_{\mathscr{D}}).

Since
L=V\hat{L}\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
and
\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is dense by Remark

17
.
1
, the morphism
\hat{L}
is independent of
the choice of
h
and
p
. By construction the maps
\hat{L}\mapsto L
and
L\mapsto\hat{L}
are mutually inverse, which proves the theorem.
∎

Remark 98

(
\mathscr{C}
-algebraic structure on
{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
)
.

From the above theorem and Remark

91
.2, we deduce

{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\cong\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}},O_{\mathscr{D}})=\prod_{s}\widehat{\mathscr{D}}((\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s},O_{\mathscr{D}})\cong\prod_{s}{|P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}|}.

(3)

\mathscr{C}

{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\cong\prod_{s}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
in

.

Proposition 99
.

{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
forms a subobject of the product

\prod_{s}O_{\mathscr{C}}^{{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}}
in
\mathscr{C}
.

Remark 100
.

More precisely, let

be the injective map

(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{\;L\;}O_{\mathscr{D}})\quad\mapsto\quad({|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}\xrightarrow{\;L_{s}\;}{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}\xrightarrow{\;\cong\;}{|O_{\mathscr{C}}|})_{s\in S},

Remark 102
.

For the free-monoid monad
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
and
\mathcal{F}=
all finite monoids, we recover an important result of Pippenger
[
31
]
: the boolean
algebra of regular languages over
\Sigma
is dual to the Stone space

\widehat{\Sigma^{*}}
of profinite words. In fact, in this one-sorted case the isomorphism (
3
) gives

{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma)\cong P(\widehat{\Sigma^{*}})
.

5.2
Syntactic
\mathbf{T}
-algebras

In algebraic language theory, a recurring theme is to characterize languages by properties of their
syntactic algebras
, i.e. their minimal recognizing algebras. For example, Schützenberger’s theorem
[
41
]
asserts that a regular language is star-free iff its syntactic monoid is aperiodic. Since the syntactic monoid of language can be computed directly from an accepting finite automaton, and aperiodicity is a decidable property of finite monoids, this implies that star-freeness is decidable.

It turns out that the language-theoretic concept of a syntactic algebra is intimately related to our purely algebraic notion of a unary presentation. The results of this subsection serve to put our concepts into the context of classical algebraic language theory; they are, however, not used in the sequel and may be skipped by readers interested only in the variety theorem and its applications.

Definition 103
.

Let
L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
be an
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language. By a

syntactic

\mathbf{T}
-algebra for
L
is meant a

\mathbf{T}
-algebra
A_{L}
together with a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism

e_{L}\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{L}
(called a
syntactic morphism
for
L
) such that

(i)

e_{L}
recognizes
L
;

(ii)

e_{L}

factors through any surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
with
A\in\mathcal{F}
that recognizes
L
, i.e.
e_{L}=h\cdot e
for some
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A_{L}
.

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{L}}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A_{L}}

Remark 104
.

The universal property determines
A_{L}
and
e_{L}
uniquely up to isomorphism. Moreover, it implies that
A_{L}\in\mathcal{F}
because
\mathcal{F}
is closed under quotients.

Example 105
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
with
O_{\mathbf{Set}}=\{0,1\}
. The syntactic monoid of a
recognizable language
L\colon\Sigma^{*}\to\{0,1\}
is formed as follows. Let
\equiv_{L}
be the monoid congruence on
\Sigma^{*}
given by

x\equiv_{L}x^{\prime}\quad\text{iff}\quad L(yxz)=L(yx^{\prime}z)\text{ for all $y,z\in\Sigma^{*}$}.

Then the quotient monoid
\Sigma^{*}/\mathord{\equiv_{L}}
is finite and the projection

e_{L}\colon\Sigma^{*}\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma^{*}/\mathord{\equiv_{L}}

is a syntactic morphism for
L
.

The above definition of
\equiv_{L}
involves for each
y,z\in\Sigma^{*}
the unary operation
x\mapsto yxz
on
\Sigma^{*}
. It can be expressed as the composite of the two unary operations
x\mapsto yx
and
x\mapsto xz
appearing in the unary presentation of
\Sigma^{*}
in Example
73
. This is no coincidence: we will show below that one can always derive a syntactic congruence from a unary presentation, and vice versa.

Notation 106
.

Let
\mathds{U}
be a set of unary operations on
T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
, and denote by
{\overline{\mathds{U}}}
its closure under composition and identity morphisms
\mathit{id}\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
.

1.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of algebras. The
S
-sorted equivalence relation
\equiv_{\mathds{U},L}
on
{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}
is defined as follows: for any two elements
x,x^{\prime}\in{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
, put

x\equiv_{\mathds{U},L,s}x^{\prime}\quad\text{iff}\quad L_{t}\cdot u(x)=L_{t}\cdot u(x^{\prime})\text{ for all $u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}$ in $\overline{\mathds{U}}$}.

2.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of ordered algebras. The
S
-sorted preorder
\leq_{\mathds{U},L}
on
{|A|}
is defined as follows: for any two elements
x,x^{\prime}\in{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
, put

x\leq_{\mathds{U},L,s}x^{\prime}\quad\text{iff}\quad L_{t}\cdot u(x)\leq L_{t}\cdot u(x^{\prime})\text{ for all $u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}$ in $\overline{\mathds{U}}$}.

Remark 107
.

If
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of algebras then
\equiv_{\mathds{U},L}
is a congruence on
T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
. Indeed,
\equiv_{\mathds{U},L,s}
is the intersection of the kernels of the morphisms
L_{t}\cdot u
, where
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
. Analogously, if
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of ordered algebras,
\leq_{\mathds{U},L}
is a stable preorder on
T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
. We write

e_{L}\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}/\mathord{\equiv_{\mathds{U},L}}\quad\text{and}\quad e_{L}\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}/\mathord{\leq_{\mathds{U},L}},

respectively, for the corresponding quotients in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
; see
C
and
C
.

Theorem 108
.

For any set
\mathds{U}
of unary operations on
T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathds{U}
is a unary presentation of the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
.

(ii)

Every
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language
L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
has a syntactic
\mathbf{T}
-algebra, and
e_{L}
carries a quotient of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
that forms a syntactic morphism for
L
.

To prepare the proof, we need to establish an important property of the output object
O_{\mathscr{D}}
:

Lemma 109
.

1.

If
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of algebras, then
O_{\mathscr{D}}
is a cogenerator of
\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
: for any object
D\in\mathscr{D}^{S}_{f}
and any
two elements
x,y\in{|D|}_{s}
with
s\in S
we have

x=y\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall(D\xrightarrow{k}O_{\mathscr{D}})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}k(x)=k(y).

2.

If
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of ordered algebras, then
O_{\mathscr{D}}
is an order-cogenerator of
\mathscr{D}_{f}^{S}
: for any object
D\in\mathscr{D}^{S}_{f}
and any
two elements
x,y\in{|D|}_{s}
with
s\in S
we have

x\leq y\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall(D\xrightarrow{k}O_{\mathscr{D}})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}k(x)\leq k(y).

Proof.

To prove (b), suppose first that
S=1
. Given
D\in\mathscr{D}_{f}
, its dual object
PD\in\mathscr{C}_{f}
is finite and thus finitely generated, so there exists a surjective morphism (i.e. a strong epimorphism)
e\colon\coprod_{i\in I}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}\twoheadrightarrow PD
in
\mathscr{C}_{f}
, where
I={|PD|}
. The dual morphism
m\colon D\rightarrowtail\prod_{i\in I}O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\mathscr{D}_{f}
is a strong monomorphism and thus order-reflecting. Therefore
x\not\leq y
in
{|D|}
implies
m(x)\not\leq m(y)
, and thus
\pi_{i}m(x)\not\leq\pi_{i}m(y)
for some
i\in I
because the product projections
\pi_{i}\colon\prod_{i}O_{\mathscr{D}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
are jointly order-reflecting. This shows that the morphism
k\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{i}\cdot m\colon D\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
separates
x
and
y
, as desired.

Now let
S
be arbitrary and
x\not\leq y\in{|D|}_{s}
. By the above argument there exists a morphism
k_{s}\colon D_{s}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}

in
\mathscr{D}
with
k_{s}(x)\not\leq k_{s}(y)
. For any sort
t\neq s
, pick an arbitrary
morphism
k_{t}\colon D_{t}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
. Such a morphism exists because, by our
Assumption
88
(ii) that the signature of
\mathscr{C}
contains a constant, we
dually have a morphism
\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}\to PD_{t}
in
\mathscr{C}_{f}
. Thus
k\colon D\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
is
a morphism in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
with
k(x)\not\leq k(y)
.

The proof of (a) is completely analogous, replacing inequations by equations.
∎

Proof of Theorem
108
.

We prove the case where
\mathscr{D}
is a variety of ordered algebras; for the unordered case, just replace
\leq_{\mathds{U},L}
by
\equiv_{\mathds{U},L}
and inequations by equations throughout the proof. For brevity, put
A_{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}/\mathord{\leq_{\mathds{U},L}}
.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
Suppose that
\mathds{U}
is a unary
presentation of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
, and
let

L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
be an
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable
language.

(a)

We show that there exists a morphism
p_{L}\colon A_{L}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
with

L=p_{L}\cdot e_{L}
, using the homomorphism theorem. Let
x,y\in{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}

with

e_{L}(x)\leq e_{L}(y)
, i.e.
x\leq_{\mathds{U},L}y
. Since
\overline{\mathds{U}}
contains
all identities, putting
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathit{id}_{(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}}
in the definition of
\leq_{\mathds{U},L}
(see Notation

106
) yields

L(x)\leq L(y)
. The homomorphism theorem gives the desired
p_{L}
.

(b)

Since
L
is
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable, there is a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism

e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
into a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
(A,\alpha)\in\mathcal{F}
and a morphism
p\colon A\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
with
L=p\cdot e
. Thus
e
is an
\mathcal{F}
-quotient, and since
\mathds{U}
is a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
, it follows that
e
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient, i.e. every
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
has a lifting
u_{A}\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}

along

e
with
e\cdot u=u_{A}\cdot e
. We claim that there exists a morphism
h\colon A\to A_{L}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
with

e_{L}=h\cdot e
. This follows from the homomorphism theorem: let

x,y\in{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
with
e(x)\leq e(y)
. Then, for all sorts
t
and
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
,

L\cdot u(x)=p\cdot e\cdot u(x)=p\cdot u_{A}\cdot e(x)\leq p\cdot u_{A}\cdot e(y)=p\cdot e\cdot u(y)=L\cdot u(y).

Thus
x\leq_{\mathds{U},L}y
, or equivalently
e_{L}(x)\leq e_{L}(y)
, and the homomorphism
theorem gives the desired
h
.

(c)

We show that (I)

e_{L}
is
\mathcal{F}
-refinable, and (II)
e_{L}
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient. This implies the claim: since

\mathds{U}
is a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
it follows that the morphism is
e_{L}
is a
\mathbf{T}
-quotient, i.e. it can be equipped with a

\mathbf{T}
-algebra
structure making
e_{L}
a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism. And part (a) and (b) show that

e_{L}
recognizes
L
and has the universal property of a syntactic morphism.

(I) is clear since
e_{L}=h\cdot e
by (b) and
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
is an
\mathcal{F}
-quotient of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
. For (II) we need to show that for all
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
and all
x,y\in{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
with
x\leq_{\mathds{U},L}y
, we have
u(x)\leq_{\mathds{U},L}u(y)
. Note that
for all sorts
t^{\prime}
and all
u^{\prime}\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t^{\prime}}
in
\mathds{U}
we have
u^{\prime}\cdot u\in\overline{\mathds{U}}
because
\overline{\mathds{U}}
is closed under composition. Thus
x\leq_{\mathds{U},L}y

implies

L\cdot(u^{\prime}\cdot u)(x)\leq L\cdot(u^{\prime}\cdot u)(x)\quad\text{for all sorts $t^{\prime}$ and $u^{\prime}\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t^{\prime}}$ in $\mathds{U}$},

which means precisely that
u(x)\leq_{\mathds{U},L}u(y)
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
Suppose that, for any
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language
L

over
\Sigma
, the morphism
e_{L}\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{L}
is a
\mathbf{T}
-quotient that forms a syntactic morphism for
L
. We verify that

\mathds{U}
is a unary
presentation. Thus let
e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
be an
\mathcal{F}
-refinable quotient in

\mathscr{D}^{S}
. We need to show that
e
is a
\mathbf{T}
-quotient iff
e
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient.

For the “only if” direction, suppose that
e
is a
\mathbf{T}
-quotient, i.e.
A
carries a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
structure
(A,\alpha)
making
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism. We need to show that
e
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient: every
unary operation
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
has a lifting
along
e
, i.e. there exists a morphism
u_{A}\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
with
e\cdot u=u_{A}\cdot e
. To this end, we again apply the homomorphism theorem.
For any morphism
k\colon A\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
we have the
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language

L_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=k\cdot e\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
, which by hypothesis has the syntactic
morphism

e_{L_{k}}\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{L_{k}}
. Since
e_{L_{k}}
recognizes
L_{k}
, there exists a
morphism
p_{L_{k}}\colon A_{L_{k}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
with
L_{k}=p_{L_{k}}\cdot e_{L_{k}}
. Furthermore, the universal property of the syntactic
morphism
e_{L_{k}}
gives a unique
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h_{k}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A_{L_{k}}
with

e_{L_{k}}=h_{k}\cdot e
. Then for all

x,y\in{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
we have the following implications:

\displaystyle e(x)\leq e(y)~

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~\forall(k\colon A\to O_{\mathscr{D}})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}e_{L_{k}}(x)\leq e_{L_{k}}(y)

\displaystyle(e_{L_{k}}=h_{k}\cdot e)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~\forall k\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x\leq_{\mathds{U},L_{k}}y

\displaystyle(\text{def. $e_{L_{k}}$})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~\forall k\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{k}\cdot u(x)\leq L_{k}\cdot u(y)

\displaystyle(\text{def. $\leq_{\mathds{U},L_{k}}$})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~\forall k\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}k\cdot e\cdot u(x)\leq k\cdot e\cdot u(y)

\displaystyle(\text{def. $L_{k}$})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~e\cdot u(x)\leq e\cdot u(y)

\displaystyle(\text{Lemma \ref{lem:cosepord}}).

The homomorphism theorem gives the desired lifting
u_{A}
. Thus
e
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient.

Conversely, for the “if” direction suppose that
e
is a
\mathds{U}
-quotient. Since
e
is
\mathcal{F}
-refinable, we can choose a quotient
\overline{e}\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow B
with
B\in\mathcal{F}
and a morphism
f\colon B\to A
with
e=f\cdot\overline{e}
. Moreover, by hypothesis every
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
in
\mathds{U}

has
a lifting
u_{A}\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
along
e
. It is our task to show that
A
is a
\mathbf{T}
-quotient, i.e. carries a

\mathbf{T}
-algebra structure making
e
a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism. For each
k\colon A\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
the language
L_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=k\cdot e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
is
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable; indeed, we have
L_{k}=k\cdot f\cdot\overline{e}
, so
L_{k}
is recognized by the
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
\overline{e}
. By hypothesis,
L_{k}
has the syntactic morphism
e_{L_{k}}\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{L_{k}}
. Since
e_{L_{k}}
recognizes
L_{k}
, there exists a morphism
p_{L_{k}}\colon A_{L_{k}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
with
L_{k}=p_{L_{k}}\cdot e_{L_{k}}
.

We claim that
e_{L_{k}}
factors through
e
. To see this, we use the
homomorphism
theorem. Given
x,y\in{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
with
e(x)\leq e(y)
, we have for all

u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
in
\mathds{U}
:

\displaystyle L_{k}\cdot u(x)

\displaystyle=k\cdot e\cdot u(x)

(def.
L_{k}
)

\displaystyle=k\cdot u_{A}\cdot e(x)

(def.
u_{A}
)

\displaystyle\leq k\cdot u_{A}\cdot e(y)

(
e(x)\leq e(y)
)

\displaystyle=k\cdot e\cdot u(y)

(def.
u_{A}
)

\displaystyle=L_{k}\cdot u(y)

\displaystyle\text{(def. $L_{k}$)}.

Thus
x\leq_{\mathds{U},L_{k}}y
, or equivalently
e_{L_{k}}(x)\leq e_{L_{k}}(y)
. The
homomorphism
theorem yields a morphism
h_{k}
with
e_{L_{k}}=h_{k}\cdot e
.

We are ready to define the desired
\mathbf{T}
-algebra structure
(A,\alpha)
on
A
for which

e
is a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism. Since
T
preserves epimorphisms, it suffices to
find a
morphism
\alpha\colon TA\to A
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
making the following square commute, see
A
:

\textstyle{TT{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{Te}
\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{A}

The construction of
\alpha
once
again
rests on the homomorphism theorem.
The proof is illustrated by the diagram below, where
\alpha_{L_{k}}
is the

\mathbf{T}
-algebra structure of
A_{L_{k}}
.

\textstyle{TT{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{Te}
\scriptstyle{Te_{L_{k}}}
\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{L_{k}}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{Th_{k}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{k}}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{O_{\mathscr{D}}}
\textstyle{TA_{L_{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{L_{k}}}
\textstyle{A_{L_{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{L_{k}}}

For all
x,y\in{|TT{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}
with
Te(x)\leq Te(y)
we have

\displaystyle k\cdot e\cdot\mu_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}(x)

\displaystyle=L_{k}\cdot\mu_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}(x)

\displaystyle(\text{def.\ $L_{k}$})

\displaystyle=p_{L_{k}}\cdot e_{L_{k}}\cdot\mu_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}(x)

\displaystyle(\text{def. $p_{L_{k}}$, $e_{L_{k}}$})

\displaystyle=p_{L_{k}}\cdot\alpha_{L_{k}}\cdot Te_{L_{k}}(x)

\displaystyle(\text{$e_{L_{k}}$ is
$\mathbf{T}$-hom.})

\displaystyle=p_{L_{k}}\cdot\alpha_{L_{k}}\cdot Th_{k}\cdot Te(x)

\displaystyle(\text{def.\ $h_{k}$})

\displaystyle\leq p_{L_{k}}\cdot\alpha_{L_{k}}\cdot Th_{k}\cdot Te(y)

\displaystyle(\text{$Te(x)\leq Te(y)$})

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle(\text{compute backwards})

\displaystyle=k\cdot e\cdot\mu_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}(y).

Since this holds for all
k\colon A\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
, Lemma
109
shows that

e\cdot\mu_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}(x)\leq e\cdot\mu_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}(y)
. Thus
the homomorphism theorem yields the desired
\mathbf{T}
-algebra structure

\alpha
. Therefore
e
is a
\mathbf{T}
-quotient.

This concludes the proof that
\mathds{U}
forms a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
.
∎

Example 110
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\ast}
on
\mathbf{Set}
. Consider the the unary presentation of the free monoid
M=\Sigma^{*}
as in Example
73
and let
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
be a regular (= monoid-recognizable) language. Then
\equiv_{\mathds{U},L}
is precisely the monoid congruence
\equiv_{L}
in Example
105
, and thus the above theorem shows that
e_{L}\colon\Sigma^{*}\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma^{*}/\mathord{\equiv_{L}}
is indeed a syntactic morphism for
L
. Similarly, for the unary presentations in Example
75
and
76
, the theorem gives a description of the syntactic
\omega
-semigroup for an
\infty
-recognizable language
[
27
]
, and of the syntactic
\mathbf{T}
-algebra for a monad
\mathbf{T}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
and a
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable language
[
11
]
. Since syntactic algebras are not used in the sequel, we leave the details to the interested reader.

Theorem
108
explains why syntactic algebras are traditionally presented as a key technique in work on Eilenberg theorems: they implicitly contain the concept of a unary presentation. However, from a categorical point of view the latter are the “heart of the matter”, and for deriving Eilenberg-type correspondences it is usually easier to directly work with unary presentations in lieu of considering syntactic algebras. We will demonstrate this in Section
8
.

6
The Variety Theorem

In this section we establish our main result, the Variety Theorem for
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable languages. To introduce varieties of languages in our categorical setting, we first need a notion of
language derivatives
extending the classical concept. Here our Assumption
88
(ii) comes to use:

Notation 111
.

Recall that the variety
\mathscr{C}
is assumed to have a constant in the signature.
Choosing a constant gives a natural transformation from
C_{\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
, the
constant functor on
\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}}\in\mathscr{C}
, to the identity functor
\mathsf{Id}_{\mathscr{C}}
. It dualizes
to a
natural
transformation

\bot\colon\mathsf{Id}_{\widehat{\mathscr{D}}}\to C_{O_{\mathscr{D}}}.

We usually drop subscripts and write
\bot
for each component
\bot_{D}\colon D\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
(
D\in\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
).

Example 112
.

The purpose of
\bot

is to model the empty set. Recall that for our categories
\mathscr{D}
of Example
90
we have

O_{\mathbf{Set}}=\{0,1\},\,O_{\mathbf{Pos}}=\{0<1\},\,O_{{\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}}=\{0<1\}\text{ and }O_{\mathbf{Vec}_{K}}=K,

see Example
92
. In each case, we choose

\bot\colon D\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
to be the constant morphism with
value
0
, i.e. the characteristic function of the empty set.

Definition 113
.

Let
L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
be a language. Then we define the following languages:

1.

The
derivative

u^{-1}L
of
L
w.r.t. a unary operation
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}

is the language over
\Sigma
defined by

\begin{cases}(u^{-1}L)_{s}=(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\xrightarrow{\;u\;}(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}\xrightarrow{\;L_{t}\;}O_{\mathscr{D}}&\\
(u^{-1}L)_{r}=(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{r}\xrightarrow{\;\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\;}(V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{r}\xrightarrow{\;V\bot\;}O_{\mathscr{D}}&\text{for $r\neq s$.}\end{cases}

2.

The
preimage

g^{-1}L
of
L
under a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is the language over
\Delta
defined by

g^{-1}L=(\,T{\mathbb{\Delta}}\xrightarrow{g}T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{L}O_{\mathscr{D}}\,).

Remark 114
.

If
S=1
, we simply have
u^{-1}L=L\cdot u
and the natural transformation
\bot
is not used. Therefore, in the single-sorted case our Assumption
88
(ii) can be dropped.

Example 115
.

1.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
. Consider the unary presentation
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
of the free monoid
M=\mathbf{T}_{\ast}\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
as in Example
73
. Then a language
L\colon T_{\ast}\Sigma\to O_{\mathbf{Set}}
, viewed as a subset
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
, has the following derivatives w.r.t. the operations in
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
:

y^{-1}L=\{\,x\in\Sigma^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}yx\in L\,\}\quad\text{and}\quad Ly^{-1}=\{\,x\in\Sigma^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}xy\in L\,\}

for
y\in\Sigma^{*}
. These are exactly the classical derivatives for languages of finite words. The preimage of
L
under a monoid morphism
g\colon\Delta^{*}\to\Sigma^{*}
is the language

g^{-1}L=\{\,x\in\Delta^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g(x)\in L\,\}.

2.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{2}
. Consider the unary presentation
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
of the free
\omega
-semigroup
A=\mathbf{T}(\Sigma,\emptyset)=(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
as in Example
75
. A language
L\colon\mathbf{T}_{\infty}(\Sigma,\emptyset)\to O_{\mathbf{Set}}=(\{0,1\},\{0,1\})
may be viewed as the subset
L\subseteq\Sigma^{+}\cup\Sigma^{\omega}
obtained as the union of the subsets
L_{+}
and
L_{\omega}
in its two sorts. Then
L
has the following derivatives w.r.t. the operations in
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
:

\displaystyle y^{-1}L

\displaystyle=\{\,x\in\Sigma^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}yx\in L\,\}

\displaystyle Ly^{-1}

\displaystyle=\{\,x\in\Sigma^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}xy\in L\,\}

\displaystyle Lz^{-1}

\displaystyle=\{\,x\in\Sigma^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}xz\in L\,\}

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}L

\displaystyle=\{\,x\in\Sigma^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x^{\omega}\in L\,\}

\displaystyle y^{-1}L_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\{\,z\in\Sigma^{\omega}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}yz\in L\,\}

for
y\in\Sigma^{*}
and
z\in\Sigma^{\omega}
. These are the derivatives for

\infty
-languages studied by Wilke
[
46
]
. The preimage of
L
under an
\omega
-semigroup morphism
g\colon(\Delta^{+},\Delta^{\omega})\to(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
is the language

g^{-1}L=\{x\in\Delta^{+}\cup\Delta^{\omega}\colon g(x)\in L\}.

3.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an arbitrary monad on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
, and consider the unary operations
[p]
of Example
76
presenting the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
A=\mathbf{T}\Sigma
. The induced
derivatives of a language
L\subseteq T\Sigma
are the languages

p^{-1}L\subseteq T\Sigma
given by

(p^{-1}L)_{s}=\{\,x\in(T\Sigma)_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[p](x)\in L_{t}\,\}\quad\text{and}\quad(p^{-1}L)_{r}=\emptyset\text{ for $r\neq s$},

where
p\colon 1_{t}\to T(T\Sigma+1_{s})

ranges over polynomials over
T\Sigma
. These
polynomial derivatives
were
studied by Bojańczyk
[
11
]
. The preimage of
L
under a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is the language

g^{-1}L=\{x\in T\Delta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g(x)\in L\}.

Proposition 116
.

Let
L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
be an
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language.

1.

If
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
is a unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
, every derivative
u^{-1}L
with
u\in\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
is
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable.

2.

For any
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
, the preimage
g^{-1}L
is
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable.

Proof.

Let
L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
be an
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language. By Theorem

97
there exists a morphism
\hat{L}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}

in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
with
L=V\hat{L}\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
.

(a)

Let
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
in
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
,
and take its continuous extension
\hat{u}
, see
Lemma
83
. Then we have the commutative diagrams below
(where
r\neq s
in the right-hand diagram).

\textstyle{(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{-1}L}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}
\textstyle{(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}
\textstyle{O_{\mathscr{D}}}
\textstyle{V(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\hat{u}}
\textstyle{V(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\hat{L}}

\textstyle{(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{-1}L}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}
\textstyle{V(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\bot}
\textstyle{O_{\mathscr{D}}}

This shows that
u^{-1}L
corresponds to the morphism

\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
being
\hat{L}\cdot\hat{u}
in sort
s
and

\bot
in every other sort. By Theorem
97
,
u^{-1}L
is

\mathcal{F}
-recognizable.

(b)

Let
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
be a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
and
\hat{g}
its continuous extension, see Lemma
18
. Then
we have the commutative diagrams below.

\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbb{\Delta}}}
\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}}
\textstyle{O_{\mathscr{D}}}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\hat{g}}
\textstyle{V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V\hat{L}}

This shows that
g^{-1}L=L\cdot g
corresponds to

\hat{L}\cdot\hat{g}\colon\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
, whence
g^{-1}L
is

\mathcal{F}
-recognizable by Theorem
97
.∎

Definition 117
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=(\,L^{s}\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}\,)_{s\in S}
be an
S
-indexed family of languages over
\Sigma
. The
diagonal
of
\mathcal{L}
is the language
\Delta\mathcal{L}
over
\Sigma
given by

(\Delta\mathcal{L})_{s}=L^{s}_{s}\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}\quad\text{for all $s\in S$}.

Notation 118
.

Recall that we work with a fixed class
\mathds{A}\subseteq\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
of alphabets.
Fix for each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
a unary presentation
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
of the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
.

Definition 119

(Local variety of languages)
.

A
local variety of languages
over

\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
is a subobject

W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)

in
\mathscr{C}
closed under
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-derivatives and diagonals. That is,

(i)

for each
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and
u\in\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
, one has
u^{-1}L\in W_{\Sigma}
;

(ii)

for each
S
-indexed family
\mathcal{L}=(\,L^{s}\,)_{s\in S}
of languages in
W_{\Sigma}
, one has
\Delta\mathcal{L}\in W_{\Sigma}
.

Remark 120
.

There are two important cases where the closure under diagonals in the above definition is trivially satisfied and thus can be dropped:

1.

For
S=1
, clearly every subobject
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
is closed under diagonals.

2.

If
\mathscr{C}
is one of the categories of Example

90
and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
contains all identity morphisms
\mathit{id}_{(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
, then every subobject
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
closed under
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-derivatives is closed under diagonals. To see this, let
\mathcal{L}=(L^{s})_{s\in S}
be an
S
-indexed family of languages in
W_{\Sigma}
. Since
W_{\Sigma}
is closed under
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-derivatives, the language
\mathit{id}_{(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}}^{-1}L^{s}
lies in

W_{\Sigma}
for each
s\in S
. Recall from Example
112

that
\bot
has been chosen as the zero map. Therefore the derivative
\mathit{id}_{(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}}^{-1}L^{s}
agrees with
L^{s}
in sort
s
,
and
is the zero map (corresponding to the empty set) in all other sorts. Now observe that for the categories
\mathscr{C}=\mathbf{BA}
,
\mathbf{DL}_{01}
,
{\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}
,
the set
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
, being a subobject in
\mathscr{C}
, is closed under union by Example
101
. This implies that the diagonal
language
\Delta\mathcal{L}=\bigcup_{s}\mathit{id}_{(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}}^{-1}L^{s}
lies in
W_{\Sigma}
. Analogously for

\mathscr{C}=\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
where
W_{\Sigma}
, being a linear subspace of
{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
, is closed under addition of languages.

Since condition 2 above is satisfied in all our applications in Section
8
, the varieties considered there will not need closure under diagonals as an explicit property.

Example 121
.

In contrast to the previous remark, subobjects of
{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma)
are not always closed under diagonals. For example, consider the identity monad
\mathbf{T}=\mathsf{Id}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{2}
. Then
\mathbf{T}
-algebras are just two-sorted sets. Fix a finite set
X\neq\emptyset
and consider the two-sorted alphabet
\Sigma=(X,X)\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{2}
. The boolean algebra of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages over
\Sigma
is given by

{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma)=P(X)\times P(X)=\{\,(K,L)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}K,L\subseteq X\,\}.

The free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)=\Sigma
has the trivial unary presentation
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}=\emptyset
. Then

W_{\Sigma}=\{\,(L,L)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\subseteq X\,\}

is a boolean subalgebra of
{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma)
that is (trivially) closed under
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-derivatives, but it is not closed under diagonals: for any two distinct subsets
K,L\subseteq X
we have
(K,K),(L,L)\in W_{\Sigma}
, but the diagonal
(K,L)
does not lie in
W_{\Sigma}
.

Next, we characterize the closure of a local variety under diagonals in terms of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
(see Lemma
123
below). First, a technical preparation:

Remark 122
.

1.

For each
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
and each sort
s
we have

\displaystyle{|P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}|}

\displaystyle\cong\mathscr{C}(\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{C}},P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s})

\displaystyle\cong\widehat{\mathscr{D}}((\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s},O_{\mathscr{D}})

\displaystyle\cong\{\,(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\xrightarrow{L_{s}}O_{\mathscr{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\in{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\,\}.

The last bijection is given by
\hat{f}\mapsto V\hat{f}\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
. To see this,
observe that for each
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language
L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
, the language
L^{\prime}\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
with

L^{\prime}_{s}=L_{s}
and
L_{r}=V\bot\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
for
r\neq s
is also

\mathcal{F}
-recognizable. Indeed, if
L
is recognized by the
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to A
via
p\colon A\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
, then
L^{\prime}
is recognized by
h
via
p^{\prime}
, where
p^{\prime}_{s}=p_{s}
and
p^{\prime}_{r}=\bot
for
r\neq s
, using the naturality of
\bot
. From this and Theorem

97
the bijection immediately follows.

Therefore, we assume from now on that
P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
is carried by the set
\{\,L_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\in{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\,\}
. With this identification, the isomorphism

{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\cong\prod_{s}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
of Remark
98

maps
an
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable language
L\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
to the
S
-tuple
((T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\xrightarrow{L_{s}}O_{\mathscr{D}})_{s\in S}
of its components.

2.

Every subobject
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
in
\mathscr{C}
contains the “empty
language”, i.e. the language with
V\bot\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{r}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in each
sort
r
. Indeed, by the
definition of
\bot
(the dual of the natural transformation choosing a
constant) this language is precisely the constant
in
{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\cong\prod_{s}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
, and every subobject of
{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
in
\mathscr{C}
must contain the
constant.

Lemma 123
.

A subobject
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
is closed under diagonals iff it has the form
\prod_{s}m_{s}\colon\prod_{s}(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}\rightarrowtail\prod_{s}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
where
m_{s}\colon(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}\rightarrowtail P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
is a monomorphism in
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Given any subobject
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
and any sort
s
, consider the morphism

\textstyle{W_{\Sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\prod_{s}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{s}}
\textstyle{P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}}

where
\pi_{s}
is the product projection, and factorize it into a surjective morphism
e_{s}\colon W_{\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}
follows by an injective morphism

m_{s}\colon(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}\rightarrowtail P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
:

\textstyle{W_{\Sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{e_{s}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\prod_{s}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{s}}
\textstyle{(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{s}}
\textstyle{P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}}

(4)

By Remark
122
.1 above,
(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}
is (up to isomorphism) carried by the set

\{\,(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\xrightarrow{L_{s}}O_{\mathscr{D}}\;\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\;L\in W_{\Sigma}\,\}.

From (
4
) we get the commutative square below, where
e=\langle e_{s}\rangle_{s\in S}\colon W_{\Sigma}\to\prod_{s}(W^{\prime}_{\Sigma})_{s}
:

\textstyle{W_{\Sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\prod_{s}(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod_{s}m_{s}}
\textstyle{\prod_{s}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}}

Clearly
e
is monic. Out task is to show that
e
is surjective (i.e. an isomorphism) iff
W_{\Sigma}
is closed under diagonals. To this end, note that every element of
\prod_{s}(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}
is an
S
-tuple
(L^{s}_{s}\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to O_{\mathscr{D}})_{s\in S}
for some
S
-indexed family
\mathcal{L}=(L^{s})_{s\in S}
of languages in
W_{\Sigma}
. That
e
is surjective means precisely that, for any such family
\mathcal{L}
, there exists a language
L\in W_{\Sigma}
with
L_{s}=L^{s}_{s}
for all
s
, i.e. with
L=\Delta\mathcal{L}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Theorem 124

(Local Variety Theorem)
.

The lattice of local varieties of languages over
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
(ordered by inclusion) is isomorphic to the lattice of local pseudovarieties of
\Sigma
-generated

\mathcal{F}
-algebras form isomorphic lattices.

To prepare the proof, we need an auxiliary lemma:

Lemma 125
.

Suppose that

W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
is a subobject closed under diagonals,
represented by subobjects
m_{r}\colon(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{r}\rightarrowtail P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{r}

(
r\in S
) in
\mathscr{C}
, see Lemma
123
. Let
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
in
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
and
\hat{u}\colon(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
its continuous
extension, see Lemma

83
. Then the
following statements are equivalent:

(i)

u^{-1}L\in W_{\Sigma}
for all
L\in W_{\Sigma}
.

(ii)

There exists a morphism
u^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}
making the following square commute:

\textstyle{(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{m_{t}}
\textstyle{(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{s}}
\textstyle{P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P\hat{u}}
\textstyle{P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}.}

(5)

In particular,
W_{\Sigma}
is a local variety iff a morphism

u^{\prime}
with
(
5
) exists for every
u\in\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

Recall from Remark
122
.1 that
P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{r}
is, up to
isomorphism, carried by the set
\{\,L_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\in{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\,\}
, and

(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{r}
by
the subset
\{\,L_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\in W_{\Sigma}\,\}
. From the definition of
\hat{u}
it
follows that
P\hat{u}
takes an
element
L_{t}
of
P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
to
L_{t}\cdot u
.
Thus (ii) is equivalent to the statement that

L_{t}\cdot u\in(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}
for all
L\in W_{\Sigma}
. From
this observation the implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) follows immediately, since

(u^{-1}L)_{s}=L_{t}\cdot u
.

Conversely, suppose that (ii) holds, and let
L\in W_{\Sigma}
. By the above
argument, we have
L_{t}\cdot u\in(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}
. Moreover, by Remark

122
.3 the
“empty language” with
V\bot\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
in each sort lies in

W_{\Sigma}
. The closure of
W_{\Sigma}

under diagonals thus implies that
the language with
L_{t}\cdot u
in sort
s
and
V\bot\cdot\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
in
all sorts
r\neq s
lies in
W_{\Sigma}
. But this is precisely the derivative

u^{-1}L
, which proves (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i).
∎

Proof of the Local Variety Theorem.

Duality + Local Reiterman! Suppose that
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
is
a subobject in
\mathscr{C}
closed under diagonals, represented by a subobject

m=\left(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.5469pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.5469pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.04692pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{}{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.07349pt\raise 5.00694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{m_{s}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.5469pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.5469pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right)_{s\in S}

in
\mathscr{C}^{S}
, see Lemma
123
. From Lemma
125
and
87
, it follows via duality that
W_{\Sigma}
forms a local
variety of languages iff
the dual quotient

\left(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 18.24483pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-18.24483pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.61984pt\raise 6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.24486pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.24486pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P^{-1}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 128.84395pt\raise 6.62001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.62001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{P^{-1}m_{s}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 165.53322pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.99997pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 165.53322pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P^{-1}(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right)_{s\in S}

in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
carries a
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebra. Thus the lattice of local varieties of languages over
\Sigma
is isomorphic to the lattice of
\Sigma
-generated profinite
{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}
-algebras. Moreover, the Local Reiterman Theorem (see Theorem
31
) asserts that the latter is isomorphic to the lattice of local pseudovarieties
of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras. This concludes the proof.
∎

Next, we consider non-local varieties of languages and prove the full Variety Theorem.

Definition 126

(Variety of languages)
.

A
variety of languages
is a family of local varieties

(\,W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\,)_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}

closed
under preimages of
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms, i.e. for each
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and each
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
with
\Sigma,\Delta\in\mathds{A}
one has
g^{-1}L\in W_{\Delta}
.

This leads to the main result of our paper, which holds under the Assumptions
88
.

Theorem 127

(Variety Theorem)
.

The lattice of varieties of languages (ordered by inclusion) is isomorphic to the lattice of
pseudovarieties of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras.

Again, we need a technical lemma to prepare the proof.

Lemma 128
.

Let
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)
and

W_{\Delta}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Delta)
be subobjects closed under diagonals, represented
by
m^{\Sigma}_{s}\colon(W^{\prime}_{\Sigma})_{s}\rightarrowtail P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
and

m^{\Delta}_{s}\colon(W^{\prime}_{\Delta})_{s}\rightarrowtail P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Delta}})_{s}
(
s\in S
), respectively (see Lemma
123
).
Then for
any
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
, the following
statements
are equivalent:

(i)

g^{-1}L\in W_{\Delta}
for all
L\in W_{\Sigma}
.

(ii)

There is a morphism
g^{\prime}\colon W^{\prime}_{\Sigma}\to W^{\prime}_{\Delta}
in
\mathscr{C}^{S}

making the
following square commute for any sort
s
, where
\hat{g}\colon{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is the continuous extension of
g
(see
Lemma
18
).

\textstyle{(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}_{s}}
\scriptstyle{m^{\Sigma}_{s}}
\textstyle{(W_{\Delta}^{\prime})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m^{\Delta}_{s}}
\textstyle{P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P\hat{g}_{s}}
\textstyle{P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Delta}})_{s}}

(6)

Proof.

Again we use that
P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
can assumed to be carried by
the set
\{\,L_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\in{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)\,\}
, and
(W_{\Sigma}^{\prime})_{s}
is the
subset
\{L_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\in W_{\Sigma}\}
, see Remark
122
.1. Analogously for
P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Delta}})_{s}

and
(W_{\Delta}^{\prime})_{s}
. From the definition of
\hat{g}
it follows that

P\hat{g}_{s}
takes an element
L_{s}
of
P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
to

L_{s}\cdot g_{s}
. Thus (ii) is equivalent to the statement that

L_{s}\cdot g_{s}\in(W_{\Delta}^{\prime})_{s}
for all
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and all
sorts
s
. From this the implication of (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) follows
immediately, since
(g^{-1}L)_{s}=L_{s}\cdot g_{s}
. Conversely,
suppose that (ii) holds, and let
L\in W_{\Sigma}
. By the above
argument, we have
L_{s}\cdot g_{s}\in(W_{\Delta}^{\prime})_{s}
for all
s
. Since
W_{\Delta}
is closed under diagonals, this implies that
g^{-1}L=L\cdot g
lies
in
W_{\Delta}
, i.e. (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) holds.
∎

Proof of the Variety Theorem.
