2.

Completely analogous to the proof of Lemma
47
.2.

3.

By Lemma
155
, the two maps
\varrho\mapsto\mathcal{T}^{\varrho}
and
\mathcal{T}\mapsto\varrho^{\mathcal{T}}
give mutually inverse and order-preserving bijections between the two posets.∎

Recall from Lemma
138
that every reduced
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-quotient
e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
has an associated reduced
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebra
e_{R}\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{R}
, viz. the smallest
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebra with
e\leq e_{R}
. Recall also the morphism
h_{\downarrow}
associated to a morphism
h
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
, see Notation
140
.

In analogy to Lemma
50
we get:

Lemma 164
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a reduced
\mathcal{F}
-theory, and let
A
be an

\mathds{A}
-generated reduced algebra in
\mathcal{F}
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

There exists
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
for some
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
such that
e_{R}
has codomain
A
.

(ii)

For every
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to A
with
\Delta\in\mathds{A}
, the morphism
h_{\downarrow}
factors through some
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) Let
e\colon T\Sigma\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
such that
e_{R}
has codomain
A
. Given a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to A
with
\Delta\in\mathds{A}
, choose a

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
with
h=e_{R}\cdot g
, see Lemma
49
. Since
\mathcal{T}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-theory, we have
e\cdot g=\overline{g}\cdot\overline{e}
for some
\overline{e}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
and some morphism
\overline{g}
. Thus we get the commutative diagram below, where the morphism
q
witnesses that
e\leq e_{R}
, see Lemma
138
.2.

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{R}}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}}

Since
e_{R,s}=e_{s}
for
s\in S_{0}
, see Lemma
138
.4, we have
q_{s}=\mathit{id}
for
s\in S_{0}
. Therefore
h_{\downarrow}=q\cdot h
, and thus
h_{\downarrow}
factors through
\overline{e}
via
\overline{g}
.
This proves (ii).

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) Since
A
is
\mathds{A}
-generated, there exists a surjective

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
for some
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
.
Thus, by (ii),
h_{\downarrow}
factors through some
q\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
, i.e.
h_{\downarrow}\leq q
. Since
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
is downwards closed, we have
h_{\downarrow}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
. Moreover, since
h
is a reduced quotient of
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
, Lemma
142
shows that
h_{\downarrow,R}=h
and thus
h_{\downarrow,R}
has codomain
A
. Therefore (i) holds with
e\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=h_{\downarrow}
.
∎

Notation 165
.

For any reduced
\mathcal{F}
-theory
\mathcal{T}
, let
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
be the class of all
\mathds{A}
-generated reduced algebras
A\in\mathcal{F}
satisfying the equivalent properties of the above lemma.

Lemma 166
.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a reduced
\mathcal{F}
-theory, then
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
is a pseudovariety of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras.

Proof.

Let
A_{i}
(
i\in I
) be a finite family of algebras in
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
and suppose that
A
is an
\mathds{A}
-generated reduced
\mathbf{T}
-algebra that divides
\prod_{i}A_{i}
. That is, there exists an injective/order-reflecting
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
m\colon B\rightarrowtail\prod_{i}A_{i}
and a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
q\colon B\twoheadrightarrow A
for some
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
B
. We denote the product projections by

\pi_{i}\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to A_{i}
. To show that
A
lies in
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
, we verify the criterion of Lemma
164
(ii). Thus let
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to A
be a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism. We need to show that
h_{\downarrow}

factors through some
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
. First, since
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}
is projective (see Lemma
49
), we can choose a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
\overline{h}
with
h=q\cdot\overline{h}
.
For each
i
, the
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{i}\cdot m\cdot\overline{h}
has codomain
A_{i}\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
. Thus, by the definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
, the morphism
h_{i,\downarrow}
factors through some
e_{i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
; that is,
h_{i,\downarrow}=k_{i}\cdot e_{i}
for some
k_{i}
. Since
\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
is a local pseudovariety, there exists
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
with
e_{i}\leq e
for all
i
, i.e.
e_{i}=g_{i}\cdot e
for some
g_{i}
. Thus we get the commutative diagram below, where
r_{i}
is the unique morphism with
h_{i,\downarrow}=r_{i}\cdot h_{i}\,(=r_{i}\cdot\pi_{i}\cdot m\cdot\overline{h})
.

\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{i}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{\overline{h}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{i}}
\textstyle{C_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k_{i}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{\prod_{i}A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{i}}
\textstyle{A_{i,\downarrow}}
\textstyle{A}

From this we get the following commutative square, using that
\prod_{i}A_{i,\downarrow}=(\prod_{i}A_{i})_{\downarrow}
:

\textstyle{T\Delta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{\overline{h}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle k_{i}\cdot g_{i}\rangle}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{\downarrow}}
\textstyle{\prod_{i}A_{i,\downarrow}}

Diagonal fill-in gives a morphism
d\colon C\to B
with
d\cdot e=\overline{h}
. But then
h_{\downarrow}=q_{\downarrow}\cdot\overline{h}=q_{\downarrow}\cdot d\cdot e
, showing that
h_{\downarrow}
factors through
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
.
By the definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
, this proves that
A\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
.
∎

Notation 167
.

For any pseudovariety
\mathscr{V}
of reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras and
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
form the family

\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}=(\,\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}\,)_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}

where, for each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
,

\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}=\{\,e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A\,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\,\text{$e$ is a reduced $\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}$-quotient with $A_{R}\in\mathscr{V}$}\,\}.

Lemma 168
.

For any pseudovariety
\mathscr{V}
of reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras,
\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-theory.

Proof.

(a)

We first show that
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
is a local pseudovariety of reduced
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-quotients for each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
.
To show that
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
is downwards closed, let
e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
and
q\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow B
be two reduced
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-quotients with
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
and
q\leq e
. Then
q_{R}\leq e_{R}
by Lemma
142
, i.e.
B_{R}
is a quotient of
A_{R}
. Since
B_{R}
is reduced and
A_{R}\in\mathscr{V}
, this implies that
B_{R}\in\mathscr{V}
because
\mathscr{V}
is closed under reduced quotients. Therefore
q\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
by the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
.

To show that
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
is directed, let
e_{i}\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{i}
(
i=0,1
) be two reduced
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-quotients in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
. We need to show that there exists a reduced
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-quotient
e
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
with
e_{0},e_{1}\leq e
. Let
e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
be the subdirect product of
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
in
\mathscr{D}^{S}
, i.e. that smallest quotient of
T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
with
e_{0},e_{1}\leq e
. By Lemma
139
,
e
is a reduced
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-quotient, and moreover
e_{R}
is the subdirect product of the two
\Sigma
-generated
\mathcal{F}
-algebras
e_{i,R}\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{i,R}
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
. Thus
A_{R}
is a subalgebra of the product
A_{0,R}\times A_{1,R}
. Moreover,
A_{R}
is clearly
\mathds{A}
-generated, and
A_{R}
is reduced by Lemma
138
.3. It follows that
A_{R}\in\mathscr{V}
because
A_{0,R},A_{1,R}\in\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{V}
is closed under division of finite products. Thus
e
lies in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
. This concludes the proof that
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
is a local pseudovariety.

(b)

To show that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
forms an
\mathcal{F}
-theory, let
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
be a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism with
\Sigma,\Delta\in\mathds{A}
and let
e\colon T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
. Factorize
e_{R}\cdot g
into a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}\Delta\twoheadrightarrow B
followed by an injective/order-reflecting
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
m\colon B\rightarrowtail A_{R}
. Let
\overline{e}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=h_{\downarrow}\colon T{\mathbb{\Delta}}\twoheadrightarrow B^{\prime}
. Now consider the following diagram:

\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{e_{R}}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{A_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Here the dashed arrows are witnessing that
\overline{e}\leq\overline{e}_{R}\leq h
and
e\leq e_{R}
, see Lemma
138
.2. Note that by Lemma
138
.4, on sorts
s\in S_{0}
the morphisms
\overline{e}
,
\overline{e}_{R}
and
h
agree, as do the morphisms
e
and
e_{R}
, and thus the dashed arrows are carried by identity morphisms on these sorts. Therefore there exists a unique morphism
\overline{g}
making the lower part of the diagram commute; specifically,
\overline{g}
agrees with
m
on sorts
s\in S_{0}
, and is uniquely determined on sorts
s\not\in S_{0}
because
A_{s}
has at most one element. Then the outer part of the diagram and all parts except possibly the upper square commute, which then implies that also the upper square commutes. Moreover, we have
\overline{e}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
since the algebra
B_{R}^{\prime}
divides
A_{R}\in\mathscr{V}
and thus lies in
\mathscr{V}
.

∎

Lemma 169
.

For any pseudovariety
\mathscr{V}
of reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras we have
\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
where
\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
.

Proof.

To show
\mathscr{V}\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
, let
A\in\mathscr{V}
. Since
A
is
\mathds{A}
-generated, there exists a surjective
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
with
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, and since
A
is reduced, we have
e=e_{\downarrow,R}
by Lemma
142
. Then
e_{\downarrow}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
by the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
and therefore
A\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
by the definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
.

For the reverse inclusion
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}\subseteq\mathscr{V}
, let
A\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
. Then, for some
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, there exists
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
such that
e_{R}
has codomain
A
. But by the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
, this means that
A\in\mathscr{V}
.
∎

Lemma 170
.

For any reduced
\mathcal{F}
-theory
\mathcal{T}

we
have
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
, where
\mathscr{V}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
.

Proof.

To show
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
, let
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
. Then
A_{R}\in\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}=\mathscr{V}
by the definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
. But then
e\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
by the definition of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}
.

For the reserve inclusion
\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, let
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathscr{V}}
, this means that
A_{R}\in\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
. By the definition of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}
, there exists
\Delta\in\mathds{A}
and
q\colon T{\mathbb{\Delta}}\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}
with
B_{R}=A_{R}
. Using projectivity of free
\mathbf{T}
-algebras (see Lemma
49
), choose a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}
with
q_{R}\cdot g=e_{R}
. This implies

q\cdot g=q_{R,\downarrow}\cdot g=(q_{R}\cdot g)_{\downarrow}=e_{R,\downarrow}=e

by Lemma
142
. Since
\mathcal{T}
is a reduced
\mathcal{F}
-theory, there exists
\overline{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
and
\overline{g}
with
q\cdot g=\overline{g}\cdot\overline{q}
.

\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\overline{q}}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{T{\mathbb{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g}}
\textstyle{B}

Therefore
e=\overline{g}\cdot\overline{q}
, i.e.
e\leq\overline{q}
. Since
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
is downwards closed, it follows that
e\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
.
∎

Lemma 171
.

The maps

\mathcal{T}\mapsto\mathscr{V}^{\mathcal{T}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{V}\mapsto\mathcal{T}^{\mathscr{V}}

give an isomorphism between the lattice of reduced
\mathcal{F}
-theories and the lattice of pseudovarieties of reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras (ordered by inclusion).

Proof.

By Lemma
169
and
170
, the two maps are mutually inverse. Moreover, they are clearly order-preserving, which proves the claim.
∎

From this and Lemma
163
we get the main result of the present subsection:

Theorem 172

(Reiterman Theorem for Reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras)
.

The lattice of
pseudovarieties of reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras is isomorphic to the
lattice of reduced pro-
\mathcal{F}

theories.

7.4
Reduced varieties of languages and the Reduced Variety Theorem

In thus subsection, we present a variety theorem dealing with languages that are “empty” outside of the sorts in
S_{0}
.

Definition 173
.

A
reduced language
over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
is a family of morphisms in
\mathscr{D}

L=(\,L_{s}\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}\,)_{s\in S_{0}}.

It is called
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable
if there exists a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow(A,\alpha)
with codomain in
\mathcal{F}
and a family
(p_{s}\colon A_{s}\to O_{\mathscr{D}})_{s\in S_{0}}
in
\mathscr{D}
with
L_{s}=p_{s}\cdot h_{s}
for all
s\in S_{0}
. We write
{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F},R}(\Sigma)
for the set of all reduced
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable languages over
\Sigma
.

Example 174
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
in
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
(where
S=\{+,\omega\}
) with
O_{\mathbf{Set}}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\omega
-semigroups. Choose
S_{0}=\{\omega\}
. Since
T_{\infty}(\Sigma,\emptyset)=(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
, a reduced language corresponds precisely to an
\omega
-language
, i.e. a language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
of infinite words. The language
L
is recognizable if there exists an
\omega
-semigroup homomorphism
h\colon(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})\to A
into a finite
\omega
-semigroup
A
and a subset
Y\subseteq A_{\omega}
with
L=h^{-1}[Y]
. Recognizable languages are precisely the
\omega
-regular languages, i.e.
\omega
-languages accepted by finite Büchi automata
[
27
]
.

Remark 175
.

In complete analogy to Remark
98
and Proposition
99
, we have

{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F},R}(\Sigma)\cong\prod_{s\in S_{0}}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s},

and
{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F},R}(\Sigma)
forms a subobject of
\prod_{s\in S_{0}}O_{\mathscr{C}}^{{|T{\mathbb{\Sigma}}|}_{s}}
.

Definition 176
.

Given an
S_{0}
-indexed family
\mathcal{L}=(L^{s})_{s\in S_{0}}
of reduced languages over
\Sigma
, the
diagonal
of
\mathcal{L}
is the reduced language
\Delta\mathcal{L}
with
(\Delta\mathcal{L})_{s}=L_{s}^{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
for
s\in S_{0}
.

Remark 177
.

In analogy to Lemma
123
, a subobject
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F},R}(\Sigma)
is closed under diagonals iff it has the form
\prod m_{s}\colon\prod_{s\in S_{0}}W_{\Sigma}^{\prime}\rightarrowtail\prod_{s\in S_{0}}P(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}
for monomorphisms
m_{s}
in
\mathscr{C}
.

Definition 178
.

Let
L
be a reduced language over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
. Then we define the following languages:

1.

The
derivative

u^{-1}L
of
L
w.r.t. a unary operation
u\colon(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\to(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}
with
s,t\in S_{0}
is the reduced language over
\Sigma
defined by

\begin{cases}(u^{-1}L)_{s}=(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\xrightarrow{\;u\;}(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{t}\xrightarrow{\;L_{t}\;}O_{\mathscr{D}}&\\
(u^{-1}L)_{r}=(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{r}\xrightarrow{\;\iota_{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\;}(V\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{r}\xrightarrow{\;V\bot\;}O_{\mathscr{D}}&\text{for $r\in S_{0}\setminus\{s\}$.}\end{cases}

2.

The
preimage

g^{-1}L
of
L
under a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is the reduced language over
\Delta
defined for all
s\in S_{0}
by

(g^{-1}L)_{s}=(T{\mathbb{\Delta}})_{s}\xrightarrow{g_{s}}(T{\mathbb{\Sigma}})_{s}\xrightarrow{L_{s}}O_{\mathscr{D}}.

Definition 179

(Local variety of reduced languages)
.

A
local variety of reduced languages
over

\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
is a subobject

W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F},R}(\Sigma)

in
\mathscr{C}
closed under
\overline{\mathds{U}}_{\Sigma}
-derivatives and diagonals. That is,

(i)

for each
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and
u\in\overline{\mathds{U}}_{\Sigma}(s,t)
with
s,t\in S_{0}
, one has
u^{-1}L\in W_{\Sigma}
;

(ii)

for each
S_{0}
-indexed family
\mathcal{L}=(\,L^{s}\,)_{s\in S_{0}}
of reduced languages in
W_{\Sigma}
, one has
\Delta\mathcal{L}\in W_{\Sigma}
.

Remark 180
.

Again, if
\mathscr{C}
is one of the four categories of Example
90
, the closure under diagonals in the above definition can be dropped; see the argument in Remark
120
.

Theorem 181

(Local Variety Theorem for Reduced Languages)
.

For each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, the lattice of local varieties of reduced languages over
\Sigma
is isomorphic to the lattice local pseudovarieties of
\Sigma
-generated reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras.

Proof.

Duality + local Reiterman! In analogy to the proof of the Local Variety Theorem (see Theorem
124
), local varieties of reduced languages over
\Sigma
dualize precisely to the concept of a reduced
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-quotient of
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{S}
. And by the local Reiterman theorem for reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras (see Theorem
181
), the latter correspond to local pseudovarieties of
\Sigma
-generated reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras.
∎

Definition 182

(Variety of languages)
.

A
variety of reduced languages
is a family of local varieties of reduced languages

(\,W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}_{\mathcal{F},R}(\Sigma)\,)_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}

closed
under preimages, i.e. for each
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and each
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
with
\Sigma,\Delta\in\mathds{A}
one has
g^{-1}L\in W_{\Delta}
.

Then we get the following version of the Variety Theorem:

Theorem 183

(Variety Theorem for Reduced Languages)
.

The lattice of varieties of reduced languages (ordered by inclusion) is isomorphic to the lattice of
pseudovarieties of reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras.

Proof.

Duality + Reiterman! In analogy to the proof of Theorem
127
, varieties of reduced languages dualize precisely to pro-
\mathcal{F}
theories, and the Reiterman theorem for reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras (see Theorem
172
) asserts that the latter correspond to pseudovarieties of reduced
\mathcal{F}
-algebras.
∎

8
Applications

In this section, we demonstrate that a number of concrete Eilenberg-type correspondences, including
new
results, emerge as special instances from our categorical framework. To derive a variety theorem for a given type of languages, we apply the following three-step procedure, as indicated in the introduction:

Step 1.
Choose a monad
\mathbf{T}
and a class
\mathcal{F}
of finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras such that the
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable languages coincide with the given type of languages.

Step 2.
Choose a class
\mathds{A}\subseteq\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
of alphabets and, for each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, a unary presentation
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
of the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
.

Step 3.
Spell out what (local) varieties of languages and (local) pseudovarieties of
\mathcal{F}
-algebras are by instantiating our definitions to
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathds{A}
and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
. Then invoke the (reduced) Variety Theorem.

8.1
The classical Eilenberg theorem

As our first example, we show how to recover the original Eilenberg theorem for regular languages. It follows from the Variety Theorem by choosing the following parameters:

{tabularx}

lX

\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{D}

\mathbf{BA}
/
\mathbf{Set}

\mathbf{T}
the monad
\mathbf{T}_{*}
on
\mathbf{Set}
with
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}_{*}}\cong
monoids

\mathcal{F}
all finite monoids

\mathds{A}

\mathbf{Set}_{f}

\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}

\{x\mapsto yx,\,x\mapsto xy\;\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\;y\in\Sigma^{*}\}

Step 1.
Choose the free-monoid monad
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\ast}
on
\mathbf{Set}
and
\mathcal{F}=
all finite monoids. Regular languages are precisely the languages recognizable by finite monoids, cf. Example
94
.

Step 2.
Choose
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}
. For each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, choose the unary presentation
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma^{*}
containing the operations
x\mapsto yx
and
x\mapsto xy
, where
y\in\Sigma^{*}
; see Example
73
. Recall from Example
115
that the
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-derivatives of a language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
are the classical derivatives.

Step 3.
Instantiating Definition
119
and
126
to
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathds{A}
and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
gives the following concepts:

1.

A
local variety of regular languages
is a subset
W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma)
languages closed under boolean operations and derivatives.

2.

A
variety of regular languages
is a family of local varieties
(W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma))_{\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}}
closed under preimages, i.e.
g^{-1}L\in W_{\Delta}
for every language
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and every monoid morphism
g\colon\Delta^{*}\to\Sigma^{*}
. This is precisely Eilenberg’s original concept.

Since
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, all finite monoids are
\mathds{A}
-generated by Example
37
. Therefore, instantiating Definition
25
and
39
gives:

1.

A
local pseudovariety of monoids over
\Sigma
is class of
\Sigma
-generated finite monoids
e\colon\Sigma^{*}\twoheadrightarrow M
closed under quotients and subdirect products.

2.

A
pseudovariety of monoids
is a class of finite monoids closed under quotients, submonoids and finite products.

Theorem
124
and
127
give Eilenberg’s theorem
[
18
]
and its local version due to Gehrke, Grigorieff and Pin
[
20
]
:

Theorem 184
.

The lattice of (local) varieties of regular languages is isomorphic to the lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of monoids.

8.2
The variety theorem for
\mathscr{D}
-monoids

Eilenberg’s theorem
[
18
]
has several variants in the literature that consider classes of languages with modified closure properties (e.g. dropping closure under complement) and replace monoids by ordered monoids
[
28
]
, idempotent semirings
[
32
]
and
K
-algebras
[
35
]
. In our previous work
[
2
,
4
,
14
]
we unified these results to an Eilenberg theorem for
\mathscr{D}
-monoids in an arbitrary commutative variety
\mathscr{D}
. Our approach in loc. cit. was automata-theoretic in nature and exploited the fact that finite automata can be dually modeled as algebras and coalgebras for suitable functors. Here we give another, completely algebraic proof: we show how to deduce the variety theorem for
\mathscr{D}
-monoids in our categorical framework.

In this subsection, we assume that
\mathscr{D}
is a
commutative
variety of algebras or ordered algebras. This means that for any
A,B\in\mathscr{D}

the
hom-set
\mathscr{D}(A,B)
carries a subobject of
B^{|A|}
in
\mathscr{D}
. For example, the varieties
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Set}
,
\mathbf{Pos}
,
{\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}
and
\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
of Example
90
are commutative. Given
A,B,C\in\mathscr{D}
, by a
\mathscr{D}
-bimorphism
from
A,B
to
C
is meant a function
f\colon{|A|}\times{|B|}\to{|C|}
such that the maps
f(a,\mathord{-})\colon B\to C
and
f(\mathord{-},b)\colon A\to C
are morphisms of
\mathscr{D}
for every
a\in{|A|}
and
b\in{|B|}
. The
tensor product
of
A
and
B
is an object
A\otimes B
in
\mathscr{D}
equipped with a universal bimorphism
t_{A,B}\colon{|A|}\times{|B|}\to{|A\otimes B|}
, i.e. such that for every bimorphism
f\colon{|A|}\times{|B|}\to{|C|}
there exists a unique morphism
\overline{f}\colon A\otimes B\to C
in
\mathscr{D}
making the following triangle commutative:

\textstyle{{|A|}\times{|B|}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{|A\otimes B|}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f}}
\textstyle{{|C|}}

Tensor products exist in every commutative variety
\mathscr{D}
, making
(\mathscr{D},\otimes,\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{D}})
a symmetric monoidal closed
category; see e.g.
[
12
, Theorem 3.10.3]
. Since the tensor product
\otimes
represents bimorphisms, the monoid objects in the category
(\mathscr{D},\otimes,\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{D}})
have the following concrete algebraic description: a
\mathscr{D}
-monoid

(D,\bullet,1)
consists of an
object
D\in\mathscr{D}
equipped with a monoid structure
({|D|},\bullet,1)
on the underlying set whose multiplication
\bullet\colon{|D|}\times{|D|}\to{|D|}
is
a
\mathscr{D}
-bimorphism
. A
morphism
of
\mathscr{D}
-monoids is a
morphism in
\mathscr{D}
that preserves the monoid structure. We denote by
\mathbf{Mon}(\mathscr{D})
the category of
\mathscr{D}
-monoids and their morphisms. Note that
\mathscr{D}
-monoids in
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Set}
,
\mathbf{Pos}
,
{\mathbf{JSL}_{0}}
and
\mathbf{Vec}_{K}
are precisely classical monoids, ordered monoids, idempotent semirings, and
K
-algebras.

To apply our framework, we need to investigate the construction of free
\mathscr{D}
-monoids. Consider the following diagram of adjunctions, where
\mathbf{Mon}
is the category of monoids (in
\mathbf{Set}
), the functors
U
,
U^{\prime}
,
{|\mathord{-}|}
are the forgetful functors and
F
,
F^{\prime}
,
(\mathord{-})^{*}
,
\Psi
are their left adjoints. By the uniqueness of adjoints, both the outer and the inner square commute.

\textstyle{\mathscr{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{{|\mathord{-}|}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Mon}(\mathscr{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}
\scriptstyle{\dashv}
\scriptstyle{U^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\vdash}
\scriptstyle{(\mathord{-})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\bot}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Mon}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{|\mathord{-}|}}
\scriptstyle{F}

For notational simplicity, let us assume that
X\subseteq{|\Psi X|}
for every set
X
and that the unit
\eta_{X}\colon X\to{|\Psi X|}
of the adjunction
\Psi\dashv{|\mathord{-}|}\colon\mathscr{D}\to\mathbf{Set}
is the inclusion map. The left adjoint
F
sends a monoid
M=(M,\cdot,e)
to the
\mathscr{D}
-monoid
FM=(\Psi{|M|},\bullet,e)
, where the multiplication

\bullet\colon\Psi{|M|}\times\Psi{|M|}\to\Psi{|M|}
is
the unique
bimorphism in
\mathscr{D}
that extends the multiplication
\cdot\colon{|M|}\times{|M|}\to{|M|}
of
M
. Moreover,
F
sends a monoid morphism
h\colon M\to M^{\prime}
to the
\mathscr{D}
-monoid morphism

\Psi{|h|}\colon\Psi{|M|}\to\Psi{|M^{\prime}|}
. This implies that the
free
\mathscr{D}
-monoid on a
free object
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}=\Psi\Sigma
in
\mathscr{D}
is given by

F^{\prime}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}=F^{\prime}\Psi\Sigma=F\Sigma^{*}=(\Psi\Sigma^{*},\bullet,\varepsilon),

(7)

where
\varepsilon
is the empty word and
\bullet
extends the concatentation of
words. In the following, we write
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
for the object
\Psi\Sigma^{*}\in\mathscr{D}
.

After these preparations, let us apply our three-step plan to derive the variety theorem for
\mathscr{D}
-monoids. We choose the parameters as follows:

{tabularx}

lX

\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{D}
varieties satisfying the Assumptions
5
and
88
(i),(iii) with
\mathscr{D}
commutative

\mathbf{T}
the monad
\mathbf{T}_{M}
on
\mathscr{D}
with
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}_{M}}\cong\mathbf{Mon}(\mathscr{D})

\mathcal{F}
all finite
\mathscr{D}
-monoids

\mathds{A}

\mathbf{Set}_{f}

\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}

\{\,{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\xrightarrow{y\bullet\mathord{-}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*},\,{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathord{-}\bullet y}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y\in\Sigma^{*}\,\}

Step 1.
Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{M}
be the monad on
\mathscr{D}

associated to the adjunction
F^{\prime}\dashv U^{\prime}
. Since both
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathbf{Mon}(\mathscr{D})
are varieties of (ordered) algebras, the forgetful functors
\mathscr{D}\to\mathbf{Set}
and
\mathbf{Mon}(\mathscr{D})\to\mathbf{Set}
(resp.
\mathscr{D}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and
\mathbf{Mon}(\mathscr{D})\to\mathbf{Pos}
) are monadic. Therefore,
U^{\prime}
is monadic by
[
12
, Corollary 4.5.7]
, and thus
\mathbf{Alg}\,(\mathbf{T}_{M})\cong\mathbf{Mon}(\mathscr{D})
. A language
L\colon T_{M}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}={\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\mathscr{D}

corresponds (via
the adjunction
\Psi\dashv{|\mathord{-}|}\colon\mathscr{D}\to\mathbf{Set}
) to a map

L^{@}\colon\Sigma^{*}\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
, and conversely a map
L\colon\Sigma^{*}\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
can be uniquely extended to a language
L^{\mathscr{D}}\colon{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
. Let us call a map of the form
L\colon\Sigma^{*}\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
a
behavior
. It is
regular
if is computed by some finite automaton with output set
{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
. Equivalently, there exists a monoid morphism
h\colon\Sigma^{*}\to M
with finite codomain and a function
p\colon M\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
with
L=p\cdot h
. This is completely analogous to the characterization of regular languages via finite monoids, cf. Example
94
.

Claim.

A language
L\colon{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
is recognizable iff the behavior
L^{@}
is regular.

Proof.

If
L
is recognizable, there exists a
\mathscr{D}
-monoid
morphism
h\colon{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\to D
, where
D
is finite, and a morphism
p\colon D\to O_{\mathscr{D}}
in
\mathscr{D}
with
L=p\cdot h
. Then
h
restricts to a monoid morphism

h^{\prime}=(\Sigma^{*}\rightarrowtail U{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\xrightarrow{Uh}UD)

that recognizes
L^{@}
via
{|p|}
. Therefore
L^{@}
is regular.

Conversely, suppose that
L^{@}
is a regular behavior. Then
L^{@}
is
monoid-recognizable (in
\mathbf{Set}
), so there exists a monoid morphism

h\colon\Sigma^{*}\to M
, where
M
is a finite monoid, and a function

p\colon M\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
such that
L^{@}=p\cdot h
. Let
p^{\prime}\colon\Psi M\to O_{\mathscr{D}}

in
\mathscr{D}
be the adjoint transpose of
p
(via the adjunction

\Psi\dashv{|\mathord{-}|}\colon\mathscr{D}\to\mathbf{Set}
). Then

\Psi h\colon{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\to\Psi M
is a
\mathscr{D}
-monoid morphism that
recognizes
L
via
p^{\prime}
, where
\Psi M
is finite because
\mathscr{D}
is
assumed to be a locally finite variety (see
Assumptions
5
).
∎

Step 2.
Let
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}
. For each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, a unary presentation of the free
\mathscr{D}
-monoid
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
is given by

\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}=\{\,{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\xrightarrow{y\bullet\mathord{-}}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*},\,{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathord{-}\bullet y}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y\in\Sigma^{*}\,\}.

To see this, we need to show that a
\mathscr{D}
-congruence
\equiv
in
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
forms a
\mathscr{D}
-monoid congruence (i.e.
x\equiv x^{\prime}
and
y\equiv y^{\prime}
implies
x\bullet y\equiv x^{\prime}\bullet y^{\prime}
) iff
\equiv
is stable under left and right multiplication by words in
\Sigma^{*}\subseteq{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
. The “only” if direction is clear. For the “if” direction, suppose that
\equiv
is stable under left and right multiplication by words. It suffices to show that
\equiv
is stable under left and right multiplication:
x\equiv x^{\prime}
implies
y\bullet x\equiv y\bullet x^{\prime}
and
x\bullet y\equiv x^{\prime}\bullet y
for all
y\in{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
. By symmetry, we only show the stability under right multiplication. Thus let
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
with
x\equiv x^{\prime}
and
y\in{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
. Since
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
is generated by the set
\Sigma^{*}
as a
\mathscr{D}
-algebra, we can express
y
as a term over the generators; that is, there is a term
t
over the signature of
\mathscr{D}
with
y=t^{{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
, where
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in\Sigma^{*}
. It follows that

\displaystyle x\bullet y

\displaystyle=x\bullet t^{{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})

\displaystyle=t^{{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}}(x\bullet y_{1},\ldots,x\bullet y_{n})

\displaystyle\equiv t^{{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}}(x^{\prime}\bullet y_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}\bullet y_{n})

\displaystyle=x^{\prime}\bullet t^{{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})

\displaystyle=x^{\prime}\bullet y.

In the second and fourth step we use that
x\bullet\mathord{-}
and
x^{\prime}\bullet\mathord{-}
are morphisms on
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
in
\mathscr{D}
, and that morphisms preserve term operations. In the third step, we use that
x\bullet y_{i}\equiv x^{\prime}\bullet y_{i}
(
i=1,\ldots,n)
since
x\equiv x^{\prime}
and
\equiv
is stable under right multiplication by words, and that
\equiv
is a
\mathscr{D}
-congruence (and thus stable under term operations).

Step 3.

Instantiating Definition
126
to
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathds{A}
and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
(and identifying recognizable languages with regular behaviors
L\colon\Sigma^{*}\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
as explained above) gives the following concepts:

1.

A
local variety of regular behaviors in
\mathscr{C}
over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
is a set
W_{\Sigma}
of regular behaviors
L\colon\Sigma^{*}\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
closed under
\mathscr{C}
-algebraic operations (cf. Remark
98
and Example
92
) and derivatives; that is, for every behavior
L\colon\Sigma^{*}\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
in
W_{\Sigma}
and every word
y\in\Sigma^{*}
, the behaviors
y^{-1}L
and
Ly^{-1}
defined by

y^{-1}L(x)=L(yx)\quad\text{and}\quad Ly^{-1}(x)=L(xy)

lie in
W_{\Sigma}
.

2.

A
variety of regular behaviors in
\mathscr{C}
is a family
(W_{\Sigma})_{\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}}
of local varieties of regular behaviors closed under preimages of
\mathscr{D}
-monoid morphisms; that is, for every
\mathscr{D}
-monoid morphism
h\colon{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{*}\to{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}
and
L\colon\Sigma^{*}\to{|O_{\mathscr{D}}|}
in
W_{\Sigma}
, the behavior

h^{-1}L=(\Delta^{*}\rightarrowtail{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{*}\xrightarrow{h}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\xrightarrow{L^{\mathscr{D}}}O_{\mathscr{D}})

lies in
W_{\Delta}
.

Since
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, all finite
\mathscr{D}
-monoids are
\mathds{A}
-generated by Example
37
. Therefore, instantiating Definition
25
and
39
gives:

1.

A
local pseudovariety
\Sigma
-generated
\mathscr{D}
-monoids
is class of
\Sigma
-generated finite
\mathscr{D}
-monoids
e\colon{\mathbb{\Sigma}}^{*}\twoheadrightarrow M
closed under quotients and subdirect products.

2.

A
pseudovariety of
\mathscr{D}
-monoids
is a class of finite
\mathscr{D}
-monoids closed under quotients,
\mathscr{D}
-submonoids and finite products.

Theorem
124
and
127
then specialize to

Theorem 185

(see
[
2
,
4
,
14
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be varieties satisfying the Assumptions
5
and
88
(i),(iii), and suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is commutative. Then the lattice of (local) varieties of regular behaviors in
\mathscr{C}
is isomorphic to the lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of
\mathscr{D}
-monoids.

Four concrete instances are listed below. The fourth column states what

\mathscr{D}
-monoids are, and the third one
describes the
\mathscr{C}
-algebraic operations under which local varieties are closed. In addition, local varieties are closed under derivatives, and varieties are closed preimages of
\mathscr{D}
-monoid morphisms. All these correspondences are known in
the literature, and are uniformly covered by Theorem

185
.

{tabularx}

8.3
Variety theorems for
\Gamma
-algebras

In
[
6
,
38
]
, a generic Eilenberg correspondence for algebras over a finitary
S
-sorted signature
\Gamma
is established. It follows from our Variety Theorem via the following parameters:

{tabularx}

lX

\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{D}

\mathbf{BA}
/
\mathbf{Set}

\mathbf{T}
the monad
\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
with
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}}\cong

\Gamma
-algebras

\mathcal{F}
all finite
\Gamma
-algebras

\mathds{A}

\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}

\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
all elementary translations

Step 1.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitary
S
-sorted signature and let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}
be the free
\Gamma
-algebra monad on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
. Thus
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}}
is the category of
\Gamma
-algebras. Put
\mathcal{F}
= all finite
\Gamma
-algebras. Then
T_{\Gamma}(\Sigma)
is the
S
-sorted set of
\Gamma
-trees with leaves labeled by constants from
\Gamma
or elements of
\Sigma
. A language
L\colon T_{\Gamma}(\Sigma)\to O_{\mathbf{Set}}
over
\Sigma
corresponds to a tree language
L\subseteq T_{\Gamma}(\Sigma)
. It is recognizable iff it is accepted by some finite tree automaton, see e.g.
[
16
]
.

Step 2.

Let
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
. For each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, choose the unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}\Sigma
given by the elementary translations on
T_{\Gamma}(\Sigma)
, see Example
76
.

Step 3.

Instantiating Definition
119
and
126
to
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathds{A}
and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
gives the following concepts:

1.

A
local variety of recognizable tree languages over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
is a set
W_{\Sigma}
of recognizable tree languages
L\subseteq T_{\Gamma}(\Sigma)
closed under boolean operations (finite union, finite intersection, complement) and derivatives w.r.t. elementary translations.

2.

A
variety of recognizable tree languages
is a family
(W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma))_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}
of local varieties closed under preimages: for every
\Gamma
-homomorphism
g\colon T_{\Gamma}(\Delta)\to T_{\Gamma}(\Sigma)
and
L\in W_{\Sigma}
, the language

g^{-1}L\subseteq T_{\Gamma}(\Delta)
with
(g^{-1}L)_{s}=\{x\in(T_{\Gamma}(\Delta))_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g_{s}(x)\in L_{s}\}
lies in
W_{\Delta}
.

Since
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
, every finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebra is
\mathds{A}
-generated by Example
37
. Therefore, instantiating Definition
25
and
39
gives:

1.

A
local pseudovariety of
\Sigma
-generated
\Gamma
-algebras
is a class of finite quotient
\Gamma
-algebras
e\colon\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}\Sigma\twoheadrightarrow A
closed under quotients and subdirect products.

2.

A
pseudovariety of
\Gamma
-algebras
is a class of finite
\Gamma
-algebras closed under quotients, subalgebras, and finite products.

As a special case of Theorem
124
and
127
we get:

Theorem 186
.

The lattice of (local) varieties of recognizable
\Gamma
-tree languages is
isomorphic to the lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of
\Gamma
-algebras.

The non-local version is due to Almeida
[
6
]
in the single-sorted case, and due to Salehi and Steinby
[
38
]
in the many-sorted case. The local version is a new result. Similarly, by taking
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Pos}
and the monad
\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma,\leq}
on
\mathbf{Pos}^{S}
representing
ordered

\Gamma
-algebras, one gets the concept of a
positive variety of recognizable tree languages
, which emerges from the above concept by dropping closure under complement (since
\mathscr{C}=\mathbf{DL}_{01}
). This leads to the following variety theorem:

Theorem 187
.

The lattice of (local) positive varieties of recognizable
\Gamma
-tree languages is
isomorphic to the lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of ordered
\Gamma
-algebras.

To the best of our knowledge, the positive variant is a new result.

8.4
The polynomial variety theorem

In
[
11
]
, Bojańczyk’s established a generic variety theorem
[
11
]
for arbitrary monads on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
, extending the work of Almeida
[
6
]
and Salehi and Steinby
[
38
]
discussed in the previous subsection. Bojańczyk’s result follows from another application of our three-step procedure using the following parameters:

{tabularx}

lX

\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{D}

\mathbf{BA}
/
\mathbf{Set}

\mathbf{T}
an arbitrary monad on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}

\mathcal{F}
all finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras

\mathds{A}

\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}

\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}

\{\,[p]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{ $p$ a polynomial over $T\Sigma$}\,\}

Step 1.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an arbitrary monad on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
, and put
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras.

Step 2.

Let
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
. For each
\Sigma\in\mathds{A}
, choose the polynomial presentation of
\mathbf{T}\Sigma
given by

\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}=\{\,[p]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{ $p$ a polynomial over $T\Sigma$}\,\}
, see Example
76
.

Step 3.

Instantiating Definition
119
and
126
to
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathds{A}
and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
gives the following concepts:

1.

A
local variety of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
is a set
W_{\Sigma}
of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages over
\Sigma
closed under boolean operations (finite union, finite intersection, complement) and polynomial derivatives (see Example
115
.3).

2.

A
variety of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages
is a family
(W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma))_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}
of local varieties closed under preimages: for every
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}\Delta\to\mathbf{T}\Sigma
and
L\in W_{\Sigma}
, the language

g^{-1}L\subseteq T\Delta
with
(g^{-1}L)_{s}=\{x\in(T\Delta)_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g_{s}(x)\in L_{s}\}
lies in
W_{\Delta}
.

Since
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
, every finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebra is
\mathds{A}
-generated by Example
37
. Therefore, instantiating Definition
25
and
39
gives:

1.

A
local pseudovariety of
\Sigma
-generated
\mathbf{T}
-algebras
is a class of finite quotients
e\colon\mathbf{T}\Sigma\twoheadrightarrow A
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
closed under quotients and subdirect products.

2.

A
pseudovariety of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras
is a class of finite
\mathbf{T}
-algebras closed under quotients, subalgebras, and finite products.

Therefore we get as a special case of Theorem
124
and
127
:

Theorem 188
.

The lattice of (local) polynomial varieties of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages is
isomorphic to the lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras.

The non-local part is due to
Bojańczyk
[
11
]
, while the local part is a new result.

In the following subsections, we present variety theorems for
\infty
-languages,
\omega
-languages, cost functions and binary tree languages that do follow from the previous categorical Eilenberg theorems in
[
2
,
4
,
11
]
, but are direct instances of our present Variety Theorem.

8.5
Variety theorems for
\infty
-languages and
\omega
-languages

We derive two variety theorems of Wilke
[
46
]
and Pin
[
29
]
for
\infty
-languages and present a new variety theorem for
\omega
-languages, based on our Variety Theorem for reduced languages.

8.5.1

\infty
-languages

We use the following parameters:

/

\omega
-semigroup monad

.

Let
