8.5.2
Pin’s variety theorem for
\infty
-languages

In
[
29
]
, Pin considered a “positive” version of Wilke’s variety theorem where in the definition of a variety the closure under complements is dropped, and
\omega
-semigroups are replaced by
ordered
\omega
-semigroups
, i.e.
\omega
-semigroups
A
with a poset structure in each sort such that the products
A_{+}\times A_{+}\xrightarrow{\cdot}A_{+}
,
A_{+}\times A_{\omega}\xrightarrow{\cdot}A_{\omega}
and
A_{+}^{\omega}\xrightarrow{\pi}A_{\omega}
are monotone. Pin’s result also follows from our three steps via the following parameters:

{tabularx}

lX

\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{D}

\mathbf{DL}_{01}
/
\mathbf{Pos}

\mathbf{T}
the monad
\mathbf{T}_{\infty,\leq}
on
\mathbf{Pos}^{2}
with
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}_{\infty,\leq}}\cong
ordered
\omega
-semigroups

\mathcal{F}
all finite ordered
\omega
-semigroups

\mathds{A}

\{\,(\Sigma,\emptyset)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}\,\}

\mathds{U}_{(\Sigma,\emptyset)}
see Example
75

Step 1.
Let
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Pos}
and choose the monad
\mathbf{T}_{\infty,\leq}
on
\mathbf{Pos}^{2}
representing
ordered
\omega
-semigroups
. Recall that for any
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, the associated free object
{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\in\mathbf{Pos}
is just
\Sigma
with the discrete order. The free ordered
\omega
-semigroup generated by the two-sorted discrete poset
({\mathbb{\Sigma}},{\mathbb{\Gamma}})
is
(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega}+\Sigma^{*}\times\Gamma)
(discretly ordered), as in the unordered case; thus
T_{\infty,\leq}({\mathbb{\Sigma}},{\mathbb{\Gamma}})=(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega}+\Sigma^{*}\times\Gamma)

Step 2.
Let
\mathds{A}=\{\,(\Sigma,\emptyset)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}\,\}
. As in the unordered case, every ordered
\omega
-semigroup
A
has a unary presentation given by the operations (i)
x\mapsto yx
and
x\mapsto xy
on
A_{+}
, (ii)
x\mapsto xz
and
x\mapsto x^{\omega}=\pi(x,x,x,\ldots)
from
A_{+}
to
A_{\omega}
, and (iii)
z\mapsto yz
on
A_{\omega}
, where
y\in A_{+}\cup\{1\}
and
z\in A_{\omega}
. The proof is completely analogous to the unordered case, see Example
75
: one just replaces the equivalence
\equiv
by a stable preorder
\preceq
and equations by inequations. For each
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, choose this unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty,\leq}({\mathbb{\Sigma}},\emptyset)
.

Step 3.

As in the unordered case,
\mathds{A}
-generated ordered
\omega
-semigroups are precisely the complete ones; the argument is identical to the one in Example
38
.
Since
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{Pos}
and
\mathscr{C}=\mathbf{DL}_{01}
, the
\mathscr{C}
-algebraic operations on
{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma)
are finite union and finite intersection, see Example
101
. Definition
126
therefore instantiates as follows:

1.

A
local positive variety of
\infty
-languages over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
is a set of regular
\infty
-languages
L\subseteq\Sigma^{+}\cup\Sigma^{\omega}
closed under finite union, finite intersection, and the derivatives of Example
115
.2.

2.

A
positive variety of
\infty
-languages
is a family
(W_{\Sigma})_{\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}}
such that (i)
W_{\Sigma}
is a local positive variety of
\infty
-languages over
\Sigma
and (ii)
(W_{\Sigma})_{\Sigma}
is closed under preimages of
\omega
-semigroup morphisms.

Definition
25
and
39
give:

1.

For any
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, a
local pseudovariety of
\Sigma
-generated ordered
\omega
-semigroups
is a class of finite ordered
\omega
-semigroup quotients
e\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})\twoheadrightarrow A
closed under quotients and subdirect products.

2.

A
pseudovariety of ordered
\omega
-semigroups
is a class of finite complete ordered
\omega
-semigroups closed under quotients, complete ordered
\omega
-subsemigroups and finite products.

From Theorem
127
we get

Theorem 190
.

The lattice of (local) positive varieties of
\infty
-languages is
isomorphic to the lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of ordered

\omega
-semigroups.

The non-local part is due to Pin
[
29
]
, and the local part is again a new result.

8.5.3
A variety theorem for
\omega
-languages

Next, we present an Eilenberg-type correspondence for languages of infinite words, an application of our Reduced Variety Theorem. We choose the following parameters:

{tabularx}

lX

\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{D}

\mathbf{BA}
/
\mathbf{Set}

\mathbf{T}
the monad
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{\{+,\omega\}}
with
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}_{\infty}}\cong

\omega
-semigroups

\mathcal{F}
all finite
\omega
-semigroups

S_{0}

\{\omega\}

\mathds{A}

\{\,(\Sigma,\emptyset)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}\,\}

\mathds{U}_{(\Sigma,\emptyset)}
see Example
75

Step 1.

Choose the
\omega
-semigroup monad
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
, where
S=\{+,\omega\}
. Choose
S_{0}=\{\omega\}
and let
\mathcal{F}=
all finite
\omega
-semigroups. Then reduced
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable languages are precisely
\omega
-regular languages, see Example
174
.

Step 2.

Let
\mathds{A}=\{\,(\Sigma,\emptyset)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}\,\}
. For each
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, choose the unary presentation
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathds{U}_{(\Sigma,\emptyset)}
of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}(\Sigma,\emptyset)=(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
as in Example
75
.
Note that the only unary operations in
\overline{\mathds{U}}_{\Sigma}(\omega,\omega)
(i.e. of the form
\Sigma^{\omega}\to\Sigma^{\omega}
) are the maps
z\mapsto yz
for
y\in\Sigma^{*}
. Therefore the
\overline{\mathds{U}}_{\Sigma}
-derivatives of an
\omega
-language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
are the languages

y^{-1}L=\{z\in\Sigma^{\omega}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}yz\in L\}\qquad(y\in\Sigma^{*}),

i.e. left derivatives w.r.t. finite words.

Step 3.

Instantiating Definition
179
and
182
to
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathds{A}
,
S_{0}
and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
gives the following:

1.

A
local variety of
\omega
-regular languages over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
is a set of
\omega
-regular languages
L\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
closed under boolean operations and left derivatives w.r.t. finite words.

2.

A
variety of
\omega
-regular languages
is a family
(W_{\Sigma})_{\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}}
such that (i)
W_{\Sigma}
is a local variety of
\omega
-languages over
\Sigma
and (ii)
(W_{\Sigma})_{\Sigma}
is closed under preimages of
\omega
-semigroup morphisms. That is, for every
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and every
\omega
-semigroup morphism
g\colon(\Delta^{+},\Delta^{\omega})\to(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})
, the preimage
g^{-1}L=\{x\in\Delta^{\omega}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g(x)\in L\}
lies in
W_{\Delta}
.

Recall from Example
37
that
\mathds{A}
-generated
\omega
-semigroups are precisely the complete
\omega
-semigroups. Therefore Definition
149
and
158
give:

1.

For any
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, a
local pseudovariety of
\Sigma
-generated reduced
\omega
-semigroups
is an ideal in the poset of
\Sigma
-generated reduced
\omega
-semigroups
e\colon(\Sigma^{+},\Sigma^{\omega})\twoheadrightarrow A
; cf. Example
144
.

2.

A
pseudovariety of reduced
\omega
-semigroups
is a class of finite complete reduced
\omega
-semigroups closed under division of finite products.

From Theorem
181
and
183
we deduce

Theorem 191
.

The lattice of (local) varieties of
\omega
-regalar languages is isomorphic to the
lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of reduced
\omega
-semigroups.

Similarly, by taking the monad
\mathbf{T}_{\leq,\infty}
of ordered
\omega
-semigroups, we obtain a version for
positive varieties of
\omega
-regular languages
where the closure under complement is dropped, and pseudovarieties of reduced ordered
\omega
-semigroups. This is completely analogous to the case of
\infty
-languages discussed above.

Theorem 192
.

The lattice of (local) positive varieties of
\omega
-regular languages is isomorphic to the
lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of reduced ordered
\omega
-semigroups.

To the best of our knowledge, the above two Eilenberg correspondences are not known in the literature.

8.6
The variety theorem for regular cost functions

In this subsection, we derive the variety theorem for cost functions proved by Daviaud, Kuperberg, and Pin
[
17
]
in our framework. Regular cost functions were introduced by Colcombet
[
15
]
as a quantitative extension of regular languages, inheriting many of their algebraic and logical properties and decidability results.
A
cost function
over a finite alphabet
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
is a function
f\colon\Sigma^{*}\to\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Two cost functions
f
and
g
are identified if, for every subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, the function
f
is bounded on
A
iff
g
is bounded on
A
.

Algebraically, cost functions are represented by
stabilization monoids

[
15
]
.
By the latter is meant an ordered monoid
(M,\leq,1,\cdot)
together with a monotone operation
(\mathord{-})^{\#}\colon E(M)\to E(M)
, where
E(M)
is the set of idempotent elements of
M
, subject to the following axioms:

(S1)

For all
s,t\in M
with
st,ts\in E(M)
, one has
(st)^{\#}s=s(ts)^{\#}
.

(S2)

For all
e\in E(M)
, one has
(e^{\#})^{\#}=e^{\#}e=ee^{\#}=e^{\#}\leq e
.

(S3)

1^{\#}=1
.

Since the operation
(\mathord{-})^{\#}
is only partially defined on
M
, stabilization monoids cannot be directly treated with the standard tools of algebraic language theory. For this reason, in
[
17
]
an equivalent algebraic theory was introduced that is easier to work with:

Definition 193
.

A
weak stabilization algebra
is an ordered algebra
M
over the signature
\Gamma=\{1,\cdot,(\mathord{-})^{\#},(\mathord{-})^{\omega}\}
satisfying all inequations
t\leq\overline{t}
between
\Gamma
-terms that hold in every finite stabilization monoid, where
x^{\omega}
is interpreted as the unique idempotent power of
x
. Morphisms of weak stabilization algebras are monotone
\Gamma
-homomorphisms.

For example, a weak stabilization algebra satisfies the inequation
(x^{\omega})^{\#}\leq x^{\omega}
, because this inequation holds in every finite stabilization monoid by (S2). Moreover, every weak stabilization algebra is a monoid. Weak stabilization algebras form a variety of ordered algebras.

Definition 194
.

A weak stabilization algebra is called a
stabilization algebra
if satisfies the implication

(S4)

x\cdot x=x\quad\Rightarrow\quad x^{\omega}=x
.

An important observation is given by the following

Lemma 195
.

Every quotient of a finite stabilization algebra is a stabilization algebra.

Proof.

Let
e\colon M\twoheadrightarrow N
be a surjective morphism of weak stabilization algebras and suppose that
M
is a finite stabilization algebra. We need to show that
N
is a stabilization algebra.

(a)

We first show that, for every
s\in M
, the element
s^{\omega}
is the unique idempotent power of
s
. Choose
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s^{n}
is the unique idempotent power of
s
. Since the equation
(x^{n})^{\omega}=x^{\omega}
holds in every finite monoid (and hence in every finite stabilization monoid), it follows that
(s^{n})^{\omega}=s^{\omega}
. Moreover, since
s^{n}\cdot s^{n}=s^{n}
and (S4) holds in
M
, we have
(s^{n})^{\omega}=s^{n}
. Therefore
s^{\omega}=s^{n}
, as claimed.

(b)

We show that
N
satisfies (S4). Let
t\in N
with
t\cdot t=t
and choose
s\in M
with
t=e(s)
. Since
e
preserves idempotent powers (being a monoid morphism) and
s^{\omega}
is the idempotent power of
s
by part (a), we have that
t^{\omega}=e(s)^{\omega}
is the idempotent power of
t
. But since
t\cdot t=t
, the element
t
is idempotent and thus
t^{\omega}=t
, proving (S4).∎

To derive the variety theorem for cost functions, we apply our framework to the following parameters:

{tabularx}

lX

\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{D}

\mathbf{DL}_{01}
/
\mathbf{Pos}

\mathbf{T}
the monad
\mathbf{T}_{S}
on
\mathbf{Pos}
with
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}_{S}}\cong
weak stabilization algebras

\mathcal{F}
all finite stabilization algebras

\mathds{A}

\mathbf{Set}_{f}

\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
all elementary translations

Step 1.
Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{S}
be the monad on
\mathbf{Pos}
representing weak stabilization algebras, i.e. such that
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}_{S}}
is the variety of weak stabilization algebras. As shown in
[
17
, Proposition 4.3]
, regular cost functions over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
correspond to languages
L\colon T_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to\{0<1\}=O_{\mathbf{Pos}}
(equivalently, to down-sets of the poset
T_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
) that are recognizable by finite stabilization algebras. Accordingly, we choose
\mathcal{F}=
all finite stabilization algebras. Note that, in contrast to all previous examples,
\mathcal{F}
is a proper subclass of
\mathbf{Alg}_{f}\,\mathbf{T}_{S}
. The class
\mathcal{F}
is closed under quotients by Lemma
195
, and under subalgebras and finite products since this holds for any implicational class of ordered algebras. Thus
\mathcal{F}
satisfies Assumption
5
(iii).

Step 2.
Let
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}
. For every
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, let
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
be the unary presentation of
\mathbf{T}_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
given by the elementary translations, i.e. the operations
x\mapsto yx
,
x\mapsto xy
,
x\mapsto x^{\omega}
and
x\mapsto x^{\#}
, where
y\in\mathbf{T}_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
; see Example
74
.

Step 3.

Instantiating Definition
119
and
126
to
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{S}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathds{A}
and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
gives the following:

1.

A
local variety of recognizable down-sets over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}^{S}
is a set
W_{\Sigma}
of
\mathcal{F}
-recognizable languages over
\Sigma
(that is, of down-sets
I\subseteq T_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
recognizable by finite stabilization algebras) closed under finite union, finite intersection, and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
-derivatives. The latter means that, for every
I\in W_{\Sigma}
, the following down-sets lie in
W_{\Sigma}
:

\displaystyle y^{-1}I

\displaystyle=\{x\in T_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}yx\in I\}\quad(y\in T_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle Iy^{-1}

\displaystyle=\{x\in T_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}xy\in I\}\quad(y\in T_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}I

\displaystyle=\{x\in T_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x^{\omega}\in I\}

\displaystyle\#^{-1}I

\displaystyle=\{x\in T_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x^{\#}\in I\}

2.

A
variety of
\mathbf{T}
-recognizable languages
is a family
(W_{\Sigma}\subseteq{\mathsf{Rec}}(\Sigma))_{\Sigma\in\mathds{A}}
of local varieties of recognizable down-sets closed under preimages: for every morphism
g\colon\mathbf{T}_{S}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\to\mathbf{T}_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
of weak stabilization algebras and
I\in W_{\Sigma}
, the down-set

g^{-1}I=\{x\in T_{S}{\mathbb{\Delta}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g(x)\in I\}
lies in
W_{\Delta}
.

Since
\mathds{A}=\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, every finite stabilization algebra is
\mathds{A}
-generated by Example
37
. Therefore, instantiating Definition
25
and
39
gives:

1.

A
local pseudovariety of
\Sigma
-generated stabilization algebras
is a class of finite quotient stabilization algebras
e\colon\mathbf{T}_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
of
\mathbf{T}_{S}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
closed under quotients and subdirect products.

2.

A
variety of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras
is a class of finite stabilization algebras closed under quotients, subalgebras, and finite products.

Therefore we get as a special case of Theorem
124
and
127
:

Theorem 196

(Daviaud, Kuperberg, Pin
[
17
]
)
.

The lattice of (local) varieties of recognizable down-sets is
isomorphic to the lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of ordered
stabilization algebras.

8.7
The variety theorem for binary tree languages

As our final application, we deduce the variety theorem for binary trees of Salehi and Steinby
[
39
]
and a new local version of it, another instance of our Reduced Variety Theorem. The algebraic theory corresponding to binary trees is given by Wilke’s tree algebras
[
47
]
. By a
tree algebra
is meant a three-sorted algebra
A=(A_{l},A_{t},A_{c})
with finitary operations

\displaystyle\iota\colon

\displaystyle A_{l}\to A_{t}

\displaystyle\kappa\colon

\displaystyle A_{l}\times A_{t}\times A_{t}\to A_{t}

\displaystyle\lambda\colon

\displaystyle A_{l}\times A_{t}\to A_{c},

\displaystyle\varrho\colon

\displaystyle A_{l}\times A_{t}\to A_{c}

\displaystyle\eta\colon

\displaystyle A_{c}\times A_{t}\to A_{t}

\displaystyle\sigma\colon

\displaystyle A_{c}\times A_{c}\to A_{c}

subject to the following equations:

\displaystyle\sigma(\sigma(p,q),r)

\displaystyle=\sigma(p,\varrho(q,r))

\displaystyle\eta(\sigma(p,q),t)

\displaystyle=\eta(p,\eta(q,t))

\displaystyle\eta(\lambda(a,s),t)

\displaystyle=\kappa(a,t,s)

\displaystyle\eta(\varrho(a,s),t)

\displaystyle=\kappa(a,s,t)

The free tree algebra generated by the three-sorted set
(\Sigma,\emptyset,\emptyset)
is carried by
(\Sigma,T_{\Sigma},C_{\Sigma}^{+})
where
T_{\Sigma}
is the set of (nonempty, full, finite and ordered) binary trees with nodes labeled by elements of
\Sigma
, and
C_{\Sigma}^{+}
is the set of nonempty
contexts
, i.e.
\Sigma+\{\ast\}
-labeled binary trees with more than one node where the label
\ast
appears only at a single leaf. The tree algebra operations on
(\Sigma,T_{\Sigma},C_{\Sigma}^{+})
are defined as follows:

•

\iota(a)
is the single-node tree with label
a
.

•

\kappa(a,t_{0},t_{1})
is the tree with root labeled by
a
and left and right subtrees
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
.

•

\lambda(a,t)
is the context with root labeled by
a
, the left child of the root is
\ast
-labeled, and the right child is the tree
t
.

•

\varrho(a,t)
is the context with root labeled by
a
, the right child of the root is
\ast
-labeled, and the left child is the tree
t
.

•

\eta(c,t)
is the tree emerging from
c
by substituting
t
for
\ast
.

•

\varrho(c_{0},c_{1})
is the context emerging from
c_{0}
by substituting
c_{1}
for
\ast
.

By extension, for the empty context
c_{\ast}
(i.e. the context consisting only of a single node with label
\ast
), we define
\eta(c_{\ast},t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=t
and
\varrho(c_{\ast},c)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=c
and
\varrho(c,c_{\ast})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=c
. A binary tree language is a set
L\subseteq T_{\Sigma}
of trees over
\Sigma
. To obtain the variety theorem for binary tree languages, we apply our Reduced Variety Theorem via the following parameters:

{tabularx}

lX

\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{D}

\mathbf{BA}
/
\mathbf{Set}

\mathbf{T}
the monad
\mathbf{T}_{B}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{\{l,t,c\}}
with
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}_{B}}\cong
tree algebras

\mathcal{F}
all finite tree algebras

S_{0}

\{t\}

\mathds{A}

\{(\Sigma,\emptyset,\emptyset)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}\}

\mathds{U}_{(\Sigma,\emptyset,\emptyset)}
all elementary translations

Step 1.

Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{B}
be the monad on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
(where
S=\{l,t,c\}
) associated to the algebraic theory of tree algebras. Choose
S_{0}=\{t\}
,
\mathds{A}=\{(\Sigma,\emptyset,\emptyset)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}\}
and
\mathcal{F}=
finite tree algebras. By the above, we have
T_{B}(\Sigma,\emptyset,\emptyset)=(\Sigma,T_{\Sigma},C_{\Sigma}^{+})
, and thus a reduced language
L\colon T_{B}(\Sigma,\emptyset,\emptyset)\to O_{\mathbf{Set}}
corresponds precisely to a binary tree language
L\subseteq T_{\Sigma}
. Recognizable binary tree languages are exactly regular tree languages, i.e. those accepted by finite binary tree automata; see
[
47
]
.

Step 2.

For each
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, the tree algebra
T_{B}(\Sigma,\emptyset,\emptyset)=(\Sigma,T_{\Sigma},C_{\Sigma}^{+})
has the unary presentation
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathds{U}_{(\Sigma,\emptyset,\emptyset)}
given by the elementary translations, i.e. all maps of the form

a\mapsto\gamma(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},a,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n})
where
\gamma
is one of the six tree algebra operations and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
are elements of appropriate sort; see Example
74
. Observe that
\overline{\mathds{U}}_{\Sigma}(t,t)
consists precisely of those unary operations
u\colon T_{\Sigma}\to T_{\Sigma}
of the form
u(t)=\eta(c,t)
, where
c
is a (possibly empty) context. Thus the
\overline{\mathds{U}}_{\Sigma}
-derivatives of a language
L\subseteq T_{\Sigma}
are the languages

c^{-1}L=\{t\in T_{\Sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\eta(c,t)\in L\},

where
c
ranges over all contexts.

Step 3.

Instantiating Definition
179
and
182
to
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathds{A}
,
S_{0}
and
\mathds{U}_{\Sigma}
gives the following:

1.

A
local variety of binary tree languages over
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
is a set of regular binary tree languages
L\subseteq T_{\Sigma}
closed under boolean operations and derivatives w.r.t. contexts (see Step 2).

2.

A
variety of
\omega
-languages
is a family
(W_{\Sigma})_{\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}}
such that (i)
W_{\Sigma}
is a local variety of binary tree languages over
\Sigma
and (ii)
(W_{\Sigma})_{\Sigma}
is closed under preimages of tree algebra morphisms. That is, for every
L\in W_{\Sigma}
and every tree algebra morphism
g\colon(\Delta,T_{\Delta},C_{\Delta}^{+})\to(\Sigma,T_{\Sigma},C_{\Sigma}^{+})
, the preimage
g^{-1}L=\{x\in T_{\Delta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g(x)\in L\}
lies in
W_{\Delta}
.

By Lemma
143
, an
\mathds{A}
-generated tree algebra
A
is reduced iff for all elements
a,a^{\prime}\in A_{l}
,

\text{if $\iota(a)=\iota(a^{\prime})$ and $\kappa(a,s,t)=\kappa(a^{\prime},s,t)$ for all $s,t\in A_{t}$}\quad\text{then}\quad a=a^{\prime},

and for all elements
c,c^{\prime}\in A_{c}
,

\text{if $\eta(c,t)=\eta(c^{\prime},t)$ for all $t\in A_{t}$}\quad\text{then}\quad c=c^{\prime}.

Therefore Definition
149
and
158
give:

1.

For any
\Sigma\in\mathbf{Set}_{f}
, a
local pseudovariety of
\Sigma
-generated reduced tree algebras
is an ideal in the poset of
\Sigma
-generated reduced tree algebras
e\colon(\Sigma,T_{\Sigma},C_{\Sigma}^{+})\twoheadrightarrow A
.

2.

A
pseudovariety of reduced tree algebras
is a class of finite reduced
\mathds{A}
-generated tree algebras closed under division of finite products.

From Theorem
181
and
183
we deduce the variety theorem of Salehi and Steinby
[
39
]
and a new local version:

Theorem 197
.

The lattice of (local) varieties of binary tree languages is isomorphic to the
lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of reduced tree algebras.

Again, all these arguments can be easily adapted to ordered algebras. By taking the monad
\mathbf{T}_{B,\leq}
on
\mathbf{Pos}^{3}
of representing ordered tree algebras (i.e. tree algebras on a three-sorted poset with monotone operations), we then obtain the notion of a
positive variety of tree languages
where the closure under complement is dropped, and of a
pseudovariety of reduced ordered tree algebras
. Therefore we get the following result that, to the best of our knowledge, has not appeared in the literature:

Theorem 198
.

The lattice of (local) positive varieties of binary tree languages is isomorphic to the
lattice of (local) pseudovarieties of reduced ordered tree algebras.

9
Conclusions and Future Work

We presented a categorical framework for
algebraic language theory that captures, as special instances, the bulk of Eilenberg theorems in the literature for pseudovarieties of finite algebras and varieties of recognizable languages. Let us mention some directions for future work.

First, we aim to investigate if it is possible to obtain a variety theorem for data languages
based on nominal Stone duality
[
19
]
. On a similar note, it would also
be interesting to see whether dualities modeling probabilistic phenomena (e.g. Gelfand or Kadison duality) lead to a meaningful algebraic language theory for probabilistic automata and languages.

Secondly, although
finite
structures are of most relevance from the
automata-theoretic perspective, there has been some work on variety theorems with relaxed finiteness restrictions. One example is Reutenauer’s theorem
[
35
]
for weighted languages over arbitrary fields
K
. As already anticipated in
[
13
]
and the first version
[
45
]
of the present paper, to cover this in our setting the results of Section
3
should be presented for
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
-structured categories
\mathscr{D}
in lieu of varieties. This has been worked out in
[
44
]
and, independently, in the recent preprint
[
37
]
. In the latter, also a formal “Eilenberg correspondence” is stated for dual
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
-categories. An important conceptual difference to our present work is that in loc. cit. one uses
discrete dualities (e.g. complete atomic boolean algebras/sets instead
of boolean algebras/Stone spaces) and that unary presentations do not
appear. This makes the concept of a variety of languages (called a

coequational theory
) and the Eilenberg correspondence in
[
37
]
easy to state, but much harder to apply in practice. The results of
[
37
]
and of our paper do not entail each other, and we leave it for future work to find a common roof.

Appendix A
Categorical toolkit

We review some standard concepts from category theory that we will use throughout
this paper. For details we refer to textbooks such as

[
23
,
1
]
, and also to
[
5
]
for an introduction to locally presentable categories.

A.1  Monads.
A
monad
on a category
\mathscr{A}

is
a triple
\mathbf{T}=(T,\eta,\mu)
consisting of an endofunctor
T\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{A}

and two natural transformations
\eta\colon\mathsf{Id}\to T
and
\mu\colon TT\to T
(called the

unit
and
multiplication
of
\mathbf{T}
) such that the following
diagrams commute:

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta T}
\scriptstyle{\mathit{id}}
\textstyle{TT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{T}

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\eta}
\scriptstyle{\mathit{id}}
\textstyle{TT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{T}

\textstyle{TTT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\mu}
\scriptstyle{\mu T}
\textstyle{TT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{TT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{T}

A.2  Algebras for a monad.
Let
\mathbf{T}=(T,\eta,\mu)
be a monad on a
category
\mathscr{A}
. By a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
is meant a pair
(A,\alpha)
of an
object
A\in\mathscr{A}
and a morphism
\alpha\colon TA\to A
satisfying the

unit
and
associative laws
, i.e. making the following diagrams commute:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathit{id}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{A}

\textstyle{TTA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{A}}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{A}

Given two
\mathbf{T}
-algebras
(A,\alpha)
and
(B,\beta)
, a

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism

h\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
is a morphism

h\colon A\to B
in
\mathscr{A}
such that
h\cdot\alpha=\beta\cdot Th
. Denote by

\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
the category of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras and

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms.
There is a forgetful functor
U\colon\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}\to\mathscr{A}
given by

(A,\alpha)\mapsto A
on objects and
h\mapsto h
on morphisms. It has a left
adjoint assigning to each object
A
of
\mathscr{A}
the
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
\mathbf{T}A=(TA,\mu_{A})
, called the
free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra on
A
, and to each morphism

h\colon A\to B
the

\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism
Th\colon\mathbf{T}A\to\mathbf{T}B
. Note that for any
\mathbf{T}
-algebra

(A,\alpha)
the associative law states precisely that
\alpha\colon\mathbf{T}A\to(A,\alpha)
is a
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphism. Moreover, the unit law implies that
\alpha
is a (split) epimorphism in
\mathscr{A}
.

A.3  Quotients of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras.

Let
\mathbf{T}=(T,\eta,\mu)
be a monad, and suppose that
T
preserves epimorphisms. For any commutative square as shown below, if
(A,\alpha)
is a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra and
e
is an epimorphism, then
(B,\beta)
is a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra.

\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{Te}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{B}

(8)

Indeed, for the associative law for
(B,\beta)
consider the diagram below:

\textstyle{TTA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{TTe}
\scriptstyle{T\alpha}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{Te}
\textstyle{TTB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{T\beta}
\textstyle{TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Te}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}

The outside square commutes by the associative law for
(A,\alpha)
, the upper part because
\mu
is natural and the left-hand, right-hand and lower parts by (
8
). Therefore the central square also commutes (since it does when precomposed with the epimorphism
TTe
), giving the associative law for
(B,\beta)
. Analogously, to establish the unit law
\beta\cdot\eta_{B}=\mathit{id}
one proves that this holds when precomposed with the epimorphism
e
:

\beta\cdot\eta_{B}\cdot e=\beta\cdot Te\cdot\eta_{A}=e\cdot\alpha\cdot\eta_{A}=e.

A.4  Limits of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras.
For any monad
\mathbf{T}
on a category
\mathscr{A}
, the
forgetful functor
U\colon\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}\to\mathscr{A}
preserves limits, being a
right adjoint (see
A
). More importantly, it also

creates
limits
. That is, given a
diagram
D\colon\mathscr{S}\to\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
and a limit cone
(p_{s}\colon A\to UD_{s})_{s\in\mathscr{S}}
over
UD
in
\mathscr{A}
, there exists a unique
\mathbf{T}
-algebra structure

(A,\alpha)
on
A
such that all
p_{s}
are
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms, and moreover

(p_{s}\colon(A,\alpha)\to D_{s})_{s\in\mathscr{S}}
forms a limit cone over
D
in
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
.
In the case where the category
\mathscr{A}
is complete, it follows that also

\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
is complete and that
U

reflects limits
. That is, a cone

(p_{s}\colon(A,\alpha)\to D_{s})_{s\in S}
over
D
is a limit cone whenever
(p_{s}\colon A\to UD_{s})_{s\in S}
is a limit cone over
UD
.

A.5  Comma categories.

Let
F\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}
be a functor and
A
an object in
\mathscr{A}
. The

comma category

(A\downarrow F)
has as objects all morphisms
(A\xrightarrow{f}FB)
in
\mathscr{A}
with
B\in\mathscr{B}
, and its morphisms from
(A\xrightarrow{f_{1}}FB_{1})
to

(A\xrightarrow{f_{2}}FB_{2})
are morphisms
h\colon B_{1}\to B_{2}
in
\mathscr{B}
with
f_{2}=Fh\cdot f_{1}
. If
F\colon\mathscr{B}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{A}
is the inclusion of a
subcategory
\mathscr{B}
,
we write
(A\downarrow\mathscr{B})
for
(A\downarrow F)
.

A.6  Kan extensions.
The
right Kan
extension
of a functor

F\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}

along
K\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
is a functor
R\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}
together with a universal natural
transformation
\varepsilon\colon RK\to F
, i.e. for every functor

G\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}
and every natural transformation
\gamma\colon GK\to F

there
exists a unique natural transformation
\gamma^{\dagger}\colon G\to R
with

\gamma=\varepsilon\cdot\gamma^{\dagger}K
. The universal property determines
R
and
\varepsilon
uniquely up to isomorphism. If
\mathscr{A}
is small and
\mathscr{C}
is
complete, the right Kan extension
R
exists, and the object
RB
for
B\in\mathscr{B}
can be computed as the limit of the diagram

(B\downarrow K)\xrightarrow{\;Q^{B}\;}\mathscr{A}\xrightarrow{\;F\;}\mathscr{C},

where
Q^{B}
is the projection functor that maps
(B\xrightarrow{f}KA)
to
A
and
h\colon(B\xrightarrow{f_{1}}KA_{1})\to(B\xrightarrow{f_{2}}KA_{2})
to
h\colon A_{1}\to A_{2}
.

A.7  Codensity monads.

Let
\varepsilon\colon RK\to K
be the right Kan extension of a functor
K\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
along itself. Then
R
can be equipped with a monad structure
\mathbf{R}=(R,\eta^{\mathbf{R}},\mu^{\mathbf{R}})
where the unit
\eta^{\mathbf{R}}
is given by
(\mathit{id}_{K})^{\dagger}\colon\mathsf{Id}\to R
and the multiplication
\mu^{\mathbf{R}}
by
(\varepsilon\cdot R\varepsilon)^{\dagger}\colon RR\to R
. The monad
\mathbf{R}
is called the

codensity monad
of
K
, see e.g.
[
22
]
.

A.8  Final functors.
Let
\mathscr{K}
be a cofiltered category (see
A
below). A functor
F\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{B}
is called
final
if

(i)

for any object
B
of
\mathscr{B}
, there exists a
morphism
f\colon FK\to B
for some
K\in\mathscr{K}
, and

(ii)

given two parallel morphisms
f,g\colon FK\to B
with
K\in\mathscr{K}
, there
exists a morphism
k\colon K^{\prime}\to K
in
\mathscr{K}
with
f\cdot Fk=g\cdot Fk
.

The importance of final functors is that they facilitate the
construction of limits. If
F\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{B}
is final, then a
diagram

D\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}
has a limit iff the diagram
DF\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{A}
has a limit, and in this case the two limit objects agree. Specifically,
any limit
cone
(p_{B}\colon A\to D_{B})_{B\in\mathscr{B}}
over
D
restricts to a limit cone

(p_{FK}\colon A\to D_{FK})_{K\in\mathscr{K}}
over
DF
.

A.9  Cofiltered limits and inverse limits.
A category

\mathscr{K}
is
cofiltered
if for every finite subcategory
D\colon\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\rightarrowtail\mathscr{K}

there exists a cone over
D
. This is equivalent to the following three
conditions:

(i)

\mathscr{K}
is nonempty.

(ii)

For any two objects
Y
and
Z
of
\mathscr{K}
, there exist two
morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon X\to Z
with a common domain
X
.

(iii)

For any two parallel morphisms
f,g\colon Y\to Z
in
\mathscr{K}
, there exists a
morphism
e\colon X\to Y
with
f\cdot e=g\cdot e
.

A
cofiltered limit
in a category
\mathscr{A}
is a limit of a diagram
\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{A}
with cofiltered scheme
\mathscr{K}
. It is also called an
inverse limit

if
\mathscr{K}
is a (co-directed) poset. For any small cofiltered category
\mathscr{K}
, there exists a final functor
F\colon\mathscr{K}_{0}\to\mathscr{K}
where
\mathscr{K}_{0}
is a small co-directed poset. Consequently, a category has cofiltered limits iff it has inverse limits, and a functor preserves cofiltered limits iff it preserves inverse limits.

The dual concept of a cofiltered limit is a
filtered colimit
, see
[
5
]
.

A.10  Finitely copresentable
objects.
An
object
A
of a category

\mathscr{A}
is called
finitely copresentable
if the hom-functor

\mathscr{A}(\mathord{-},A)\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathbf{Set}^{op}
preserves cofiltered limits.
Equivalently, for any cofiltered limit cone
(p_{i}\colon B\to B_{i})_{i\in I}
in

\mathscr{A}
the following two statements hold:

(i)

Every morphism
f\colon B\to A
factors through some
p_{i}
.

(ii)

For any
i\in I
and any two morphisms

s,s^{\prime}\colon B_{i}\to A
with

s\cdot p_{i}=s^{\prime}\cdot p_{i}
, there exists a connecting morphism
b_{ji}\colon B_{j}\to B_{i}
in the given
diagram with
s\cdot b_{ji}=s^{\prime}\cdot b_{ji}
.

A.11  Locally finitely copresentable categories.

A category
\mathscr{A}
is called

locally finitely copresentable
if it satisfies the following three
properties:

(i)

\mathscr{A}
is complete;

(ii)

the full subcategory
\mathscr{A}_{f}
of finitely copresentable objects is
essentially small, i.e. the objects of
\mathscr{A}_{f}
(taken up to isomorphism)
form a set;

(iii)

any object
A
of
\mathscr{A}
is a cofiltered limit of finitely
copresentable objects; that is, there exists a cofiltered limit cone
(A\to A_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathscr{A}
with
A_{i}\in\mathscr{A}_{f}
for all
i\in I
.

This implies that
\mathscr{A}
is also cocomplete.
If
\mathscr{A}
is locally finitely copresentable, so is any functor category
\mathscr{A}^{\mathscr{S}}
, where
\mathscr{S}
is an arbitrary small category. In particular, this holds for any product category
\mathscr{A}^{S}
(where
S
is set) and for the arrow category

\mathscr{A}^{\to}
. The latter has as objects all morphisms of
\mathscr{A}
, and as morphisms from
(A\xrightarrow{f}B)
to
(C\xrightarrow{g}D)
all pairs of morphisms
(a\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\to C,b\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B\to D)
in
\mathscr{A}
with
b\cdot f=g\cdot a
. The finitely copresentable objects of
\mathscr{A}^{\to}
are precisely the
arrows with finitely copresentable domain and codomain.

A.12  Cofiltered limits in locally finitely copresentable
categories.
Let
\mathscr{A}
be a locally finitely copresentable category. A cone
(p_{i}\colon B\to B_{i})_{i\in I}
over a cofiltered diagram in
\mathscr{A}
is a limit
cone iff

(i)

every morphism
f\colon B\to A
with
A\in\mathscr{A}_{f}
factors through some

p_{i}
, and

(ii)

this factorization is essentially unique: given
i\in I
and
s,s^{\prime}\colon B_{i}\to A
with
s\cdot p_{i}=s^{\prime}\cdot p_{i}
, there exists a morphism
b_{ji}\colon B_{j}\to B_{i}
in the diagram with
s\cdot b_{ji}=s^{\prime}\cdot b_{ji}
.

Note that if all
p_{i}
’s are epimorphisms, condition (ii) is trivial.

A.13  Canonical diagrams.
Let
\mathscr{A}
be a
locally finitely
copresentable
category. Then for each object
A\in\mathscr{A}
the comma category
(A\downarrow\mathscr{A}_{f})
is essentially small and cofiltered. The
canonical diagram of

A
is the cofiltered diagram
K_{A}\colon(A\downarrow\mathscr{A}_{f})\to\mathscr{A}

that maps an object
(A\xrightarrow{f}A_{1})
to
A_{1}
and a morphism
h\colon(A\xrightarrow{f_{1}}A_{1})\to(A\xrightarrow{f_{2}}A_{2})
to
h\colon A_{1}\to A_{2}
. Every object
A
of
\mathscr{A}
is
the cofiltered limit of its canonical diagram, that is,
K_{A}
has the limit cone

(f\colon A\to K_{A}f)_{f\in(A\downarrow\mathscr{A}_{f})}.

A.14  Pro-completions.
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a small
category. By
a
pro-completion
(or a
free completion under cofiltered limits
)
of
\mathscr{B}
is meant a category
\mathsf{Pro}\,\mathscr{B}
together with a
full embedding
I\colon\mathscr{B}\rightarrowtail\mathsf{Pro}\,\mathscr{B}
such that

(i)

\mathsf{Pro}\,\mathscr{B}
has cofiltered limits.

(ii)

For any functor
F\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}
into a category
\mathscr{C}
with cofiltered
limits, there exists a functor
\overline{F}\colon\mathsf{Pro}\,\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}
, unique up to natural
isomorphism, such that
\overline{F}
preserves cofiltered limits and
\overline{F}\cdot I
is
naturally isomorphic to
F
.

The universal property determines
\mathsf{Pro}\,\mathscr{B}
uniquely up to equivalence of
categories. If the category
\mathscr{B}
has finite limits, then
\mathsf{Pro}\,\mathscr{B}
is locally
finitely copresentable, and its finitely copresentable objects are up to
isomorphism the objects
IB
(
B\in\mathscr{B}
). Conversely, every locally finitely
copresentable
category
\mathscr{A}
arises in this way: we have
\mathscr{A}=\mathsf{Pro}\,\mathscr{A}_{f}
.

The dual concept of a pro-completion is an
ind-completion
, i.e. the
free
completion under filtered colimits. For more on pro-completions see
[
21
]
, and for ind-completions see
[
5
]
.

A.15  Factorization systems.
A

factorization system
in a
category

\mathscr{A}
is
a pair
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
where
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{M}
are classes of morphisms of
\mathscr{A}
with
the following properties:

(i)

Both
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{M}
are closed under composition and contain all
isomorphisms.

(ii)

Every morphism
f
of
\mathscr{A}
has a factorization
f=m\cdot e
with
e\in\mathcal{E}

and
m\in\mathcal{M}
.

(iii)

The
diagonal fill-in
property holds: given a commutative square
as shown below with
e\in\mathcal{E}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
, there exists a unique morphism

d
making both triangles commute.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{D}

Factorization systems are discussed at length in
[
1
]
. We will use three standard facts about factorization systems:

(a)

Suppose that
\mathcal{M}
is a class of monomorphisms. If
(p_{i}\colon A\to A_{i})_{i\in I}
is a limit cone in
\mathscr{A}
, then the
factorization

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 18.15248pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-18.15248pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{p_{i}=(A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.67151pt\raise 5.00694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{e_{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.15248pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.99997pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.15248pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A_{i}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.48178pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{}{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.8498pt\raise 5.00694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{m_{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.98177pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.98177pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A_{i}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)
with

e_{i}\in\mathcal{E}

and

m_{i}\in\mathcal{M}
yields another limit cone
(e_{i}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I}
over the
same scheme.

(b)

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}
is a class of epimorphisms. If
\mathbf{T}
is a monad on

\mathscr{A}
that preserves
\mathcal{E}
, i.e.
e\in\mathcal{E}

implies

Te\in\mathcal{E}
, then
\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
has the factorization system of
\mathcal{E}
-carried and

\mathcal{M}
-carried
\mathbf{T}
-homomorphisms.

(c)

Every locally finitely copresentable category
\mathscr{A}
has the (epi,
strong mono)
factorization system. Its arrow category
\mathscr{A}^{\to}
, see
A
, has the
factorization system of componentwise epimorphic and strongly
monomorphic morphisms.

A.16  Quotients and subdirect products.

Suppose that
\mathscr{A}
is a category with a factorization system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
, where
\mathcal{E}
is a class of epimorphisms. For any two quotients (=
\mathcal{E}
-morphisms)
e_{i}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A_{i}
(
i=0,1
) put
e_{0}\leq e_{1}
if
e_{0}
factorizes through
e_{1}
, i.e.
e_{0}=h\cdot e_{1}
for some morphisms
h
. This defines a preorder on the class of quotients of
A
, which can be turned into a partial order by identifying any two quotients
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
with
e_{0}\leq e_{1}\leq e_{0}
.

If
\mathscr{A}
has binary products, then the
subdirect product
of
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
is
the image
e\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\twoheadrightarrow A
of the morphism
\langle e_{0},e_{1}\rangle\colon\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\to A_{0}\times A_{1}
.

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{\langle e_{0},e_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{e_{i}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{A_{0}\times A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{A_{i}}

It can be characterized as the smallest quotient of
A
with
e_{0},e_{1}\leq e
.

Appendix B
Topological toolkit

In this section, we recall some important properties of cofiltered limits in the
category
\mathbf{CHaus}
of
compact
Hausdorff spaces and continuous maps. The proofs of the first three lemmas below can be found in Chapter 1
of
[
36
]
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\tau\colon D_{1}\to D_{2}
be a natural transformation between cofiltered
diagrams (over the same scheme) in
\mathbf{CHaus}
. If each
\tau_{i}\colon D_{1}i\twoheadrightarrow D_{2}i
is surjective,
so is the mediating map
\Lim\tau\colon\Lim D_{1}\to\Lim D_{2}
.
In particular, if
(\tau_{i}\colon X\twoheadrightarrow Di)
is a cone of
surjections over a cofiltered diagram
D
, then the mediating map
X\to\Lim D
is surjective.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
D
be a cofiltered diagram in
\mathbf{CHaus}
.
If all connecting maps
D(i\xrightarrow{f}j)

are surjective, so is each limit projection
\varrho_{i}\colon\Lim D\to Di
.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
D
be a cofiltered diagram of non-empty spaces in
\mathbf{CHaus}
.
Then
\Lim D
is non-empty.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
(p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to X_{i})_{i\in I}
be a cofiltered limit in
\mathbf{CHaus}
, where all
X_{i}
’s are finite (and thus discrete).
For
each
i\in I
, there is some
j\in I
and a connecting map
g_{ji}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{j}\to X_{i}
with
p_{i}[X]=g_{ji}[X_{j}]
.

Proof.

By
A
we may assume that
I
is a codirected poset.

(i)

For each
x\in X_{i}\setminus p_{i}[X]
, there exists some
j\leq i

such that
x\not\in g_{ji}[X_{j}]
. To see this, suppose the contrary.
Then, for each
j\leq i
, the set
X_{j}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\,y\in X_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g_{ji}(y)=x\,\}
is
non-empty. Moreover, for
k\leq j\leq i
, the connecting map
g_{kj}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{k}\to X_{j}

restricts to
X_{k}^{\prime}
and
X_{j}^{\prime}
. Thus
(X_{j}^{\prime})_{j\leq i}
forms a subdiagram of

(X_{j})_{j\leq i}
, and by Lemma
B.3
its limit
(p_{j}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X^{\prime}\to X_{j}^{\prime})_{j\leq i}
is non-empty. Consider the unique map
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X^{\prime}\to X
with

p_{j}\cdot g=s_{j}\cdot p_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\leq i
, where
s_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{j}^{\prime}\rightarrowtail X_{j}
is the
inclusion. Then,
choosing any
x\in X^{\prime}
, we have
x=p_{i}(g(x^{\prime}))
, contradicting the assumption
that

x\not\in p_{i}[X]
.

(ii)

Since
X_{i}\setminus p_{i}[X]
is a finite, by (i) and codirectedness of

I
there is some
j\leq i
such that
g_{ji}[X_{j}]\subseteq p_{i}[X]
. Moreover, we
have

g_{ji}[X_{j}]\supseteq p_{i}[X]
because
(p_{i})

is a cone. This proves the claim.

∎

Two important classes of compact Hausdorff spaces are Stone spaces and Priestley spaces. A
Stone space
is a compact topological space
X
such that for any two elements
x\neq y
of
X
there is a clopen (= simultaneously open and closed) set containing
x
but not
y
. A
Priestley space
is an ordered compact space
X
(i.e. a compact topological space endowed with a partial order
\leq
on
X
) such that for any two elements
x\not\leq y
of
X
there is a clopen
\leq
-upper set containing
x
but not
y
. We denote by
\mathbf{Stone}
the category of Stone spaces and continuous maps, and by
\mathbf{Priest}
the category of Priestley spaces and continuous monotone maps.

Appendix C
Algebraic toolkit

In this section, we review some concepts of many-sorted universal algebra.

C.1  Signatures.

Given a set
S
of sorts, by an
S
-sorted signature
is meant a set
\Gamma
of
operation symbols
with specified input and output sorts from
S
. Formally, to each
\gamma\in\Gamma
one associates a triple
(\kappa,\mathbf{s},s)
where
\kappa
is a cardinal number,
\mathbf{s}\in S^{\kappa}
and
s\in S
. We call
(\kappa,\mathbf{s},s)
the
type
and
\kappa
the
arity
of
\gamma
. If
\kappa=n
is finite and
\mathbf{s}=(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n-1})
, we write
\gamma\colon s_{0}\times\ldots\times s_{n-1}\to s
to specify the type of
\gamma
. If all operation symbols in
\Gamma
are of finite arity,
\Gamma
is called a
finitary signature
.

C.2  Algebras and ordered algebras.

Let
\Gamma
be an
S
-sorted signature.
A
\Gamma
-algebra
is an
S
-sorted set
A\in\mathbf{Set}^{S}
with associated
\Gamma
-operations. That is, to each operation symbol
\gamma\in\Gamma
of type
(\kappa,\mathbf{s},s)
, where
\mathbf{s}=(s_{i})_{i<\kappa}
, one associates an operation
\gamma^{A}\colon\prod_{i<\kappa}{A_{s_{i}}}\to A_{s}
. A
morphism
(also called a
\Gamma
-homomorphism
) between two
\Gamma
-algebras
A
and
B
is an
S
-sorted function
f\colon A\to B
such that the following diagram commutes for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
of type
(\kappa,\mathbf{s},s)
:

\textstyle{\prod_{i<\kappa}{A_{s_{i}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\prod_{i}h_{s_{i}}}
\textstyle{A_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{s}}
\textstyle{\prod_{i<\kappa}{B_{s_{i}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma^{B}}
\textstyle{B_{s}}

Quotients
and
subalgebras
of
\Gamma
-algebras are represented by sortwise surjective and injective morphisms, respectively.

An
ordered
\Gamma
-algebra
is a
\Gamma
-algebra
A
with an additional poset structure
\leq
on each
A_{s}
such that all
\Gamma
-operations
\gamma^{A}\colon\prod_{i<\kappa}{A_{s_{i}}}\to A_{s}
are monotone maps. A
morphism
between ordered
\Gamma
-algebras is a sortwise monotone
\Gamma
-homomorphism.
Quotients
and
subalgebras
of ordered
\Gamma
-algebras are represented by sortwise surjective and order-reflecting morphisms, respectively. Here a morphism
h
is called
order-reflecting
if
hx\leq hx^{\prime}
implies
x\leq x^{\prime}
(and thus
hx\leq hx^{\prime}
iff
x\leq x^{\prime}
by monotonicity). Note that every order-reflecting morphism is injective.

C.3  Congruences.

Let
\Gamma
be an
S
-sorted signature and
A
a
\Gamma
-algebra. An
S
-sorted equivalence relation on
A
is family
\mathord{\equiv}=(\equiv_{s})_{s\in S}
, where
\equiv_{s}
is an equivalence relation on
A_{s}
. If the sort
s
is clear from the context, we drop the subscript and write
\equiv
for
\equiv_{s}
. We call an
S
-sorted equivalence relation
\equiv
a
congruence
on
A
if it is stable under all
\Gamma
-operations: for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
of type
(\kappa,\mathbf{s},s)
and any two elements
a,b\in\prod_{i<\kappa}A_{s_{i}}
,

a_{i}\equiv_{s_{i}}b_{i}\text{ for all $i<\kappa$}\qquad\text{implies}\qquad\gamma^{A}(a)\equiv_{s}\gamma^{A}(b).

For any congruence
\equiv
, the
S
-sorted set
A/\mathord{\equiv}=(A_{s}/\mathord{\equiv_{s}})_{s\in S}
of
\equiv
-congruence classes can be equipped with a
\Gamma
-algebra structure: for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
of type
(\kappa,\mathbf{s},s)
, put

\gamma^{A/\mathord{\equiv}}\colon\prod_{i<\kappa}{(A_{s_{i}}/\mathord{\equiv_{s_{i}}})}\to(A_{s}/\mathord{\equiv_{s}}),\qquad([a_{i}]_{\equiv_{s_{i}}})_{i<\kappa}\mapsto[\,\gamma^{A}((a_{i})_{i<\kappa})\,]_{\equiv_{s}}.

This is the unique
\Gamma
-algebra structure on
A/\mathord{\equiv}
making the projection

e_{\equiv}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A/\mathord{\equiv}
a
\Gamma
-homomorphism.

Conversely, every quotient
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
of
A
yields a
\Gamma
-congruence on
A
, viz. the
kernel congruence

\equiv_{e}
given on sort
s
by

a\equiv_{e,s}b\quad\text{iff}\quad e_{s}(a)=e_{s}(b).

The maps
\equiv\,\mapsto\,e_{\equiv}
and
e\,\mapsto\,\equiv_{e}
give a bijective correspondence between quotients of
A
and congruences on
A
.

C.4  Stable preorders.

Let
\Gamma
be an
S
-sorted signature and
(A,\leq)
an ordered
\Gamma
-algebra. An
S
-sorted preorder on
A
is a family
\mathord{\preceq}=(\preceq_{s})_{s\in S}
where
\preceq_{s}
is a preorder on
A_{s}
. If the sort
s
is clear from the context, we drop the subscript and write
\preceq
for
\preceq_{s}
. We call an
S
-sorted preorder
\preceq
a
stable preorder
on
A
if (i)
\preceq
is coarser than
\leq
, i.e.
\mathord{\leq}\subseteq\mathord{\preceq}
, and (ii)
\preceq
is stable under all
\Gamma
-operations: for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
of type
(\kappa,\mathbf{s},s)
and any two elements
a,b\in\prod_{i<\kappa}A_{s_{i}}
,

a_{i}\preceq_{s_{i}}b_{i}\text{ for all $i<\kappa$}\qquad\text{implies}\qquad\gamma^{A}(a)\preceq_{s}\gamma^{A}(b).

If
\preceq
is a stable preorder then the equivalence relation

\equiv\,=\,\preceq\cap\succeq
is a congruence, see

C
. The
\Gamma
-algebra
A/\mathord{\equiv}
can
be turned into an ordered algebra by putting

[a]_{\equiv_{s}}\leq_{s}[b]_{\equiv_{s}}
iff
a\preceq_{s}b
. We denote
this ordered algebra by
A/\mathord{\preceq}
. The projection

e_{\preceq}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A/\mathord{\preceq}
is a monotone surjective

\Gamma
-homomorphism.

Conversely, every quotient
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
of
A
yields a stable preorder on
A
, viz. the
ordered kernel

\preceq_{e}
given on sort
s
by

a\preceq_{e,s}b\quad\text{iff}\quad e_{s}(a)\leq e_{s}(b).

The maps
\mathord{\preceq}\mapsto e_{\preceq}
and
e\mapsto\mathord{\preceq}_{e}
give a bijective correspondence between quotients of
(A,\leq)
and stable preorders on
(A,\leq)
.

C.5  Elementary translations.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitary signature and
A
an
\Gamma
-algebra. By an
elementary translation
is meant a map
u\colon A_{s}\to A_{t}
(
s,t\in S
) of the form

u(a)=\gamma^{A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},a,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n})\quad\text{for all $a\in A_{s}$},

(9)

where
n\geq 0
,
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
,
\gamma\colon s_{1}\times\ldots\times s_{i}\times s\times s_{i+1}\times\ldots\times s_{n}\to s
is an
(n+1)
-ary operation symbol in
\Gamma
, and
a_{i}\in A_{s_{i}}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. A
S
-sorted equivalence relation
\equiv
on
A
forms a congruence iff it is stable under all elementary translations, that is, for every elementary translation (
9
) and any two elements
a,a\in A_{s}
with
a\equiv_{s}a^{\prime}
, one has
u(a)\equiv_{s}u(a^{\prime})
; see e.g.
[
25
,
26
]
. Analogously, an
S
-sorted preorder on an ordered algebra
(A,\leq)
is a stable preorder iff it is stable under all elementary translations.

C.6  Varieties of algebras and ordered algebras.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitary
S
-sorted signature. A
variety of
\Gamma
-algebras
is a class of
\Gamma
-algebras specified by equations
s=t
between
\Gamma
-terms. Similarly, a
variety of ordered
\Gamma
-algebras
is a class of ordered
\Gamma
-algebras specified by inequations
s\leq t
between
\Gamma
-terms. By an
S
-sorted variety of (ordered) algebras
is meant a variety
\mathscr{A}
of (ordered)
\Gamma
-algebras for some finitary
S
-sorted signature
\Gamma
. For every single-sorted variety
\mathscr{A}
and set of sorts
S
, one can view the product category
\mathscr{A}^{S}
as an
S
-sorted variety.

Categorically, varieties can be modeled by monads: for every
S
-sorted variety
\mathscr{A}
of (ordered) algebras, there exists a monad
\mathbf{T}
on
\mathbf{Set}^{S}
(resp.
\mathbf{Pos}^{S}
) with
\mathscr{A}\simeq\mathbf{Alg}\,{\mathbf{T}}
.

C.7  Topological algebras.

Let
\Gamma
be an
S
-sorted signature. A
topological
\Gamma
-algebra
is a
\Gamma
-algebra
A
endowed with a topology on each component
A_{s}
such that for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
of type
(\kappa,\mathbf{s},s)
the operation
\gamma^{A}\colon\prod_{i<\kappa}{A_{s_{i}}}\to A_{s}
is continuous. An
ordered topological
\Gamma
-algebra

A
is additionally endowed with a partial order on each sort such that every
\gamma^{A}
is continuous and monotone. Morphisms of (ordered) topological
\Gamma
-algebras are (monotone) continuous
\Gamma
-homomorphisms. Given a variety
\mathscr{A}
of
\Gamma
-algebras, we denote by
\mathbf{Stone}(\mathscr{A})
the category of topological
\Gamma
-algebras carrying a Stone topology such that the underlying
\Gamma
-algebra (forgetting the topology) lies in
\mathscr{A}
. Similarly, if
\mathscr{A}
is a variety of ordered
\Gamma
-algebras, we denote by
\mathbf{Priest}(\mathscr{A})
the category of ordered topological
\Gamma
-algebras carrying a Priestley topology such that the underlying ordered
\Gamma
-algebra lies in
\mathscr{A}
.

C.8  Homomorphism theorem.

Let
\Gamma
be an
S
-sorted signature, and let
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
and

f\colon A\to C
be two morphisms of
\Gamma
-algebras with
e
sortwise surjective. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a morphism
g\colon B\to C

with

g\cdot e=f
.

(2)

For all sorts
s
and

a,a^{\prime}\in{|A|}_{s}
, if

e(a)=e(a^{\prime})

then
f(a)=f(a^{\prime})
.

Similarly, if
e
and
f
are morphisms between ordered
\Gamma
-algebras, then (i) is equivalent to

(2’)

For all sorts
s
and
a,a^{\prime}\in{|A|}_{s}
, if
e(a)\leq e(a^{\prime})
then
f(a)\leq f(a^{\prime})
.

If moreover
e
and
f
are morphisms between (ordered) topological
\Gamma
-algebras whose topologies are compact Hausdorff, then the map
g
in (i) is continuous. This follows from the fact that for every continuous surjection
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
between compact Hausdorff spaces, the space
B
carries the final topology w.r.t.
e
.
